"Forgive me," Jude said, quieter, collecting himself. "A sore subject. The point stands, all the same — whoever sold you that wants finding, and questioning, properly. That man belongs in a cell, and I mean it."

"I've connections of my own in the market," Paul said. "I don't doubt you've a few as well. And I'll not pretend it doesn't interest me — there's no harm I can see in tracing where the thing came from."

"No harm." Jude's voice hardened by a degree. "It ties us to the Brotherhood, Paul. And they sit well above your Toli rats — greedy opportunists, the Toli, the whole breed of them. For all we know the Toli run errands for the Brotherhood and never learn whose hand feeds them."

"And where do you come by that?" Paul asked, an edge rising to meet his. "Because you say it?"

"Because I've spent a lifetime doing the work set in front of me, and learning its shape." Jude did not raise his voice, which somehow made it worse. "You'll do this. What is it you object to — that we take the vendor, lean on him, threaten him if we must?"

"That we'd do it on no evidence at all," Paul said.

"On no evidence that touches what you care for, you mean."

"I care that we've the right man before we break him," Paul said. "That is all."

For a moment they only looked at one another, the disagreement settling into something that was not quite a quarrel and not quite trust. It was Jude who broke it.

"Then we put it to Jamis," he said. "He's your godfather, near enough. Let him weigh it."

"Fine," Paul said. "We'll take it to Jamis."

They did not have long to wait. They sat glowering companionably over the dregs of the wine, and perhaps five minutes on the door opened and Lord Jamis stepped in, the council behind him written in the tiredness of his face.

"I see you've made short work of the refreshments," he observed, waving for a servant to bring more before he settled himself. "You've had time enough to take each other's measure, I trust. Tell me what you've learned."

"We've a lead I don't think much of, and Jude thinks a great deal of," Paul said. "The mask — the one I bought. It belongs to the Scarlet Brotherhood, some order down in the southern isles, beyond the Toli. Jude would have us trace the vendor who sold it."

Jamis's expression did not change, but something in his stillness sharpened. "The man who sold that mask will be looked into," he said. "By others. Not by you. Leave him be — don't accost him, don't bring him in." He let that settle. "Keep the mask, if you like, against some later need. But you are not to walk the city in it."

Paul kept his own counsel, though the ruling chafed — to be told he might keep the mask yet never wear it was to be handed a fine thing and have it taken back in the same breath, and he wondered, sourly, what he was meant to have for it.

"Now — what of the rest?" Jamis said.

Jude gave him the shape of it: the man who'd tried for Paul's life the night before, the thieves'-guild mark across his back, a guild master named Janus some eight months in his seat. "I've set him to learn whether Janus takes Toli coin. And he says it's common talk in the streets that the Toli assassins quit the city some five days past — all four at once."

"Five to seven," Jamis said. "It fits what I've heard."

"Which is strange in itself, that every gutter in the foreign district should know it. I asked whether any agent had stayed behind. He named an elf — a woman, hard to follow, seen near your own residence." Jude watched him carefully. "I took her for Kalisi."

"Leave her be — for now." Jamis said it more gently than the rest of it. "I've eyes enough on Kalisi already, and I'd sooner see which way she jumps before I set the two of you on her trail. Push at her too soon and she goes to ground, and we learn nothing for it. Let her make her next move in her own time; when there's cause to run her down, you'll be the ones I send. Until then, leave her to me." He drew a breath. "You gave the guildsman coin?"

"Five gold, to start."

"Keep it minimal. We may want the funds before this is done." He nodded slowly. "Turning him was well done. Where is the meeting to be?"

"Ferd's Bar, three days on."

Paul stirred at that. "He was nervous of the place. Said he'd be killed for setting foot in it."

"He's not wrong about its custom," Jamis said, with the ghost of something that might have been amusement. "You know it well enough, Jude — you and the men who run it. You helped make its name what it is."

"It's a filthy hole," Jude allowed. "But safe enough, for us — I've killed men there myself."

"Just so." Jamis let the silence hold a beat, then set his cup aside and leaned in, and the room changed with him. "But the guild and the mask must keep. I've other work for you now — the reason for this morning's council. I want the two of you on a boat."

Jude grimaced. "I hate the water. To where?"

"A corsair is making for Helm Island. You'll be aboard her." Jamis folded his hands. "Last night there was a storm in the straits — a fog bank, unnatural, that swallowed the whole channel for hours and then lifted as though it had never been. By day the Helm looks well enough from shore. But it has not answered with the correct signal — the watch out there swears all is well, yet they will not give us the day's proper watchword."

"So someone may hold the fortress," Jude said slowly, "and wears the garrison's face over it — under cover of that fog. Would that be the front of a larger invasion?"

"Who seizes a fortress meaning to stop there?" Jamis said. "Admiral Fairwind commands the Helm — a seasoned man, five-and-forty or thereabouts, no greybeard but no green recruit either. It may be nothing worse than a weary officer giving a wrong order. But we must know."

"And how are we put ashore?" Jude asked.

"Openly. Paul goes as himself, in daylight — as Prince Jeon's representative, and he'll carry a writ that says so. You'll be known to the captain as his man." Jamis's gaze moved to Jude. "You are to lend your arts where they're needed, and to see that no harm comes to Paul. The ship goes in force — two hundred and fifty Marines, a full crew. This is no secret errand; we send warships to the Helm often enough. Investigate the whole of the island. Root out any traitor, any Toli, any hand that shouldn't be there — and report to me."

"When do we sail?" Paul asked.

"Within the hour. Gather your kit."

Jude glanced, briefly, toward the door and the market beyond it. "Time enough to stop at a still on the way?"

"For something to steady your stomach on the crossing?" Jamis said. "No. Sober and armed, both of you. The tide won't wait, and neither, I think, will whatever holds the Helm."

---

## PART TWO: TO HELM ISLAND

# Chapter Five — The Royal Docks

At the Royal Docks in Monmurg, Tyrus and Folsom had already been standing at attention for the better part of an hour, part of a marine detachment — crossbowmen and infantry both — summoned down from the Harbor District with no more explanation than the rest of the gathering battalion had been given.

They were a mismatched pair to find shoulder to shoulder in a squad, though the docks knew them both. Tyrus was the larger — six foot four and built to match, with the loud, easy charm of a man half the harbor counted a friend. He had come up hard beneath it: the son of a serving-woman who spun him grand tales of a father he never truly knew, soldier and pirate by turns, the story always ending with a promise of the real one someday. Raised in the loft of his great-uncle Gaius — a freight-runner rough as a barnacled keel, who ruled his little island fleet by no law but his own — Tyrus had gone to sea by the age of eight and lost both his mother and his uncle to a house fire before he was fourteen. What he had carried away was a sailor's hands, a soldier's eye for tactics coaxed from rum-loosened veterans over long evenings, and the hard lesson that the surest way to win a fight was to talk, steer, or loom a man clear out of ever starting one. He loved the Monmurgian navy better than almost anything, and the three islands scarcely less — he knew, as few did, that they were the shield that kept Monmurg from being severed at the neck.

Folsom stood beside him, and if Tyrus was the harbor's friend, Folsom was its music. He was of ordinary height and evenly made — no warrior's build to him at all, though nothing gawky in it either; the kind of man who crossed a crowded deck without ever seeming to be in anyone's way. A shipwright's son and a seamstress's, he had lost his heart to sound as a boy, when a wandering bard out of Gradsul pressed a fife into his hands and moved on; the melodies had never left him since. He had carried the gift into the navy, where his officers found ready use for it — signals passed ship to ship on a few clean notes, and, worth more than any signal, a tune to lift men's hearts on a long or a bloody crossing. Jovial, unfairly handsome, and no great hand in a fight, he would far sooner bind a wound than open one, and no one who had heard him play seemed much to mind. Somewhere behind the good humor he kept a quiet dream of making his way one day to Gradsul — a city he had never seen — to find the great college of bards he had only ever heard tell of, and learn what a man with a true gift might make of himself there.

Rumor ran fast and thin through the ranks — reinforcement, invasion, escort duty, no one quite certain which — and the fact that the Royal Docks themselves had been given over to the mustering only deepened the unease.

"Escort, they're saying now," Folsom murmured out of the side of his mouth, not troubling himself to come to any proper attention. "Though I heard invasion not an hour past, and a fellow down the line swears we're off to put down a mutiny at one of the beacons."

"They don't muster a full battalion out of the Royal Docks for an escort." Tyrus kept his voice low, his eyes moving over the gathering companies the way another man might read a darkening sky. He had spent half his life on these wharves, and knew what an ordinary sailing looked like; this was not one. "You want to shift grain or troops, you do it from the harbor quays like everyone else. The Royal Docks are for royal business — the prince's own ships, the prince's own eyes. This is something Jeon wants kept close under his own hand."

"Comforting," said Folsom.

"Count the ballistae they're hauling aboard. Count the crossbowmen." Tyrus nodded at the studded-leather men filing past. "That's no crew sent to fetch a cargo. That's a crew that reckons it may have to fight for one." He said it easily enough, for the sake of the younger troopers within earshot — but Folsom had known him long enough to catch the thread of disquiet running under the ease of it. "Whatever's waiting out there, somebody a deal higher than a sergeant is afraid of it."

Folsom let a slow breath go and, for once, found nothing light to say to it — which, in Folsom, was as near to worry as the man ever came.

It sharpened into something else entirely when a figure came down from the Royal Palace by its own private stair — dressed head to toe in black, plainly too warm for the weather, a silvered mask fixed over his face, and beside him a sour, tight-lipped elf in a wide hat doing a poor job of disguising what he was. The two of them drew stares the length of the dock before climbing to the sterncastle to speak with the captain.

"That's Paul Rivera," Tyrus murmured. "Prince Jeon's nephew. Never laid eyes on him myself before now."

Tyrus and Folsom knew the old stories well enough — kings of legend who wore silver masks not from vanity but mercy, sparing their subjects the sight of some ruinous illness beneath. Whatever the truth of Paul Rivera's face, the rumor in Monmurg ran that he'd been disfigured as a boy, in some accident no one spoke of plainly, son of Jeon's late sister Lady Alaria.

Down the line a sergeant's voice went up, brass and carrying — gear on, packs shouldered, every man ready to board the moment the word was given. The order rippled back through the ranks, and the dock dissolved into the ordinary chaos of it: men hauling up crossbows and bedrolls and bundles of bolts, chivvying one another into rough columns before the gangways.

It was amid that readying that Merrick and Gouge found them, crossing the dock with the particular purpose of men who already knew more than they meant to say.

"Tyrus — good to see you," Merrick called.

"Merrick!" Tyrus clapped him on the shoulder, then turned to the disguised man beside him. "And who's this?"

"My colleague," Merrick said, giving the cover name they'd settled on. "Aristotle."

"Good to meet you," Gouge said, wearing the alias as easily as the borrowed studded leather.

"Likewise," Tyrus said, and for a moment the pleasantries ran on a beat too long, the whole exchange faintly absurd against the backdrop of two hundred fifty men arming for something none of them understood.

The sergeant's voice cut through it, warm with theater. "Merrick! Jamis said you'd be joining us." The warmth was real enough — the two of them went back a long way — but it thinned at the edges, and his eyes lingered a beat too long on Merrick's face. The sergeant was fond of Merrick; he also knew what it meant when Jamis put his own scout aboard an ordinary crossing. It meant the crossing was not ordinary at all. "Can I ask why you're along for this?"

"Lord Jamis's orders," Merrick said easily. "Heard the weather's been rough out that way — wanted someone with a steady eye, in case we run into trouble."

"Any notion why we're bound for Helm Island?" the sergeant went on, unwilling to let it lie. "Escort work, is what I've been told — minding a shipment in. But it doesn't sit right, considering the muster and the choice of port." He said it lightly enough, but his eyes never left Merrick's face, measuring, the way a man reads someone who has already crossed the ice before he trusts his own weight to it. "Nothing in it worse than that, I'd think. Would you?"

It was not quite a question, and Merrick knew better than to treat it as one. "Could be nothing at all," he said — and it could; that much was true. "A signal that came in wrong, weather playing tricks. Like as not we make port, find everything in order, and sail home bored of each other." Every word of it honest, and not one of them the whole of it — and the sergeant nodded slowly, reassured on the surface and, Merrick could see plainly, not one inch beneath.

The sergeant's eyes moved over Gouge with open appraisal, and what he found there settled something cold in his stomach. It was not confidence he saw, nor a man playing dress-up in borrowed leather — it was a killer, plain as a scar. Gouge had a shark's eyes, flat and incurious, and the flask never far from his hand told the rest of it, carried openly by a man who did not care in the least who marked him for a drinker. The sergeant had known the type the whole of his life; they were the men who had built the Principalities themselves, out of blood and plunder — pirates born, not cold exactly, not without feeling, but wholly untroubled by whatever they felt. You did not send a man like that to mind a shipment. Seeing him here, in this unit, on this crossing, the sergeant understood in his gut that things were worse than anyone had troubled to tell him.

"Weather crossbowman," Gouge offered. "Just assigned."

It was an odd thing to claim, and a prouder one than the man let on. The weather crossbowmen had been a true distinction once — the finest marksmen of the buccaneer crews, the men who could put a bolt through an enemy across a heaving deck in a full gale — but that had been decades gone, back in the wild, lawless days before Monmurg's privateers were leashed and made respectable. No such rank survived in the navy now. A younger trooper would have heard the words as so much old sea-cant and thought nothing of them. The sergeant was not a younger trooper. He knew precisely what they meant, and precisely what it told him that a man should still wear the title with pride: here stood someone who had learned his trade among the old buccaneers, in a harder and bloodier age than this one.

"You do well in foul weather, then?" he said, and let none of it show.

"Best off the side of a ship, in the worst of it."

The sergeant grunted, unconvinced but unwilling to press it with Merrick standing silent at his side. Instead he drifted a few paces down the dock to where Tyrus and Folsom waited, and dropped his voice to something meant for the two of them alone — men he'd sailed with long enough to trust with a worry he'd not have spoken aloud to anyone else. "I don't suppose Merrick's going to tell us what's really going on," he confided, with a short backward glance at Merrick and his shark-eyed companion. "But you don't turn the Royal Docks over to a muster this size just to mind a shipment. We'll board regardless — only keep your wits about you, the pair of you."

"Sounds good to me," Tyrus said cheerfully, and turned to the handsome, dashing figure at his other shoulder. "Folsom — give us a tune to march aboard by."

Folsom struck up something bright and quick on his flute, and to its rhythm the squad filed up the gangplank — twenty Marines in scale mail with shields and longswords and ten crossbowmen in studded leather without shields — thirty strong all told, Tyrus among the Marines and Gouge among the bowmen in his guise as Aristotle — plus their sergeant, and Merrick, who served as their scout-navigator and answered to no one unit alone.

"Keep an eye on that one," the sergeant murmured to Tyrus, with a nod toward Merrick's new companion — low enough to reach no one else, and if it carried as far as Gouge, the very man he meant, he gave not the slightest sign of caring.

"Yes, sir," Tyrus said, and said no more.

They cleared the Royal Docks by half past three, the corsair cutting a clean line out into calm water under a light and favorable wind — a pleasant enough afternoon for what was beginning to feel, more with every passing minute, less like an escort and more like a battalion bound for war.

Merrick had walked Helm Island's paths before, in the years Jamis himself had held the Helm — the barracks, the lighthouses, the roads between them, if never the fortress proper at the island's crown. He drifted near the captain and the two strange newcomers as the voyage wore on, close enough to catch fragments of their talk without seeming to listen at all, and found himself troubled by what little he overheard.

"I'm only going out there to confirm all's well," the captain was saying, more than once, as though repeating it might make it truer.

"You'll want to be prepared regardless," the elf told him, each time, urging a caution the captain plainly hadn't expected to need. Whatever authority the masked young lord carried, he seemed content to let it rest entirely behind the elf's judgment — a strange enough dynamic that even Merrick, listening from a distance, could feel the captain's unease sharpening as the island drew near.

It came to a head as they closed on the southern beacon, a quarter mile off.

"I'd approach the docks cautiously," the elf said, nodding toward the pale tower ahead. "That lighthouse worries me."

"I see nothing wrong with it," the captain said.

"You've had no signal from it at all. You don't know what's happening there."

The captain looked ready to refuse — and then the masked lord leaned in and murmured something too low for Merrick to catch, a handful of words, no more. Whatever they were, they struck harder than all the elf's arguing had. Something in the captain's face shifted — worry sliding toward something closer to anger, and then past it into grudging assent — before he turned and called down to the deck. "Sergeant — second squad, into the longboats. Go up to the southern beacon, see what you find."

"Tyrus — get them ready," the sergeant said, and the deck erupted into motion, men hauling longboats to the port rail as the corsair began its slow turn.

# Chapter Six — The Corsair

For Paul and Jude, that same crossing wore an altogether different face. By three in the afternoon they had their gear together and were making for the Royal Docks, where the corsair sat low in the water under the weight of two hundred fifty Marines and their equipment. Paul had never crossed to Helm Island before, and he found himself measuring the distance with his eyes as they walked — a speck of rock and lighthouse fire sitting in the middle of the Monmurgian Straits, between the peninsula and the looming bulk of Flotsam Island, well clear of the cold northern waters where the turtle dragons were said to nest.

"Three to five miles, at most," Jude said, following his gaze. "Not the half-day you were expecting."

Around them, the dock swarmed with activity — scale-armored Marines hauling crates of bolts and provisions up the gangway, sergeants barking men into rough columns, the whole scene lit gold and hazy in the late-afternoon sun. Two hundred fifty men. Not an invasion, Paul thought, studying the orderly chaos of it, but not a courtesy visit either.

They were led up to the sterncastle, where the ship's captain stood waiting — a weathered man of perhaps five-and-thirty, breastplate marking his rank, the kind of face that had long since stopped being impressed by lords and their nephews. Jude fell in a half-step behind and to the side, a wide-brimmed hat shading a face no one in Monmurg would mistake for human at ten paces, a borrowed quill tucked behind one ear to suggest scribe rather than blade.

"I'm Captain Richard," the man said, looking Paul over. "I take it you're the nephew of Prince Jeon."

"I am," Paul said.

"I can tell by the mask." The captain's eyes flicked briefly to Jude, then back. "Mind telling me why we've the pleasure of your company today?"

"I'm here as my uncle's personal representative, to see what's happening on the island."

The captain's mouth thinned. "I'd suggest you stay out of the way. You're welcome on the upper deck with me, and you may ask what questions you like — but understand, I command this expedition."

Paul held the man's gaze through the mask a moment, weighing his answer, then let his voice come rough and honest rather than smooth. "Jeon has granted me authority to take charge should the need arise, and I'd rather not have cause to use it. I've no wish to make that necessary. I hope we can remain on good terms."

Something in the captain's shoulders eased — relief, Paul thought, at not having drawn some smarmier courtier for this crossing. "Very well. You'll find I'm quite professional, when it comes to command."

"We'll see," Paul said, and the captain almost smiled.

By the time the sun had climbed past its peak, the last of the Marines had boarded — thirty-man units in scale mail and shield, spears bristling, crossbowmen mixed among them, light-armored but carrying the heavier bows. The corsair pushed off from the dock at half past three, three masts catching the wind, and began its run across the straits — fast, lean, built for boarding actions rather than siege, three ballistae mounted along her deck in place of the catapults a larger warship might carry. Monmurg's corsairs hunted in packs, Paul had heard it said — wolves against the lions of the Keoland fleet, swift enough to flee what they couldn't overwhelm.

Standing near the captain on the sterncastle, alongside the first mate at the wheel and one of the Marine officers, Paul let a good half hour pass before he began asking questions in earnest.

"I've never been to the Helm," he admitted. "What fortifications does it have?"

"The keep itself sits on the island's highest point," Captain Richard said. "Three lighthouses — east, west, and south; we'll raise the southern one first. The quays sit behind the Helm, on the leeward side. Only the one true fort, though there are guard towers by most of the lighthouses, and a large barracks midway up the island, behind a wooden palisade, below the Helm proper."

"And signals to Monmurg — do they come from the keep itself, or from outside it?"

"Every lighthouse can signal after a fashion — change the color of the light, red for an attack. From here we can't see the eastern light, so it can't reach Monmurg direct, but the west could reach the Helm, and the east could relay word on to Flotsam if needed. Direct word to the city, though — that comes from the great tower at the Helm. Several braziers up there, each meaning something different."

Paul turned that over as the ship cut through the chop, the wind carrying the mingled smell of salt and pitch and the low murmur of two hundred fifty men settling into the short crossing. Whatever the captain had or hadn't been told, this was no supply run — a battalion this size didn't sail for a courtesy call.

Jude knew the answer to his own question — Jamis had given it to him plainly enough, in private, before ever they sailed. He asked it anyway, watching the captain's face as he did: partly to keep the man a little off his balance, and partly to learn whether Jamis had let him in on the same suspicions, or left him to make this crossing blind.

"Why so many men," he asked, "if this is meant to be routine?"

"We're confirming Lord Fairwind's had no trouble," Captain Richard said, and something in the flatness of it told Jude the man believed that answer about as much as he himself did.

"You're not exactly subtle about it, if that's all this is."

"We're making contact with Lord Fairwind," the captain repeated, unmoved. "I take it that's your errand as well?"

"It is."

They fell into an uneasy rhythm after that — Jude pressing for details of the Helm's defenses, the quay's fortifications, the disposition of the garrison, and the captain answering each readily enough, though every time he did his gaze flicked to Paul, plainly weighing whether the elf's impertinence was a thing he was obliged to suffer. Paul gave him nothing to read either way, and so Captain Richard went on answering. When Jude suggested a few squads be kept ready to intercept trouble at the hard points, the captain's eyes narrowed behind his weathered calm.

"Who is this man beside you," the captain said to Paul, his patience finally worn through, "and why am I to suffer his advice?"

"He is an advisor," Paul said.

The captain turned to Jude. "I'll do my job, sir — but understand, I've no intention of assaulting anything."

"I didn't say assault," Jude said. "I said be ready — should there be a catastrophic problem."

"You handle your business," the captain said. "I'll handle mine."

An hour and a quarter into the crossing, Helm Island rose out of the haze on the western horizon, and the ship swung south along its flank. The southern lighthouse stood a quarter mile off, pale stone against the darkening sky, and beside it a wooden watchtower — forty feet of timber, clearly built to cover any landing at the small dock bay below.

The silent beacon only hardened Jude's resolve, and he pressed the point once more, past the last of the captain's patience. "I'd have you approach those docks cautiously," he said, with a nod at the pale tower ahead. "The southern beacon should have hailed us by now, and hasn't — not once since we cleared the harbor. You don't know what's waiting beneath that light any better than I do. Send men ahead to be sure of it, before you commit the whole ship."

"I see nothing wrong with it," Captain Richard said, and there was warning in the flatness of it now.

Paul spoke before the refusal could harden — quietly, in the tone he kept for the rare occasions he meant to be obeyed. "Do as he asks, Captain. My uncle sent me to look at this island with open eyes, and I'll not have him told afterward that his corsair sailed a battalion blind into a silent harbor because her captain was certain all was well. An hour's precaution costs us nothing. Being wrong might cost us the ship."

The name of Prince Jeon did what Jude's caution alone could not. Something in the captain's face shifted — worry sliding toward something closer to anger, then past it into grudging sense — before he turned and called down to the deck.

*Get the men ready! Second squad, stand ready!*

"Better to apologize afterward than end up as fish food," Jude murmured at Paul's shoulder.

"I'd rather make a proud man kneel and kiss the ring than that," Paul agreed, and watched the second squad assemble on the open deck below — thirty men in all, ten crossbowmen mixed among them, every one of them moving with the crisp discipline of soldiers who had drilled this a hundred times.

Beside him, Jude let his own eyes drift across the ranks, cataloguing faces out of old habit, and stopped on one — a man in studded leather among the crossbowmen, wearing his disguise as easily as a second skin. Gouge. Jude gave no sign of recognition, no nod, nothing that might mark the man to anyone watching, but he took careful note of who stood at Gouge's shoulder on either side, filing the faces away for later.

Thirteen minutes later, Captain Richard gave the order, and longboats dropped away from the corsair's sides, fifteen men to a boat, rowing hard for the southern lighthouse while the ship itself continued its slow curve around the island's edge.

"He's taking proper precautions," Paul murmured, watching the boats pull away.

"We don't want to get caught flat-footed," Jude said. "Two hundred fifty Marines asleep on a ship, while the whole island burns around them."

Rounding the island's southern face, they came upon a natural breakwater — jagged stone jutting from the water, treacherous to sail but offering shelter on the leeward side where the quays and docks sat waiting, calm water in the shadow of the rocks.

Paul scanned the docks for any sign of life. No ships. No flags. No men walking the walls at all.

"Is that usual?" he asked.

"It is not," the captain said, and something in his voice had gone very still.

They continued their slow circuit, past the eastern lighthouse — another stone tower, another connecting bridge, no proper dock but mud flats where men might still be put ashore — before finally turning toward the main harbor itself, taking the longer, more dangerous water rather than risk an unseen ambush along the straighter path.

"I'd expect patrols by this hour," Richard admitted, when Paul pressed him. "At least a shift or two. There are none. As you've already noticed."

"What would you recommend?"

"I don't know that I have a recommendation beyond caution. The lighthouse fires are still lit, at least — if the island were truly abandoned, I'd expect them dark by now. Someone's tending them, or they were only just left before we arrived."

"Then we ought to assume," Paul said slowly, "that whatever this is, it's worse than it looks, not better."

"Agreed," the captain said. "The Helm must be secured, one way or another. I'll hold to the plan — you disembark with a squad, go ashore, and see if the harbor master can be found. If not, I'll send more men to search the barracks. Whatever we find, we cause Lord Fairwind no undue alarm unless the situation demands it. But I'll welcome any further counsel you have."

The corsair eased into her berth among the empty docks, stairs rising from two separate points toward the higher ground, battlements jutting out at regular intervals along the quay wall — hard points, archers' posts, all of them silent and unmanned. Above, thirty feet up, a secondary tier of defenses sat equally still.

A Marine sergeant moved among his men, readying them — crossbowmen falling in behind the spearmen, shields checked, everyone scanning both port and starboard at once.

"Third Squad," the sergeant called, "this is us."

Ladders dropped along the ship's side. Paul counted his detachment as they assembled — twenty men in scale mail, ten more with crossbows slung — and made his way down after them, careful, even now, not to tread on the captain's authority more than he already had. Jude came down at his heels almost as an afterthought — attaching himself to the venture the way he attached himself to most things, without asking leave and without being refused it.

The sergeant saluted as Paul's boots hit the dock.

"At ease," Paul said. "Fall in as you see fit. Let's find the harbor master." He glanced at the crossbows, still unloaded, waiting on the order that would make them dangerous. "Understood — you don't load until you mean to use them."

Jude drew the sergeant aside a moment, voice pitched low. "A word on my signals, if it comes to that. If I say *flee*, that means find cover and kill whatever's coming at you, however you're able. If I say *get out of here*, that means leave — truly leave — because I may not be able to handle what's happening myself."

"Understood, sir," the sergeant said, repeating it back with the flat precision of a man who took orders seriously. "Flee means hide and fight. Get out means go."

"Exactly that. Let's move."

They set off down the length of the pier, Marines flanking, Paul walking beside them rather than before or behind, Jude a silent shape at his shoulder with one hand resting near a wand he had not yet drawn.

# Chapter Seven — The Empty Harbor

The silence was the worst of it. No harbor master waiting at the dock's end. No patrols on the stairs leading up. Nothing but the slap of water against pilings and the measured tread of Marines' boots on old stone, sixty feet of empty pier between them and the stairwell rising toward the island's interior.

Paul fell in beside the sergeant, keeping his voice low. "I don't mean to step on your command — but I'd have the crossbows loaded and ready now, if it were mine to order. This kind of quiet doesn't sit right with me."

"Nor me, my lord," the sergeant admitted, and raised his voice for the column. "Crossbows up!"

Bolts clicked into place along the line. Jude drew his wand and held it loose and ready at his side, eyes tracking the shadowed hard points above as they advanced, weapons loaded now, toward the waiting stairs.

The first volley came without warning — bolts hissing down from concealed positions built into the stonework overhead. The front rank took the worst of it on raised shields, iron points skittering off scale mail, embedding themselves harmlessly in wood and leather. A shout rang out from somewhere above, and figures began pouring down from the hard points to meet them at the base of the stairs — a full unit, moving with grim purpose.

What followed was ugly, close, and short. Bolts and arrows traded from elevated ground, Marines locking shields and grinding forward step by bloody step, the sergeant's voice cutting through the din — *push forward, shield wall, advance* — as the defenders gave ground grudgingly, contesting every foot of stone.

It was partway through the fighting that Jude went very still, staring hard at the men holding the stairs above.

"These aren't Fairwind's soldiers," he said, low and urgent. "Look at their faces."

Paul looked — really looked — and felt something cold settle in his stomach. Not men at all. Hobgoblins, red-skinned and unmistakably inhuman up close, dressed in armor cut in the exact style of a Monmurgian Marine, down to the buckles.

"Either his garrison's been replaced outright," Paul said grimly, "or the whole island's compromised."

"Either way, we cut through them," Jude said. "No surrender to expect from this lot — not like men who might yield."

The Marines pressed on, the sergeant driving the advance with a steadiness Paul found himself grateful for, and then Jude raised his wand toward a cluster of hobgoblins holding one of the hard points and spoke a single low word.

*Burn.*

Flame roared up and out, catching the tower's timbers as much as the creatures crowded upon it. Their screams were nothing like a man's — high, panicked, utterly wrong — and one didn't clear the blaze in time, swallowed whole as the structure itself began to smoke and buckle.

"Keep moving with them," Paul called, pressing forward alongside the main body as the burning position collapsed behind the advancing shield wall.

The fighting ground on, the defenders thinning position by position as fire spread and the Marines cleared each hard point in grim, methodical turn, until only a pair of hobgoblins remained, holding the base of the stairs with the stubbornness of the doomed.

"I'll finish this," Jude said, and put a bolt of light through the nearer one clean enough that the fight simply ended.

It was only then, in the ringing quiet after, that Paul became aware of something worse unfolding behind them — shouts and the clash of steel carrying from the direction of the docked ship, and beyond it, along the shoreline, motion in the water that had nothing to do with any hobgoblin.

"Something's wrong back there," Jude said sharply. "Call out to the ship."

Paul was already moving. "I'm not leaving the crew exposed to whatever's coming out of that water," he said. "Sea creatures swarming a hull — that's every sailor's nightmare, and it'll break even good men."

"I'm with you," Jude said, falling in at his side.

"Sergeant — secure the landing!" Paul shouted back, gesturing toward the cleared ground around the dock, and watched the man rally what remained of the squad to hold it even as he and Jude broke away, weapons drawn, sprinting back across open ground toward the ship and whatever horror was rising to meet it.

# Chapter Eight — Out of the Deep

Jude and Paul were still fifty yards from the water line when the first of them broke the surface — a shape man-like in outline, but wrong in every particular, a wide, lipless mouth crowded with teeth, eyes flat and black and utterly without warmth.

"Ready yourself," Jude snapped, drawing up short, wand already rising.

A second one surfaced nearby, and beyond it, deeper down the dock, more still — dozens, climbing out of the water and righting themselves on webbed feet, cutting off the straight path to the ship entirely. Past them, barely visible through the chaos, the corsair itself was locked in close, desperate melee, her crew badly outnumbered.

Jude's eyes measured the distance — two hundred feet, at least a hundred of it choked with enemies — and weighed, for one bitter moment, whether a fireball flung now might reach far enough to matter. It wouldn't, not without running straight through three of the creatures and whatever more were crawling up on either side.

"Not worth it," he muttered, and fell back instead, rejoining the sergeant's position, wand held ready. *If anyone closes on Paul, I put them down*, he told himself.

"The ship's got two hundred Marines of her own," he called to Paul. "She's not undefended, even outnumbered. It's our own men we can't lose — they may be all that's left of us before this is done."

Paul fell back with him, crossbow coming up, readied rather than fired. One of the shark-men broke from the pack and charged with a barbed spear leveled.

"Sahaugin," Paul said, recognizing the shape of it even through the dread. "They can't stay ashore forever — they'll need the sea again eventually. Best case, we draw this fight toward open water, away from where they're strongest."

"The whole island counts as coastline, more or less," Jude said grimly, and loosed a single restrained blast from his wand rather than his full strength — enough to slow the creature, not enough to spend himself before the fight had truly begun.

It came on regardless, spear and claw both, and Paul felt the barbed point catch him — once in the ribs, hot and sharp, and again as a webbed claw raked across his chest, the worst of it turned aside by his mask and the leather beneath. He staggered but kept his feet, and as the creature hauled its spear back for the killing thrust he stepped in under its reach and drove his sword up beneath its jaw to the hilt. The sahaugin convulsed, the spear slipping from its webbed hands, and Paul wrenched the blade free and let the thing fold at his boots. One down — but Paul knew it bought only a moment. Already he could feel the first drag of weariness in his arms and lungs, the leaden heaviness that would only deepen the longer the fight wore on; let it run too long, and exhaustion alone might finish what blade and claw had not.

"We need to finish this quickly," Jude said through his teeth, backing a step. "I will burn the lot of them."

"Mind the two of us," Paul managed.

"Already accounted for." Jude drew the spell's shape tight around them both — an evoker's old trick of sparing chosen allies from his own fire — and let it go.

The blast rolled out over the cluster of sahaugin in a wash of white and gold, and this close, even sheltered from the worst of it, Paul felt the heat of it on his face. Two of the creatures never had the chance to scream. A third staggered, blackened and reeling, and the rest scattered, badly burned, barely still standing.

"I've almost nothing left," Jude admitted, low, "and I am not explaining to Lord Jamis how I let the lot of them overrun us. Fall back — get clear of this line."

Paul disengaged, moving fast and low, putting distance between himself and the reach of any remaining blade or claw. One of the burned sahaugin closed on Jude's position but couldn't quite make the distance after its own dash; Jude, retreating steadily, put a bolt of light through its throat as it tried to scramble up onto the dock, and it went down without a sound.

The last one standing came on regardless, scorched black along one side, moving with the desperate, wounded fury of a creature with nothing left to lose. Jude finished it where it stood rather than risk the opening a retreat might offer, then fell back thirty feet in a single hard sprint, Paul at his shoulder.

"We consolidate with the unit," Jude said, breathing hard, scanning the water for any further shapes rising out of the dark. "Push toward the citadel together — no more splitting up."

They ran.

# Chapter Nine — The Barracks

Paul and Jude found the sergeant already standing over one of the fallen hobgoblins, having driven a boot into its ribs to be certain of it — and up close, the wrongness of the thing was plainer still: armor and weapons cut in a style so near to a Monmurg Marine's own that it could only have come from the same craftsmen, turned to some other, darker purpose.

"Corrupted Marines," Jude said flatly, nudging the body with his own boot. "We push for the citadel."

"We push for the citadel," Paul repeated, weighing it.

The sergeant's face, when they asked him for an assessment, told them everything before he spoke a word. He pointed up the hill, where a column of some fifty men was already descending toward them at a steady march.

"My lord's wounded," the sergeant said, looking hard at Paul. "We need him safe."

"How far off, if they meant to rush us?" Paul asked.

"Not yet close enough to matter — but close enough."

"We're outnumbered outright," Jude said. "Is there any hope of joining the second squad?"

"Our best chance," the sergeant said, "is taking this squad and fighting clear to the ship." He hesitated, reconsidering even as he said it. "No — we need cover, and quickly."

"Where?" Paul asked.

"The barracks." The sergeant pointed to a walled compound perhaps a thousand feet distant. "Defensible walls, at least."

"Then that's where we go," Paul said. "There may be men there worth freeing."

"Crossbows ready regardless," Jude added. "There could be enemies waiting there too."

They moved at a hard, disciplined pace rather than a rout — Jude insisting on the distinction even as they went — Marines in formation, shields still up, crossbowmen watching every shadow. Overhead, the column from the Helm continued its own march down toward the docks, ignoring them entirely, bound for whatever fresh disaster was still unfolding at the harbor.

"They're not even looking our way," Jude observed. "Good. Let them stay busy elsewhere." He glanced at Paul, weighing the wound still seeping under his torn leathers. "I can't heal you, but I can make you hard to find." He spoke the words of the spell, and Paul felt the world's attention slide away from him, his own hands fading from sight even as he watched. "Don't fight unless you're truly about to go down — it'll break the moment you do."

"Understood," Paul said, and fell in among the troops, unseen but for the space he left in their formation.

Six hundred feet from the barracks gate, the sergeant called a halt and stepped forward alone, calling up to a shape in the watchtower above. "Who holds this?"

"This barracks is still held for the sea principalities. Who are you?"

"Give the word," the voice above answered.

"On the honor of Jeon."

The sergeant glanced back, satisfied. "That's the password."

The gates opened, and more guardsmen spilled out to help drag the wounded in — sea-principality men, plainly, faces drawn with the particular exhaustion of a garrison that had held under siege for longer than anyone had expected them to.

Inside, a garrison commander met them, his relief plain even through the discipline of his bearing. "You're the relief," he said. "We've been under attack since that unnatural fog rolled over the island. Come — quickly, bring your wounded."

Of the barracks' hundred-man garrison, fifty-seven still stood, untouched by whatever had swept through the rest of the island — a fortunate remnant, holding a position that had somehow gone unassaulted even as everything around it burned.

The commander led Paul and Jude to a quieter corner of the compound, where the sergeant made the introduction plain. "This is Paul Rivera, nephew of Lord Jeon. He's come to speak with Lord Fairwind, regarding the silence from this island since the storm."

"The signal was sent," Paul said, "but not properly coded. What's happened here?"

"The Helm has been taken," the commander said without preamble, "as have most of the lighthouses."

"And Lord Fairwind?"

"No word. We believe he's still within the Helm — the inner sanctum, if he's fortunate. It's a well-defended position; hard for any force to take outright."

"What manner of attackers?"

"Not the shark-folk — that's a separate horror entirely — but something else. Red-faced men, if you've seen them yet."

"Hobgoblins," Paul said.

"Is that the word for them? And working in concert with the sahaugin, it seems — two of our ships in the bay are already sunk."

"Any word from the eastern lighthouse?"

"None. Both remaining lighthouses gone dark, same as the Helm. There's a strange ship been sighted in the bay as well — your own vessels will need to come round the far side of the island to avoid it."

"How many men do you have here, all told?"

"A hundred in garrison, none lost. A hundred twenty-two, counting your own."

Paul turned the numbers over as the commander waited. "We have two choices, as I see it. Wait it out — risky, but with me here, the general will likely respond within a day or two regardless. Or move faster — reach one of the towers, if the signals still function. I'd suggest the one where we left our other troops."

"That may not be the tower you mean," the commander said carefully. "I don't believe we know yet whether that's the one your other men were sent to hold."

"Where is Monmurg, relative to us?" Paul asked, and the commander sketched it out — the bay, the garrison, the small island where the first party had gone ashore, the citadel looming over all of it. "Because they command the water," Paul said slowly, working it through aloud for the commander's benefit as much as his own, "we can't know the true scale of what we're facing. Not just what remains of Fairwind's men — something larger than that."

"You're doing your best to put a bright face on it," the commander observed, not unkindly.

"But we can't sit idle for that reason," Paul went on. "We could send a single runner, but I think we press instead — force word back to Monmurg that more men are needed. We're facing what you call sahaugin —"

"Sahaugin," the commander confirmed.

"— so, a squad. Five men, say."

"Who is this?" the commander asked, with a glance at Jude.

"An advisor of mine."

"Then let me say this to your advisor as much as to you," the commander said. "Any movement toward the lighthouses now is deeply dangerous — the sahaugin move through open water easily, and they're swarming this island in numbers we've never seen from them before. It's strange to me they'd come here at all — they've left this place alone for centuries, since they were broken here long ago."

"Unless," Jude said slowly, "given everything else — the embargo, the isolation, whatever protection this island once carried against them — that protection's weakened. Something's changed, and this is simply the next consequence of it."

"You may be right," the commander admitted, troubled.

"Whatever we do," Jude went on, "it must be done carefully. We can hold this barracks — the red-faced men won't take it from us. But the beaches are a death sentence for anyone who tries them now."

"Which is the greater threat," Paul asked, "the sahaugin, or the hobgoblins?"

"The sahaugin, without question — there are simply more of them. We've seen perhaps a hundred of the red-faced men at most, all come off that boat."

Jude considered the lighthouse visible from where they stood, turning the question over with the particular calculation of a man measuring what his own fire could and couldn't reach. Could he burn one of the towers outright — everyone inside, in a single stroke? He doubted it. The lighthouses stood near eighty feet, several floors apiece; a fireball would gut the base and all who sheltered there, but whoever held the upper stories would ride out the flames and come down after. Not that, then. He kept the reckoning to himself and let it go.

"For now," the commander said, "you should rest, and heal. We've no way to signal at all — every other tower's been taken, and I don't believe it was the hobgoblins who did it. More likely the sahaugin themselves, or the hobgoblins that came ashore during the attack."

"Where do the other towers sit, precisely?" Paul asked. "Is there any way to signal them from here at all?"

"We've tried. Nothing."

Jude considered it a long moment. "Send the name *Karmirg*, once the light allows it. If all three towers can see it, and if the men we left behind still hold that first tower, they'll know the name — friends, not enemies. If the other two answer with nothing but confusion, we'll know what that means as well."

"When the sun sets," the commander said, "we'll make the attempt."

"There's little else to send until then," Paul agreed. "But once we know who's friendly, we can say more."

"A sound plan," the commander said. "It's more than I had on my own. You're wounded — you should rest while there's time for it. We'll bring food. This place is safe enough, for now — the sahaugin won't scale these walls, and the hobgoblins haven't the numbers to take it from us."

"I need four hours to meditate, if I'm to recover what magic I've spent," Jude said.

"You may have that much, God willing — perhaps nearer nine, depending on how the night goes. If we raise our friends out there, we may yet coordinate with them. And if I'm wrong, and they're the enemy after all, they'll have learned nothing they didn't already know."

"If they answer with a name we'd know," Jude put in, "— Gouge, or Jude — then we can trust them with the whole of it." He offered the second name as evenly as the first, giving no sign that it was his own.

"We wait for sunset, then," he said. "It's near enough an hour off."

"It won't be long," the commander agreed, already turning to see to his own duties. "I must go and see to my own prayers, while there's quiet enough for it."

Left to himself, Jude found a quiet corner and settled into the discipline of a short rest, tending the spear-wound at his side and the claw-marks along his neck — worse in memory than in truth, he found, once the blood was cleaned away and the leather peeled back to reveal bruising rather than any lasting harm. He spent the last of his hard-won calm calling back a portion of the magic he'd burned through in the fireball's wake, choosing the shorter recovery over the longer, so that he might still rise ready if the signal came, or if it didn't, and the night demanded something worse of him instead.

---

## PART THREE: THE MARINES' PATH

# Chapter Ten — Tracks on the Beach

They rowed in under a clear sky and calm water, twenty minutes to shore, the whole squad falling into that particular stillness that comes over men who suspect, without quite saying so, that something is wrong before they can name what.

Merrick felt it first as a wrongness in the water itself — more sharks cutting the surface near the rocks than he had ever seen in these waters in his life. He kept his longbow near but unstrung, and passed the word quietly down the boat.

"Keep sharp — there's more shark in this water than I've ever seen here. Might be something died upstream. Might not. Either way, mind yourselves."

"Sharks, near Helm Island?" Tyrus said, low. "There's never been sharks near Helm Island."

"I know," Merrick said, and said nothing else, because there was nothing reassuring left to add.

The docks, when they reached them, offered no better comfort. No one came to greet the boats — no harbor master, no watch, nothing but the beacon fire burning uselessly in broad daylight, and a guard tower standing empty above them where a dozen eyes should have marked their approach a mile off.

"Strange, this time of day especially," Tyrus muttered, and turned to organize the landing — shields to the front, crossbowmen behind, the whole squad forming ranks the moment their boots hit the dock.

"Should we send someone to the tower first?" Merrick asked.

"We go together," Tyrus said. "But let's see what the beach itself tells us before we move." He glanced at Merrick. "See what you can find."

Merrick moved along the tideline while the rest held position, reading the sand the way he'd read a hundred beaches before, and it took the better part of ten minutes before he found what he was looking for — a single print, recent, left since the last tide had scoured the shore clean. Bare. Webbed.

He carried the knowledge back to the sergeant like something heavy, keeping his voice down so the rest of the men wouldn't catch the shape of his unease before he'd said it plainly.

"I don't want to alarm the others," he murmured, "but there's a print here I haven't seen on this coast in a long, long time."

"Spit it out," Tyrus said, catching the edge in his voice even from a distance.

"Sahaugin," Merrick said. "Shark-folk. I'd swear to it."

"Impossible," Tyrus said flatly. "They haven't set foot on this island in centuries."

"I know it. But there were sharks in that water on the way in, more than I've ever seen here, and now this. This island's held some manner of ward against them for longer than anyone living remembers."

"There are greater magics in this world than either of us is privy to," Tyrus said, though his voice had lost its certainty.

"I'm not fighting them in the water, whatever the truth of it," Merrick said. "Let's get up to the quay."

They climbed the stairs in formation — ten wide, two ranks deep, shields at the front, crossbowmen behind, Tyrus leading, Merrick falling back to keep watch on their rear as they advanced toward the barracks and the tower beyond.

The windows told the story before the arrows did. Twice, in the space of a hundred paces, someone caught a shape in a window — the tower, the barracks — gone the instant anyone looked directly at it, quick and shy and watching all the same.

"Someone's in the barracks!" Tyrus shouted, and the words had barely left him before a crossbow bolt struck his shield hard enough to stagger him — fired from an arrow slit in the tower above, from a shooter none of them had seen in time to dodge.

And then the tower's defenders were no longer hiding at all. Figures poured out onto every defensible ledge and doorway, weapons already leveled, and the fight that had been building since the empty docks finally broke wide open.

# Chapter Eleven — The Tower

The volley came first — a hail of bolts from the tower's heights, most of it turned aside by raised shields, but not all of it. Three men went down in that opening exchange before anyone could drag them clear, leaving seventeen where twenty had stood a moment before.

Up close, in the chaos of return fire, the defenders resolved into something far stranger than the mercenaries anyone had expected. Red-skinned, broad-jawed, unmistakably inhuman even through the visor slits of armor cut in an unnervingly familiar style.

"Hobgoblins," Merrick called out, recognition dawning cold and sure. "I've heard of troops like these before — used by certain nobles out of Berghof. God knows what they're doing this far from home."

The fighting ground on in grim exchanges, bolt for bolt, the Marines returning fire from what cover the terrain allowed while the tower's defenders picked at them from height. It was Tyrus who broke the stalemate, throwing his shoulder against the tower's door alongside two of his men, feeling the bar within crack and give beneath their combined weight.

The door burst inward on five waiting hobgoblins, one stepping forward in a breastplate to meet the charge himself, and Tyrus went in without hesitation, sword ringing against the creature's blade in the first of what would become a long, brutal exchange. He took a solid hit, and another, blood running warm down his side, and held his footing anyway.

"Don't give away more than you have to!" he called back to the men crowding the doorway behind him, even as more bolts rained down from above, claiming another Marine and wounding two more.

Folsom pressed in near the doorway, flinging a cutting verse of Vicious Mockery at one of the defenders — enough to sting, not enough to fell him — before the Marines surged past their commander in a solid wall, engaging the remaining hobgoblins room by room.

"Way to go!" Folsom called to Aristotle over the din, snatching a quick, bright phrase on his flute even amid the chaos, the kind of small encouragement that meant more in a fight like this than anyone not present would ever quite believe.

The hobgoblin leader landed two solid blows on the sergeant before the fight turned — the first enough to draw a fresh line of blood, the sergeant grimly shrugging it off with something close to a laugh: "Take my hair, then, if that's the best you've got" — and the second bad enough that Tyrus felt his stomach drop at the sight of it, the sergeant swaying, barely upright, before somehow finding his feet again.

Room by room, the Marines cleared the tower's lower levels, hobgoblins falling back as their numbers thinned. A trapdoor further up refused to budge under Tyrus's shoulder, and rather than risk being caught exposed on a ladder, he chose to climb back down and regroup rather than force the issue.

Reinforcements found them there — more Marines rounding the corner, one pausing beside Folsom with open concern.

"You're bleeding," the man said.

"Must be someone else's," Folsom said, checking himself over and finding, to his own mild surprise, that he was mostly whole.

Merrick pressed forward alongside Gouge to finish off one of the last defenders, landing a solid, decisive blow, and the hobgoblin who'd taken it staggered, badly wounded, before one of his fellows simply dropped his weapon outright at the sight of the reinforcements pouring in.

"We need archers up here — now!" Tyrus called down, and as the last of the defenders broke and ran, he turned to the men nearest him. "This one — the one who surrendered. Do we let him live?"

"Not a chance," Gouge said, flat and certain, and it was done before anyone could argue otherwise.

The retreat, when it came, was ragged and short-lived — the fleeing hobgoblins picked off as they ran, unable to answer fire while their backs were turned, until the last of them vanished beyond effective range and the tower fell, at last, silent.

They had won it. But the cost sat plain in the wounded groaning on the stone floor, and in the sergeant himself, upright only by will alone, blood soaking through the shoulder of his tunic as the unit began, raggedly, to regroup.

# Chapter Twelve — The Karmirg Signal

The tower wasn't fully theirs even then. Bolts still fell from the barracks buildings nearby, killing three more Marines before anyone could drag the bodies clear, and for a long, grinding stretch the fight settled into a stalemate — neither side willing to break from cover, seventeen of the original twenty left standing, most of them wounded, the archers holding the tower's upper floor without daring to show themselves at the windows.

"Is he dead," Tyrus asked, of the one hobgoblin they'd managed to bring down without killing outright, "or worth keeping?"

No one among them spoke Hobgoblin, but the sergeant took the creature into custody regardless, on the chance it might prove useful, while the rest of the wounded defenders were put down without ceremony.

"There are still men on that wall," Gouge said, eyes already climbing the stonework. "I want them dealt with directly."

"Now, then," Tyrus agreed, and laid out the shape of it quickly — himself hammering on the main door to draw every eye, Gouge and Folsom scaling opposite flanks of the wall while the defenders' attention was fixed elsewhere.

"I'll go up quiet," Gouge said. "I don't fancy a volley of arrows the moment my head clears the parapet."

Both of them made the climb in near-perfect silence, and Gouge risked a careful look over the edge rather than simply vaulting the wall — two hobgoblins standing there, swords drawn, listening hard to the racket Tyrus was making below.

"Surrender," Gouge called, in plain Common. "We've taken the tower."

"We surrender," one answered, broken but clear enough, and threw down his sword.

Gouge came over the wall anyway and struck without hesitation, driving a wound deep into the creature before it could think better of its own surrender. It didn't fall outright, and Tyrus was there a heartbeat later to finish what Gouge had started. The second hobgoblin, already wounded, drew a dagger despite its companion's fate and lunged — Gouge caught the worst of it on his buckler, took a shallow cut for his trouble, and put the creature down with a return stroke that ended the matter for good.

"That's the pair of them," Gouge said, with the grim satisfaction of a man closing accounts.

They found the hatch at the tower's summit and forced it open, only to discover the brazier that should have stood ready there was simply gone — no signal fire, nothing to warn the approaching corsair of what waited on the island below.

"We need to signal that ship, somehow," Gouge said. "A flaming arrow, anything that'll carry."

"Longbow's got the range for it," Gouge decided, weighing it against the heavier crossbows. "Two hundred feet further, easily."

"Let's put color to it," Folsom said, already digging through his entertainer's kit for anything that might burn bright and strange enough to read as a true signal rather than an ordinary fire. He found phosphorus among his oddments, scraped nickel filings from a fallen hobgoblin's blade, soaked a strip of cloth in a measure of Gouge's own harsh liquor for good measure, and wrapped the whole improvised mess around a bolt's tip.

"Up and out," Gouge said, sighting toward the distant ship rather than the water below. "Not down."

The bolt arced high, trailing a smoky, unmistakably blue flame across the evening sky, and a flare answered from the corsair a moment later — acknowledgment, at least, that someone aboard had seen it.

"Good work," Gouge said to Folsom, watching the flare fade.

But the ship sailed on toward the docks regardless, and a squad of Marines began disembarking even as they watched — and among them, unmistakable even at this distance in the last low light of evening, the glint of a silvered mask.

"That's fantastic," Gouge said grimly, "and also terrible, given we're meant to be protecting that particular man."

"We report everything to the sergeant," Tyrus said. "All of it — the signal, the ship, everything."

The sergeant took the news with the flat calm of a man who had already run out of good options. "You're wounded," he said to Tyrus, unconvinced by any protest to the contrary.

"I'm fit to move, sergeant." Tyrus laid his own hands over the worst of his wounds, murmuring the words that had never failed him yet, and felt the ache close over into nothing more than memory. "Good as new."

"You can't march out there with archers still active in that other building," the sergeant said.

"That'd be my recommendation regardless — unless you've a better one. Our orders were to take the barracks. If Paul Rivera dies out there before we can reach him, does that count as our mission failed outright?"

"You tell me," the sergeant said. "We're near a mile from those docks. Twenty minutes at a dead run, even without a fight in our way, and he's got two hundred Marines with him already. There's nothing more we can do from here but rest, and watch."

So they watched — helpless, from a distance too great to close — as Paul's detachment marched the length of the pier toward the quay, and soldiers atop the walls opened fire on them in turn, the exchange playing out across the bay like something happening to strangers in another life entirely.

"Where are they, exactly, relative to us?" Gouge asked.

"Far side of the bay," the sergeant said. "Hobgoblins hold the bridge between here and there. We're pinned as surely as they are."

A flare of magical flame bloomed suddenly among the distant hobgoblin positions.

"Someone on our side is buying them time," Tyrus said, low.

And then the water itself began to move — pale, man-shaped things crawling up out of the sea by the hundred, swarming the docks and the ship alike.

"That's sahaugin," Merrick said, the words coming out of him like something torn loose. "Hundreds of them. On the Helm. I can't credit it — this hasn't happened in centuries."

They watched the first squad of Marines reach the top of the quay and vanish from sight — their own people, Paul's escort among them — even as the corsair itself disappeared beneath a swarm of shapes like ants overrunning some larger, dying thing.

"Can they even fight up here, away from the water?" Tyrus asked, horrified.

Merrick called on everything he'd ever learned of the old enemy and found, at least, some small comfort in the answer. "They can survive ashore near four hours before they'd suffocate for want of the sea — but they hate it, and they'd rarely come this far inland unless truly desperate."

"Then we hold the high ground," Tyrus said, "and stay well clear of any coastline."

"We're watching our own people be overrun," Gouge said, low and furious, "and there's nothing we can do about it from here."

"If it comes to a choice," Tyrus said, quiet and hard, "Paul stays protected above nearly everything else — that's the mission, whatever the cost to the rest of us. The elf with him can likely see to himself. But Paul comes first." He turned to Merrick. "Whatever you know about this island, now's the time to share it."

"I know the political weight of keeping him alive," Merrick said. "But there's something else at work here too — something we were sent specifically to look into, something wrong with this place that none of us wanted to alarm the others with before now. That fog wasn't natural. Nothing in all my years walking this coast prepared me for what we're seeing tonight. It isn't only the sahaugin — someone worked magic against this island, and unless we can gather real strength and move as one—"

"Are the men drunk?" the sergeant asked, skeptical of the whole account.

"Not yet," Gouge said, "though the day's given me every reason to be."

"You're speaking nonsense."

"I haven't seen the sahaugin do anything like this in all my years," Merrick said, quieter now, something old and unhealed moving behind his eyes. "My own father was killed by hobgoblins, they told me, once. And now this."

"Fewer fish-men, more solid ground beneath us," Tyrus muttered, pacing the length of the tower's parapet. "That's all I ask of tonight."

They rested where they stood, tending wounds as best the hour allowed — Gouge spending what strength he had left to knit closed the worst of his own hurts and then, at the sergeant's quiet urging, doing the same for eight men worse off than himself, until only two remained too badly hurt to march, one of them barely conscious.

"Do we leave them," Gouge asked no one in particular, low enough that only Merrick caught it, "or do we find some other use for men who can't walk?" He went and checked on both himself before deciding anything, unwilling to leave the question to guesswork alone.

He climbed back to where he'd loosed his arrows earlier and recovered every one of them from the field below, methodical even in exhaustion, while Merrick studied the darkening water and weighed what the night ahead might still demand of them.

"You know this island as well as anyone breathing," Gouge said to him. "Can we move safely after dark, staying off the beaches?"

"The hobgoblins at the barracks likely see well enough in the dark to trouble us at close range," Merrick said, "but not at any real distance, and moonlight weakens that regardless. We should be able to move, if we're careful. This stretch of island should already be swept clear of anything worse than birds and a few tree-dwellers — nothing left alive here that hasn't already been hunted off or killed."

"Then we move tonight," Gouge said.

The sergeant, when they laid the plan before him, was cautious to the point of reluctance. "I'd rather not send men out blind into whatever's still out there. But Merrick knows this ground better than any of us — send him ahead, under cover of dark, to learn whether the barracks still hold."

"I'll go with him," Gouge said at once.

"We both move well enough in the dark," Merrick said. "You watch my back; I'll find the way."

"If you reach the barracks and find it clear," the sergeant asked, "how will you signal us?"

Merrick checked his explorer's pack and found what he needed. "A tinderbox. I can light an arrow — enough for you to see from a distance, even if it gives away exactly where we are for a moment."

"That'll mark your position to the enemy as well as to us."

"If we're low against the wall by then, I doubt they'd risk exposing themselves just to answer it," Merrick said. "We'll work out the exact signal as we go."

It was near an hour later that one of the remaining archers came hurrying in from the wall. "Sir — there's a signal from the direction of the barracks."

Gouge read it without difficulty, the old training surfacing easily enough even after years away from it — a light blinking out, over and over, the same broken word.

*K-A-R-M-I-R-G. Karmirg. Karmirg.*

"Signal back," Gouge said. "G-O-U-G-E. And wait."

The reply came haltingly, half-garbled in the returning light, but the shape of it resolved clear enough between the two of them: the barracks was held, a hundred fifty Marines safe within its walls, though the Helm itself had fallen. Paul Rivera was there among them. *Come as soon as you're able,* the message closed.

"I don't want to bring the whole party," Gouge said, weighing thirty exhausted men against a hundred fifty already safe. "Signal that we're coming tonight."

"We're on our way," he sent back, and turned to what remained of the group. "No time for a full rest — we go now, and take it properly once we're behind those walls with them."

"What of the enemy, moving in the dark?" the sergeant asked.

"I doubt they're capable of much tonight," Gouge said. "But we hold this bridge regardless — leave men and archers here to keep it, and this tower besides. It ties down whatever's still out there, keeps them from moving up behind us, and gives us a place to signal from if Jeon sends another ship."

He turned to Merrick. "You know this ground better than any of us — lead us to the barracks. I'll be at your shoulder the whole way."

"What's your true name, by the way," Merrick asked him, half in earnest, half simply worn thin by the day, "when you're not playing at being someone else?"

"Jude knows. Karmirg knows. That's enough people carrying it around for one lifetime," Gouge said. "Gouge will do. My cover's blown regardless, for now — but keep the rest of it to yourselves all the same."

"Understood," Merrick said.

They chose their escort with care — two crossbowmen, three shield-bearers, the sergeant himself staying behind to hold the tower and keep the signal line open, Tyrus and Folsom joining once word came that the way ahead was truly clear.

"Stay off the beaches," Gouge warned them all, one last time, before they moved out into the dark. "None of us see well enough at night to trust to it blindly, and the sahaugin own that water after sundown more than they ever have before tonight. Mind the bridge, and mind each other."

And with that, what remained of the southern squad slipped out into the darkness of Helm Island, bound for the barracks, and for whatever waited with Paul Rivera behind its walls.

---

## PART FOUR: SIGNALS IN THE DARK

# Chapter Thirteen — Word From the Tower

Sunset came grudgingly, the sky over the Helm gone the color of a healing bruise, and with the last of the light the garrison commander made good on his word. Up on the barracks' watch-platform, a signalman worked a hinged mirror against the dying sun, tilting it in short, practiced bursts — the old code of the sea principalities, dots and dashes of reflected fire, the same method that had once carried word the length of the Monmurgian coast in a matter of minutes.

*Karmirg,* the mirror said, over and over, into the gathering dark.

For a long stretch there was nothing but the commander's patience and the creak of the platform underfoot. Then, faint but unmistakable, an answering flicker kindled from the direction of the Southern Guard Tower — a name flashing back across the strait, deliberate and slow, as though whoever held the glass on the other end wanted no possibility of it being misread.

*G-O-U-G-E.*

Paul let out a breath he hadn't known he was holding. "That's him," he said. "Or someone who knows to say so."

"Either way," Jude said, "we're not alone out here anymore."

The exchange that followed came in fits and starts, the signalman calling out each word as it resolved, Paul and Jude standing close at the rail to hear it. The Marines who had landed at the southern docks had been set upon by hobgoblins in their turn — the same red-skinned, borrowed-armored horror — and had fought their way into the wooden watchtower there, holding it now with twenty-seven men where thirty had started the day. They had watched the corsair go down in the harbor, though not the particulars of it; they could only report that the ship had vanished beneath the swarm and not been seen since.

"Wait," Jude said, when the signalman relayed that piece. "The ship's actually gone?"

"They didn't see the end of it," the commander said, "only that it was overrun, and that nothing's come from that direction since. They assume the worst, and so do I."

They were anxious, too, for word of Paul specifically — a prince's blood being no small thing to lose track of in the middle of a battle — having watched his detachment march the length of the pier before the fighting swallowed them from view.

"Did they see us reach the beach?" Paul asked.

"They saw you go as far as the stairs," the commander relayed. "After that, nothing — they'd hoped you made the barracks, but hadn't dared assume it. They know now."

Word went back the other way in turn — the numbers Paul had helped the commander reckon earlier: the barracks' own hundred, the twenty who had come ashore with Paul, and now the tower's twenty-seven, set against whatever remained of the southern squad's original strength. A hundred fifty Marines still held out somewhere on the island proper, the tower's answering flashes confirmed — which put the full reckoning, once losses were weighed against everything the night had already cost, somewhere near two hundred men still capable of fighting, scattered across three isolated points that could not yet see or reach one another.

"We'll be fighting the sahaugin on top of the hobgoblins, then," Jude said, low, watching the mirror flash its patient rhythm across the dark water.

"They're a different problem entirely," Paul said. "For now it's the hobgoblins that matter most. The sahaugin can't come up onto this land and stay — not for long, and not by choice."

The tower meant to hold its position regardless, the signal made clear, if only to keep any relief ship from Monmurg from sailing blind into the same trap that had already claimed one corsair.

"Could that tower reach all the way back to the city?" Paul asked. "Isn't that what it was built for?"

The answer, when it came, was no — not any longer. The brazier meant for a true signal fire had been carried off by the hobgoblins along with everything else; all that remained to the men holding the tower were a handful of short-range flares, enough to warn a ship at close range but nothing that could carry word across open water to Monmurg itself.

"What happens to them if the sahaugin decide to press the tower directly?" Jude asked. "That's not much of a position to hold against a real push."

The fortifications there were modest, the tower's own men admitted through the mirror, and they were asking for relief all the same — though for the moment, clear of the open beach, they didn't rate the sahaugin as their most pressing danger.

"Does that square with what we already know of them?" Paul asked.

It did, so far as the commander understood it. The shark-folk could come ashore and endure it for a handful of hours before the lack of water began to tell on them, but they hated the exposure and rarely pushed inland by choice — which meant any position built up on stone or behind a proper wall held a real advantage over them, whatever terror they inspired down on the sand.

One message remained, and it came last, in a longer, more deliberate hand than the rest — Gouge's own, unmistakable even reduced to dots and dashes of borrowed sunlight. *Coming tonight,* it read. *Gathering what men I can, bringing them in with me. We'll slip past the hobgoblins somehow. Expect us before morning.* Whatever came next, the message implied, would have to be worked out face to face, once he and his crew had made it through the dark.

Paul lowered the mirror's answering flag and looked out at the black water, the last of the daylight gone now, the island's fires — theirs and the enemy's both — the only light left between here and the Helm's brooding silhouette.

"Then we'd best have a plan ready by the time he arrives," he said.

# Chapter Fourteen — All In

They took their short rest where they could find it — a bench, a corner of the commander's own quarters — and by nine that evening, restored enough to think clearly, Paul and Jude found a quiet moment alone to speak plainly before anyone else's counsel crowded the room.

"We need to know how many hobgoblins actually made it ashore," Jude said, pacing a short line across the floorboards, restless in the low lamplight. "If the captain's vague on the number, that's going to cost him some trust with me."
