"He's told us everything he knows so far," Paul said, settling into a chair with the deliberate calm of a man determined not to match the elf's agitation. "That's not nothing. It's not the behavior of someone in league with them."

"I'm not saying he is. I just want the numbers. Unless you think he is?"

"No — well." Paul spread his hands, a small, wry gesture. "I suspect everybody, if I'm honest. Isn't that the job?"

"Fairwind's not exactly a man I'd vouch for either," Jude admitted, dropping into the chair across from him at last. "I've been turning it over for a day now — sorry, it's been sitting on me."

"It's fine. You asked why I'd suspect someone, and the truth is I don't suspect everyone specifically. Go ask the captain how many hobgoblins he's counted."

"He's not Fairwind, though."

"No, I know that." Paul rubbed at the edge of his mask, an old habit standing in for rubbing his eyes. "It's Fairwind himself that's strange to me. Maybe he's simply held up somewhere in that keep, unable to get word out."

"If he's still loyal," Jude said, "he's either a prisoner, or he's dug himself into the inner sanctum and is defending it alone. Either way, that would keep him hidden from us as much as from them."

"If Gouge and his people make it here," Paul said, "I'll commit to moving on the keep tonight."

"If Gouge makes it — and I've no reason to think he won't — are you truly ready for that? Tonight?"

"Yes," Paul said, without hesitation this time.

Jude studied him a moment, weighing the answer. "Do you carry anything for your own protection? Some device, some trick? I don't need the particulars — I only want to know if there's some gap I ought to be covering."

"Nothing like that," Paul admitted.

"Did Jeon or Jamis give you any instruction beyond what you've told me?"

"You know everything I do," Paul said. "Come here, confirm all's well, and be seen doing it — represent Jeon properly. That's the whole of it."

Jude turned that over, then set it aside for a more practical question. "How do our twenty stack up against the garrison here — better trained, worse, or level?"

"Level," Paul judged, after a moment's thought. "Same drill, same arms — Marines are Marines. Though ours answer to me more directly than the garrison does, in theory. The captain's still senior in practice — if Fairwind were here, he'd command outright, but I carry enough weight as Jeon's blood that he listens. Think of it as a general taking counsel from someone he can't quite overrule."

"Then the real question," Jude said, "is whether we go all in with the captain and the garrison behind us — a hundred seventy men, near enough — or hold what we have and wait."

"We could go all in," Paul said, "but I want you fronting it with the captain, not me. They're going to look to you before they look to me."

"Wait — I thought you were the one working the signal, just now, with the mirror."

"That's the tower holding its own position," Paul said. "Warning off relief ships so they don't sail blind into whatever sank the corsair. That's separate. What I mean is — Lord Jamis was plain that the Helm has to be secured. We hold the barracks, but without the keep itself, we don't hold the island. We're going to have to move on it, and the captain's the one who can tell us how."

"I'm bringing this to you first," Paul went on, "because it needs to look like you're the one leading, not me. I don't want every word out of my mouth met with 'and who exactly is this.'"

"Fair enough — though I ask myself the same question some days," Jude said, something dry beneath it. "I've fought in wars before. I'm also trying rather hard not to die in this one."

"Neither do I," Paul said. "But this is about the rank you carry, not the rank I do. You need to be the one doing most of the talking."

"So you want me speaking for us."

"I'll go fetch the captain," Paul said, already rising. "Frame it as a distraction, if that helps — an assault meant to draw eyes, even if in truth it does more than that."

"That's exactly what I was thinking," Paul added, half to himself, working through the captain's likely temperament. "I doubt he wants to climb a ladder under a rain of arrows. He'll favor something quieter — a hole blown in a wall, a couple of guards silenced, a stretch of stone scaled in the dark. He'll hear that and say 'good, let's do that' — which means it'll likely be him, the two of us, and whatever crew Gouge brings with him."

"But if we can get the captain to commit everything he has—"

"Then we do whatever the moment calls for," Paul said. "Rescuing Fairwind. Finishing him, if it comes to that. You understand."

"I do. Either way, the keep still has to fall."

"So — all in, or dig in and wait? I don't know how we stand on food and water."

"The captain mentioned weeks of both," Jude said.

"Then worst case, if this goes badly, we fall back here and hold out that long," Paul said. "We could survive that."

"I say we go all in," Jude decided. "We've no way out regardless, short of another ship."

"I'd guess two days before Monmurg sends one," Paul said. "Three, at the outside."

"With hobgoblins loose on the island, that's a long two days," Jude said. "I don't know that Gouge's thirty can hold if they're pressed hard — depends entirely on how many of the enemy are still out there, away from the barracks and the towers."

"Let's put it to the captain," Paul said, and went to send for him.

# Chapter Fifteen — The Captain's Ledger

The garrison captain arrived within minutes, a lean, weathered man with the particular patience of someone who had answered a great many royal summonses in his time and expected this to be one more. He inclined his head to Paul.

"Prince — how might I serve?"

"Your best estimate," Paul said, "of how many hobgoblins hold this island."

The captain considered the question with the care of a man doing sums in his head. "At least a hundred confirmed, moving in and out of the Helm itself — how they came to be there at all, I couldn't say. I'd assume they overran the hundred fifty who garrisoned the keep, though we've only laid eyes on perhaps a hundred patrolling this side of the island. Smaller bands of twenty or thirty have been sighted elsewhere. Every beacon's gone dark. However many squads it took, I'd wager twenty to forty of them were needed just to take those positions outright."

"That tracks with what we've seen," Paul said.

"Add the southern tower's account — fifty encountered, twenty killed, thirty left standing against them — and the full count likely runs past two hundred. Perhaps two hundred fifty."

"That's the strength of a proper warship's complement," Paul said, "though we've seen no such vessel anywhere in the harbor. Is there another place on this island where a ship that size could put men ashore unseen?"

"If they had safe anchorage, moved by night, or had someone inside working the gates for them — otherwise I'd fear something worse behind it."

"It was the fog," Jude said quietly. "The storm that rolled in."

"That fog killed every man's sight past five feet," the captain agreed, something grim settling into his voice. "They could have slipped in under it entirely unseen, and I believe that's precisely what happened. Once it lifted, our lookouts could see in some directions and not others — but we know now there's no ship waiting on the island's far side."

"So if they were landed at all," Paul said, "it happened during the fog itself."

"Just so. There's an anchorage on that side, a stretch of beach that would serve — they could have stood off and ferried men ashore in small boats. How they breached the gate itself, I couldn't guess, unless they had someone inside to lower the drawbridge for them. There's no moat, only a ramp and the bridge — meant for heavy traffic, not for keeping anyone out on its own. The fortifications otherwise are formidable; a hundred fifty men could hold every face of that keep at once."

"One more thing, if you'll indulge it," Paul said. "Is your water shipped in, or drawn from wells here?"

"Shipped, entirely. No freshwater source on the island save rain, caught in cisterns. That's the whole of our supply."

"Is there any manner of tunnel beneath the fortress," Jude asked, "sewers, or otherwise?"

The captain studied him a long moment before answering, weighing, perhaps, how much a prince's household truly ought to know. "That's a closely held secret — but Prince Jeon sent you, so I'll trust you're meant to hear it. There are rumored passages beneath the Helm, a cave system navigable by small boat, an old smugglers' route from centuries past. I've never set foot down there myself; the Admiralty keeps it close. But the rumor places an entrance somewhere on this side of the island. If the hobgoblins knew of it, that might explain how they came up from beneath — though how they'd have found their way through those caverns at all, I couldn't begin to guess. Understand, this is rumor, nothing I could swear to."

"I ask," Jude said, "because I'm concerned the sahaugin might use any water-fed tunnel to reach the interior."

"That's the strangest part of tonight," the captain said. "Historically, the sahaugin have never been able to reach this island at all — it's held some manner of warding for over a century, since the last great war against their kind. Even sharks give these waters a wide berth, or did."

"Who maintains that warding?" Jude pressed. "Or has it simply held on its own strength all this time?"

"That it's broken now is the true crisis, more than the hobgoblins," the captain said. "I couldn't tell you who tends it, or how — only Lord Fairwind and the Admiralty hold that knowledge."

"Is the enchantment tied to the turtle dragons in any way?" Jude asked.

"I know nothing of that," the captain admitted. "I've heard the great turtles favor sahaugin much as they favor sharks — natural enemies, if the old sailors' talk is to be believed. If sharks avoid these waters, perhaps that's the whole of it, and the sharkmen inherited the same instinct."

"If that protection's truly gone," Paul said, half to himself, "it may tie into everything else pressing on Monmurg just now — the blockade included."

He turned back to the captain. "You've spent time enough in these waters without being native to the sea principalities yourself — do the different princes' Marines carry distinguishing marks? Colors, by house?"

"Without question. The design's near-identical across the board — scale mail, standard-issue bows for the archers, commanders in breastplates like mine. But I don't recognize the colors these hobgoblins wear."

"Heraldry, or simple war paint?"

"War colors, plainly. Blue for Fairwind's men. Green for Monmurg. Yellow would mark Toli, who I'd expect us to be fighting regardless, given the state of things."

"And the hobgoblins?"

"A strange brownish red. Belongs to no prince I've ever served under. The armor beneath it, though, is close enough to ours to trouble me."

"Would it be wise," Paul asked, "to throw everything we have at the Helm directly, and try to retake it outright?"

"We'd die for nothing," the captain said flatly. "They hold the height, archers looking down the length of any approach. Barring those rumored tunnels, the only way in is the front gate, and the walls stand fifty feet."

"So we'd be forced through the same gate they used," Paul said.

"Just so. And even past the gate, the garrison house, the tower, and the inner sanctum are each separately walled off — it's a wonder the hobgoblins managed it at all. Breaching the gate is only the beginning of the work."

"If Fairwind still holds the inner sanctum," Paul said, "with whatever's left of his own men — say two-thirds lost, if he was caught unprepared — would there be value in linking with him, if he could break out from within?"

"There's an agent already making for us from the tower," the captain said. "You had the same message I did — bringing men of his own. Between that and whatever Fairwind can muster, I'd say we'll find some way in."

"You mean to rescue my lord?" Jude asked, the question carrying more weight in his mouth than the words alone explained.

"If he's alive, and able to help retake the Helm, yes. Even if all he manages is bringing down that gate from the inside, that alone would be worth a great deal — though we'd still have the main tower to fight through after. Everything depends on how many men he has left against how many hold the walls. Charging that gate outnumbered would be a slaughter."

"Would you expect them to pull men off to retake the positions we already hold?" Paul asked.

"They might draw some — the South Beacon, this barracks — but a force big enough to matter risks being cut off itself, given we've near a hundred seventy men between the landing party and this garrison. We could block any such push, and turn the numbers against them if it came to open ground. We might even consider retaking the Western Beacon ourselves, or the others, to warn shipping properly from every direction."

"Do you have the means to repair a beacon, if we retake one? Oil, polish, that sort of thing?"

"Oil enough, yes — though not if the fixture itself is gone, which is what the southern men reported. We do keep spare braziers, and the makings of a proper signal fire."

The captain's mouth tightened before he went on, unprompted. "The honest trouble is that until these hobgoblins are driven off entirely, I don't see real relief coming. Why the warding failed, none of us can say — only Fairwind, and the Admiralty above him, understand how it was maintained. He'd know the why of it, if anyone living does."

"Does Fairwind hold any magical gift himself, to your knowledge?" Paul asked.

"I couldn't say whether any of the princes do. I've never been briefed on such matters — that knowledge stays close to the Admiralty alone."

"On the matter of the smuggling tunnels," Jude said, "is there anyone under your command with service on this island old enough to know something of them firsthand?"

"None under me," the captain said, with a small shake of his head. "Admitting to such knowledge would be treason on my part regardless. Only the pilots know for certain, and they answer to the Admiralty directly, not to me. All I have is the vague word that it lies somewhere on the northern coast — a well-hidden cave, the waters treacherous enough that only a small craft could manage the approach."

"Only reachable by water, then?" Jude asked.

"That, and the tide besides. And the sahaugin swim those waters better than any of us — they'd have you in pieces before you came within reach of it."

"They wouldn't expect us," Paul said, "if we went carefully enough."

"Caution would serve you well regardless," the captain said. "I'd not send anyone after that without real confidence in their stealth."

"I don't see how we could assault it outright, in any case," Paul admitted.

"We can't restore the warding at all without Fairwind first," the captain said. "That much I'm certain of."

"Undoubtedly," Paul agreed. "And we still need at least one working beacon for the signal fire, regardless of everything else."

He turned the thought over aloud, half for the captain's benefit and half his own. "Even with braziers relit, would that truly reach Monmurg, or only warn ships passing near?"

"It would do both, in truth," the captain said. "Ships passing at night would see the light and steer wide, and word would carry to the city better than it does now. Retaking even one tower would be worth doing for that alone."

"I think what the captain means," Paul said to Jude, "is that the broken warding against the sahaugin is the true crisis here — more than the hobgoblins, more than anything else."

"It is," the captain confirmed. "If reinforcement comes and the sahaugin swarm the bay the way they did tonight, we could lose entire companies before they ever make land — as we already lost two hundred men aboard that corsair. Short of a full assault with a thousand men or better, depending on their numbers, that threat alone could break us."

"How would you go about retaking the other two towers, given we already hold the southern one?" Paul asked.

"The eastern position would be hardest — we'd stand in the shadow of the Helm's own cliffs, exposed to fire from above the moment we moved. The Western Beacon would be easier. Fifty men, marching tonight, could likely retake it, depending on how many hold it now. If each remaining position carries fifty defenders, that's a hundred fifty on top of the hundred already seen marching from the Helm — which puts us squarely in that two hundred fifty to three hundred range you'd already guessed at."

"So fifty men, at minimum, holding each of those places?"

"That would be my estimate. Add perhaps thirty more moving through the interior, and the barracks itself still needing a proper garrison — I'd want seventy men here at all times, without fail, which leaves a hundred free for any assault we mount. I wouldn't split our strength any further than that. Strike one beacon with full force, hold it, and move from there."

"How many of them have you killed so far, all told?"

"Twenty, by my count — the southern garrison claims the same. We ourselves haven't taken any yet, here at the barracks."

"So we land close to that two hundred fifty figure regardless," Paul said.

"Roughly so. We're matched in numbers, perhaps slightly outmatched, but they hold height and stonework in their favor. If we take a beacon quickly enough, they may even turn men back toward it as a distraction of their own. I expect Monmurg's agent — Gouge, you called him — will want to breach the Helm himself, and soon. Climbing those walls unseen would be no small feat for anyone."

"It might be managed," the captain allowed, "with the right distraction — an assault at the front to draw every eye, while others go over the walls where no one's watching."

"If we stage that kind of distraction," Paul asked, "is there a real chance of slipping into the Helm from the seaward side?"

"With a proper assault on the gate, yes — you'd have to climb by the garrison house and drop into the courtyard from there. It would take real skill at the climb, and the cliffs themselves would need scaling first."

"With rope, and Gouge's own skill, that's manageable," Jude said. "And I have magic that can lift a man where rope won't reach."

The captain's eyes sharpened with sudden interest. "You can levitate a man?"

"I can."

"Then it wants to be a small group," the captain said. "Any larger, and the men still holding that wall would simply cut down whoever tried to climb. But if we've drawn most of their strength to the gate, and left only a token force above, a small party might slip past unnoticed."

"With the fewest lives spent as possible," Paul said, "could you offer a real distraction without a genuine assault behind it? Some show of force enough to hold their attention?"

"We could march up, begin assembling a ram in plain sight, and trade fire at range with whatever holds the wall," the captain said. "That alone should serve to fix their eyes on the gate."

"How long would it take a hundred men to retake that tower tonight, assuming no more than fifty defenders?"

"I couldn't say precisely how well fifty men could hold such a place — but I'd guess two hours, given a determined push."

"If that's our window to get into position," Paul said, "could your men also begin maneuvering before the Helm itself — the ram, the show of aggression — to hold their attention there as well?"

"That's entirely doable."

"Would night favor us more than dawn, do you think, with fires already lighting the field?"

"I doubt it matters greatly, given how desperate things already look from where they stand," the captain said. "Retaking the Western Beacon tonight is the sensible move regardless — we need that light burning for shipping's sake. And any true attempt on the Helm itself would logically come after dark, when confusion favors us more than them."

"Then once we're in position," Paul said, "you'd signal readiness, and we'd time the climb for one or two in the morning?"

"We can coordinate the signal to suit — you'll want to be certain we're ready before you commit to the wall, but two in the morning serves well enough."

"Rope — plain rope, nothing more particular?"

"We have plenty."

"I can't carry everyone myself," Jude said, "but between the rope and what magic I have left, some can climb while others are lifted."

"Could we recover any of the hobgoblin dead from the harbor, under cover of dark?" Paul asked. "Their armor, specifically."

"We can send men for that, yes."

"Only to secure the bodies — no needless risk. But if we can salvage that gear, we might pass for hobgoblins ourselves at any distance, once we're inside."

"If you've a skill for disguise," the captain said, studying Paul with new interest, "that would serve you well. Would you care to begin work on the masks?"

"I can try," Paul said. "I've made masks before — paper-mâché, mostly. I won't need to cast a face directly; bring me a body to study, and I can match the shape of it well enough — the set of the jaw, the coloring, whatever's left of the hair. Helmets will carry the rest, in low light."

"Once we've bodies with their armor and faces intact to work from," Jude said, "we could see some of Gouge's people disguised as well — or at least the two of us, in cloth if not full plate."

"Their armor likely won't sit right on me," Paul admitted, "but the clothing, I can manage."

"By the time Gouge reaches us," Paul went on, turning back to the captain, "I'd like a rough plan already standing for the Western Tower, under your command, so he's only left to weigh it and add what he will. If he means to move tonight — and I expect he does — we'll be ready to begin the moment he arrives."

"Is there anything else that might aid us in the stealth of it?" Paul asked.

"Only that more men make for less quiet," the captain said. "I'd keep the party small, and under your authority rather than mine, so that I can remain here and hold the barracks properly. I couldn't guess how many Gouge will bring — knowing the man's reputation, not many. He trusts few enough as it is."

"I'd have you ready your own troops and a plan for the Western Tower," the captain said, "and when your agent arrives from Monmurg, put it before him — see what he'd change, and settle on a proper diversion besides. I want as few lives spent as possible, especially with the sahaugin still a threat to holding this island at all. Don't throw men away at that front gate unless I'm the one lighting the fire there myself."

He studied Paul a moment longer. "You mean to go with the party that goes over the wall?"

"I do," Paul said.

"You're certain of this?"

"I am. We'll begin readying for the beacon."

"Before your agents arrive, or after?"

"After," Paul said. "Though we can only wait so long, once we're set. There are still some thirty hobgoblins loose somewhere in the interior — they should give us little trouble if they stay scattered. It's open ground and light jungle between here and the Helm; if thirty men were massing anywhere close, we'd see it, even after dark, if we're watching properly."

"Very well," the captain said. "Be ready to move on the Western Tower by eleven, if this holds to two hours as I expect. I'll see the bodies brought in, the men prepared, and two squads readied for the beacon itself."

"Good," Paul said. "My thanks."

The captain inclined his head once more and withdrew, leaving Paul and Jude alone once again with the shape of a plan now settling, however roughly, into place.

# Chapter Sixteen — Two Kinds of Skill

They sat together afterward in the quiet the captain left behind, the lamp burning low, the night outside gone fully dark now save for the distant, watchful glow of the Helm's stolen fires.

"You've no objection to my telling him you could carry a man up on the wind?" Paul asked, something almost teasing beneath the question.

"None," Jude said. "Though it's a little funny, hearing it said plain like that. You looked at me, and I could all but see you deciding to just say it outright — and I went along, because what else was there to do?"

"I don't usually lead with what I can do," Jude admitted. "But we'd already gotten to talking, and he seemed to think it impossible I could be of use — so I let it slip that I was a caster, and that settled him quick enough."

"You didn't see what it looked like from the dock," Paul said. "The wall of fire. That's not a small thing to witness."

"He wasn't on the dock to see it," Jude said. "He was still aboard the corsair."

"Right. Well — regardless. Are you ready for tonight?"

"I am," Paul said. "I'll get started on the masks."

"You're still set on making them yourself, then?"

"If I can manage even one that passes, that's worth every hour it costs," Paul said. "Two or three, and I'll count it a triumph."

"You'll need a proper look at their faces to begin," Jude said.

"I saw enough of them at the dock to start from memory. I expect there'll be a body or two brought in I can work from directly, once the captain's men bring them up."

"Don't lose too much time to it," Jude said. "We still don't know how many of their suits will even be salvageable."

"I can't fight properly in one of those anyway — not and still move the way I need to," Paul said. "A cloak over the shoulders will do, if it comes to that. Paper over a hood reads well enough at a distance, in the dark."

"I won't need any of it," Jude said. "I'll be over the wall and in the air the whole approach — up and over, while the rest of you are down in the thick of it. I'll be watching from above."

"Right — I keep forgetting that part," Paul admitted, something wry in it.

"If I end up needing a hobgoblin's face myself, something's gone very wrong indeed," Jude said. "Picture it — one of them, floating, disguise half-slipping, drifting past the real ones on the wind. They'd take one look and wonder what in the world they were looking at."

Paul laughed despite himself. "Fair enough." He sobered, some weight settling back into his voice. "Listen — you should know you'll likely be taking orders once we're with Gouge. Not from me. From him."

"Is he like that?"

"It's a dangerous trade he's in," Paul said. "You're welcome to stay back with the captain instead, if you'd rather, and you'd do good service either way. But if you go in with Gouge, you follow where he leads."

"You know him, then."

"By reputation, mostly — and by what my uncle's people say of him," Paul said. "He's one of Jamis's, kept for the work that doesn't bear a name. That's enough to tell me how a man like that runs a thing."

"Ah."

"Is he — quick to act? A man who moves before he thinks?"

"He's built to kill," Paul said, without embellishment. "Slit a throat and have the knife back in your hand before you've noticed it's gone missing. That's the whole of him, in most matters."

"Good to know," Jude said.

"I only say it because he already knows who you are — he was asking after you before we'd even landed," Paul went on. "On a mission like this, if he tells you to do something, you can still hold to your own judgment — I'm not saying keep silent and simply obey — but understand that arguing with him isn't much in his nature."

"Understood."

"Any time I've come close to arguing with Gouge," Paul said, "I've gone in half-expecting I might have to kill him for it, or that he might try the same on me."

"So you have argued with him, then."

"No — we've never truly come to it. I only mean every time I thought it might."

"You just told me you've argued every time you thought there'd be an argument."

"No," Paul said, patient. "Every time I thought one was coming. Which, believe me, has been often enough."

"Sure, sure," Jude said, unconvinced but willing to let it lie.

"I only mean I know how to take what he offers without flinching from it," Paul said.

"Well," Jude said, "I throw walls of fire, and I've filled a tower with troops before lighting the whole thing up around them. And I'll be in the air the entire time, so — picture that alongside whatever Gouge brings to the table."

"So that's how you see this going."

"I've more besides that, if it comes to it."

"I don't doubt it," Paul said.

"Will you be able to answer to Gouge running things, truly?" Jude asked.

"So long as he's not drinking," Paul said. "I don't expect he will be — he never touched much before a job, not that I ever saw, and I don't think that's changed. He's never raised a hand to me either, for whatever that's worth."

"There's a kind of justice in that, somewhere," Jude said, something almost gentle beneath the needling.

Paul laughed. "Fine — laugh it up."

"I'm going to go meditate," Jude said, rising, "and see what I can recover before Gouge arrives and puts an end to that too. Do you have what you need, to keep at the masks?"

"I've got what I need," Paul said. "Go recover what you can — that matters more just now."

"Should I push for as long as I can manage, or will a shorter rest have to serve?"

Left to himself a moment, Jude weighed the question against the night still ahead. He had used what he had freely already — a wall of fire on the docks, a fireball among the sahaugin — and a longer meditation would restore more of it, if the hours allowed. "I took a short rest earlier, so I've a portion back already. I'll see how far the night lets me go before I'm called on again."

"I'll keep at the disguises while you do," Paul said. "Worst case, you only get partway through, and we make do with what's there."

"I'll go, then," Jude said. "The captain already has the shape of the plan — brief Gouge on it yourself when he arrives, so I'm not disturbed mid-meditation. Losing the thread now would cost me everything I've already recovered."

"Understood. Here it is, in short, for when he lands: we mean to take the Helm from the seaward flank, climbing the wall where the captain's men make their approach, while the garrison stages a show of force at the front gate to hold every eye there instead."

"That's the plan you and the captain settled on?"

"It is."

"One more thing," Jude added, already turning to go. "Remind him of the water caves beneath the Helm, even though none of us know precisely where the entrance lies. It may matter before the night's through."

"I will," Paul said. "Go rest. I'll be here."

"I'll be back once I've what I can recover," Jude said, and left him to the lamplight and the work of paper and paste and borrowed faces, while outside the barracks walls, the island waited for whatever Gouge would bring with him out of the dark.

---

## PART FIVE: GOUGE'S CREW

# Chapter Seventeen — Through the Woods

At the Southern Guard Tower, the hour of waiting had worn thin. Merrick had spent it turning the mirror-signals over in his mind, and Gouge had spent it, as he spent most things, in restless silence, until word came at last that Jude and Paul Rivera held the barracks — a hundred fifty men there, and urgency besides, since the island lay ringed by sahaugin and no relief could reach it until they were driven clear.

"He's pretty sharp, that one," Gouge said, when the shape of Jude's plan was laid out for him — a small party going ahead on foot, three swordsmen and two bowmen in tow, while the tower's own captain held the rest back to guard against anything still lurking behind them, and to warn off any ship that came sailing blind into the strait.

"That's been Jude signaling, this whole while?" Merrick asked, and was told yes — back and forth, mirror to mirror, the whole strange conversation carried on light alone.

By ten they had said what needed saying and turned to the question of when to slip away. Sooner, Merrick judged, was always better than later, and the plan settled quickly enough: the tower's archers would loose a volley toward the barracks grounds to draw any watching eyes, while the party slipped out the back under cover of the noise.

"We go out while they're looking elsewhere," Merrick said, testing the shape of it aloud, and gathered the five who would go with them at the base of the tower — two crossbowmen, three men with swords, all of them waiting on his word.

"I'll lead," he said. "I know this ground better than anyone left standing."

"I'll be close behind you," Gouge said. "Not far enough to lose sight of you."

"Stay behind him," Merrick told the rest, "and I'll stay quiet as long as I've got him in view."

They went out the back of the tower and struck for the bridge, Merrick moving first and alone, reading the coastline's broken shapes the way another man might read a well-worn page — thirty feet, forty, the moonlight finding him for an instant and losing him again just as fast. He crossed a hundred fifty feet before he judged it safe to look back, found the ground beyond the bridge clear of any watching shape, and signaled the rest forward.

Gouge came next, and came beautifully — a shadow among shadows, not so much as a whisper of cloth against stone — with Tyrus close on his heels, keeping himself deliberately between the ranger and the men still coming up behind. The crossbowmen and swordsmen fared worse. Scale mail clinked despite every care, a boot caught on loose stone in the dark, and somewhere behind them a sound carried far enough to matter.

The first bolt hissed past a heartbeat later, then two more — all wide, but close enough that no one could pretend the noise had gone unheard. Fire answered from the tower above, the archers there doing what they could to cover the retreat.

"Fall back," Tyrus called to his own men, already turning.

"I can drop a fog bank over them," Merrick offered. "Costs me a slot, but it'll hide the retreat."

"Do it," Tyrus said, and Merrick let the spell go, a grey curtain rolling low across the ground exactly where the trailing men needed it most. The five fell back toward the tower under its cover, while Merrick, Gouge, and Tyrus pressed on alone, three now where six had started, and reached the bridge without further incident.

At the rail, Merrick leaned out and looked down into the black water below — sharks cutting pale wakes beneath the surface, and further off, unmistakable even at this distance, a sahaugin riding one of them like a man might ride a hound.

"We don't cross bunched together, and not near the edges," he said. "Down the center, in pairs, and we regroup on the far side."

"Gouge and I first," Tyrus said. "You two follow."

They went in twos, weapons drawn, exposed the whole four hundred feet of open span but shielded at least from anything watching along the water's edge. No one so much as glanced their way. The four of them gathered again on the far bank, checked once more for any fresh signal from the tower, and found none.

"We stay off the coastline from here," Merrick decided. "Deeper into the wood, even if it costs us time — better that than being seen from the water."

He led the way into the trees, hugging the edge of the palm growth where the shadow ran deepest, the party trailing in careful order behind him. The distance to the barracks was less than a mile as the gull flew, but stealth stretched it to the better part of an hour, the four of them threading through sparse woodland and scattered palms until, close to eleven, the barracks' palisade rose ahead of them — watchtowers dark against the sky, Marines pacing the wall in plain sight.

"Do they see us?" Merrick murmured.

"Not yet," Gouge said. "We're still under cover."

"We need to be recognized, not mistaken for something worse," Merrick said. He turned to Gouge. "Try the wall — see if you can find the door."

"Solid, or climbable?" Gouge asked, studying the palisade's dark bulk.

"Climbable, if it comes to it," Merrick judged, "but risky — a hundred fifty men posted along that wall, watchtowers spaced the whole length of it. I don't think we need to sneak in at all, honestly. They know we're coming — the signals have made that plain enough. This is obviously us, not hobgoblins."

He weighed the distance to the wall — seventy feet, with a bare thirty-foot clearing between the treeline and the palisade itself — and settled on a quieter approach than climbing. A whispered word carried on magic reached one of the distant guards directly, a voice arriving in the man's mind rather than his ears: *We're here — Gouge and his party, at the edge of the woods.*

The answer came back the same way, calm and already expecting them. *We've been watching for you. Follow the west wall to the front gate, and you'll be let through.*

They moved along the base of the palisade with weapons still drawn, and by the time they reached the gate it was already lifting, the four of them slipping beneath it into the compound proper — where a captain in a breastplate stood waiting to greet them.

"Merrick! Good to see you standing," the captain said, then turned to the others in easy recognition. "Tyrus — you know this man, one of Fairwind's own. Good you've made it up. And you as well, Gouge."

"How does it stand to the south?" Gouge asked.

"We hold a small garrison there — twenty men or so at the lighthouse, though the tower itself sits empty. Our ship secured that ground cleanly enough." The captain's face darkened. "Here, it's another matter. We cleared the hobgoblins from the barracks proper, but sahaugin have taken parts of the structure since."

"Both, then," Gouge said. "Hobgoblins and sahaugin."

"Both, in different corners," the captain confirmed. "The hobgoblins cost us men before we drove them out. The sahaugin we've not yet dislodged — ten or fifteen, by our best guess, holed up in what's left of the barracks building itself. We hold the tower, warn what ships we can, and wait." He paused, weighing how much more to say. "We watched your corsair go down in the bay. Overrun. We believe there were no survivors among the two hundred who sailed with her — though the detachment escorting Paul Rivera made it ashore, so far as we can tell."

Gouge's eyes moved over the compound, searching. "Where's the elf?"

"Not here," the captain admitted, then studied Gouge more closely, something like recognition settling over his face. "You'll be Gouge, then, by the look of you. And Folsom — welcome. Paul Rivera and his companion wish to speak with you. Come."

He led them down into the heart of the barracks, toward what had clearly been made into a command office, and there, waiting for them, sat young Paul Rivera — no sign of Jude beside him. In person he cut a strange figure: a silver mask fixed over his face, tight leather clothing that clung close despite the warm night, gloves worn even indoors. The four of them filed into the room and paused at the threshold, uncertain, for a moment, quite what they were looking at.

# Chapter Eighteen — The Prince in the Mask

"My Prince," the sergeant said, ushering them in, "four men reporting to you."

Paul rose slightly from his seat, studying each of them in turn through the mask's fixed, unreadable eyes. "Hello. Which one of you is the Gouge I've heard so much about?"

Gouge stepped forward. "That would be me."

"I've heard a great deal about you," Paul said, "though rather less about the rest of your company. I'm glad regardless that you're all safe. How was your crossing — where did you come from, before tonight?"

"The south beacon."

"Things aren't well here," Paul said, something dry in the understatement.

"No," Gouge agreed. "We gathered as much, coming in."

"We've got what control we can manage, at least," Paul said. "The sergeant here has command well in hand." He gestured for introductions.

"Tyrus," Gouge said, and Paul inclined his head.

"Good to meet you."

"We came in unseen," Tyrus offered. "Through the wilderness rather than the coast — saw sharks in the water, and sahaugin riding among them, which was reason enough to keep well clear of the shore."

"Merrick," the ranger said, stepping forward in turn. "Sent by Lord Jamis."

"Paul Rivera," Paul said. "Nephew to Prince Jeon, whether or not the name means much to you."

"Prince Jeon's nephew — I have that right?"

"Prince Jeon, yes." Paul studied them a moment longer. "I take it you already know the shape of things here?"

"No," Merrick admitted. "We don't, in truth."

"Hobgoblins have us surrounded," Paul said. "That's the whole of it, more or less. We reckon two hundred fifty to three hundred of them on the island, all told. We'd already begun working out a plan with the captain here — Sten, that is, the barracks commander — before you arrived. We came ashore where you did, near enough, took this tower, though the lighthouse and the barracks proper both remain contested. Our own escort's down to twenty-five men of the thirty who landed with us. For now, they hold this position and little else."

"And the Helm itself?" Paul asked. "Do you have word of that?"

"I might," Gouge said.

"How many Marines have we, all told, here at the barracks?"

"Near a hundred seventy, I'd reckon," Gouge said.

"Then between myself, Jude, and the captain, we've been weighing our options," Paul said. "There are a few paths open to us."

Gouge's head turned sharply, some instinct catching a sound at the edge of the room. "What are you doing?"

A beat of silence answered him — someone, plainly, moving where they oughtn't. "Are you sneaking up on us?" he demanded, and got a wordless confirmation for his trouble before the matter settled itself and talk resumed.

"We've three options as I see them," Paul went on. "Sit tight and wait a day or two for proper reinforcement — though I'm wary of any merchant ship or ordinary traffic wandering into these waters in the meantime, blind to what's happened here. Or: stage an assault with a hundred men or so as pure distraction, while Jude carries a small party over the back wall, one at a time, by levitation. Risky, if we're seen — though I've put together rough disguises, enough that we might buy a second look before anyone reacts outright. Or, riskiest of all but perhaps most valuable — there's a cavern said to run beneath the Helm from the seaward side, with a hidden entrance somewhere along that coast. We don't know where it leads, or even where the mouth of it truly lies. Only that it's rumored to exist."

"Does anyone actually know where it is?" Gouge asked.

"No one's confirmed it," Paul admitted. "It's rumor, nothing more — though it has to sit somewhere along the coast facing the sahaugin-infested waters, which is its own trouble."

"Is there any way to call for help, if we take that route and need it?"

"None that I can think of," Paul said. "The disguises come with us regardless of which path we choose. As for signal — nothing, once we're inside. I'd still want the garrison staging a diversion to buy us the time to get in, but past that, we're a small group on our own. If we're caught exposed, there's no help coming."

"I believe we should move tonight," Paul said.

"It's already dark," Gouge observed.

"Then within the hour, once everyone's had their say."

"I'm in," Gouge said. "Myself, I'd favor the wall — stealth, and force the issue there directly. It comes down to two real choices in the end: hunt the coastline for that cave mouth, or find our own opening and make one."

"Is there any way out of this compound besides the front, or straight over the wall?" Paul asked.

The captain, still standing near the door, answered for them. "There's a south door, if it comes to that — though the only proper entrance to the Helm itself is through the gatehouse, the very building you're standing in now."

"Could we slip out that way instead," Gouge asked, "and move stealthily from there?"

"You could," the captain confirmed.

"Then here's the shape of it," Gouge said. "We send the diversion out the front, slip out ourselves, and from there it's the wall or the cave. I lean toward the wall, myself — it takes the sahaugin clean out of the equation, and I'm not inclined to trust that we won't run straight into them in those tunnels regardless."

"They'll have lookouts posted along the wall too, likely in some numbers," Paul warned.

"If we can reach the base of it unseen, that's the first real test," Gouge said. "From there, Merrick and I can go hunting the hidden entrance instead, if climbing proves too costly."

"I should be able to tell if there are sahaugin near," Merrick offered. "I know this coastline as well as anyone living. I led us in earlier, scouting the whole way — if I spot trouble, I can drop fog to cover us and keep moving unseen."

"How many times could you manage that?" Paul asked.

"As many times as I've spells left for it."

"Does it want components?"

"It does," Merrick said. "I'll need to be sure I'm carrying what it calls for."

"Who exactly is this elf everyone keeps mentioning?" Paul asked.

"Jude," Gouge said. "Resting, I'd expect, just now."

"At a time like this?"

"Trancing, most likely," Gouge said. "Elf custom — it's how they recover what magic they've spent."

"I'd like to speak with him before we settle anything," Paul said. "For what it's worth, I lean toward the cavern myself — but I want him in the room first."

"He's in that trance right now," Gouge repeated. "Something like meditation, for elves — recovering his spell slots."

"We'll likely need him more than any of us, before this is through," Paul admitted. "Here's the trouble — it's eleven now, and he'll want three or four more hours before he's fully recovered. Are you willing to wait until three?"

Gouge did the arithmetic aloud. "Three in the morning leaves us barely two hours of real dark after that, and a full hour just to reach the wall from here."

"I don't want to wait that long," Paul said, plainly.

"Neither do I," Gouge agreed. "Though — do hobgoblins see well in the dark?"

The question sent him briefly into his own memory of old lore, and the answer came back grim: they did, near as well as any elf. "Merrick's fog might offset that some," Gouge said, thinking it through. "I'd rather have Merrick scout well ahead of the rest of us regardless — I don't fancy dragging a heavy party through this the whole way blind."

"Only Gouge can see properly in the dark among us, I take it," Paul said. "What's his range — sixty feet? A hundred and twenty?"

"Sixty," Gouge said.

"Same as elven sight, or do they see further?"

The answer came back the same, roughly — sixty feet either way. "Not especially far," Paul mused. "If we keep well up from the shoreline, the sahaugin can't follow far inland regardless — they can bear it thirty minutes or so if truly pressed, but they hate every moment of it."

"They hate it enough that they're unlikely to stand guard far from the water," Gouge said. "Hobgoblins might hold that ground instead, though."

"Possibly," Paul allowed. "The Helm itself likely holds two hundred to two hundred fifty of them, from everything we've gathered — and probably comparable dark sight. But if the garrison stages a real diversion at the front, that ought to buy us the room to slip around."

"Once we're inside," Gouge said, "what's the actual goal?"

"Find Fairwind," Paul said. "Signal back if we're able, though I hold little hope of that part succeeding."

"And once we've found him?"

"Depends entirely on the state we find him in," Paul said. "If he's dead, we get clear if we can, or else try for the Helm outright. If he's alive—"

"Remind me who we're even looking for," Gouge said, half a beat behind.

"Lord Fairwind."

"He's the one who can restore the warding over this island," the captain put in quietly.

"Just so," Paul said. "So — dead, captured and unable to act, or worst of all, no longer truly on our side at all. But there's reason enough to think he might be shut away in some kind of protected chamber."

"Does anyone know this place well enough to guess where that might be?" Gouge asked.

Merrick considered it, drawing on everything he remembered of the keep's interior. "You mean the inner sanctum — the safe room, more or less?"

"That's it," Paul said.

"Casters can bend a man's mind," Merrick said slowly. "Even with a warding tied to him specifically, there's a real chance he's under some manner of compulsion, and not acting freely at all."

"Do we have any map of the place? A floor plan?"

"Nothing," the captain admitted.

"Then we go carefully," Paul said. "Whatever's happened here, it's unlike anything I've heard documented before — magically speaking."

"Does Sten know the layout? Does Merrick?"

The captain answered for himself. "I know the interior well enough. Merrick's walked these halls many times too, though never through whatever tunnels might lie beneath — not the moment to go exploring for its own sake, tempting as that might be."

"This was near enough my own father's post, in a manner of speaking," Merrick said, something quieter beneath the words. "Does anyone have a sense of where Fairwind's safe room would actually sit, in terms of reaching it directly?"

"I believe he'll be in the sanctuary," the captain said. "The most heavily defended part of the whole keep — only the Admiralty's normally permitted there, and it sits behind every other defense the Helm has to offer. The barracks proper is cut off from the rest of the fortress by the central tower."

"Do we hold keys to any of these doors?" Paul asked.

"None here," the captain said. "I'm no keeper of the Helm itself."

"Not to boast," Gouge said, something wry in it, "but I can open a few doors myself, given the chance."

"The best route we've discussed," the captain said, "is the lower wall near the garrison house — there's an entrance to the tower there. Force that first door, and you'll have access to the rest of the keep, and from there a path to the inner sanctum, where you can see for yourselves who's actually guarding it. If Fairwind's still himself, and in command of his own mind, he should know you and let you through. That's the most direct road to him I can offer."

"I'd favor the coastal approach," Paul said. "Not hugging the shore itself, but generally that direction — searching for the tunnel mouth as we go."

"Levitating over the wall serves well enough," Gouge said, "and hobgoblins certainly can't follow us into the air, which counts for something either way. Still, I favor the coastal route myself — the most direct path, to my mind. I've no real notion how those tunnels connect to the interior, and I'd rather not discover they dead-end into a room full of hungry sahaugin."

"That's a fair worry," Paul allowed.

"I'll put it to a vote, then," Paul said. "Does the elf have a voice in this?"

"Of course he does," Gouge said.

"Do we wake him, and bring him into it?"

"We're not waiting until three regardless," Gouge said.

"Agreed," Paul said. "I won't decide this without him in the room."

And so, with the shape of the choice still unsettled, the six of them went to find Jude.

# Chapter Nineteen — No Time to Finish

They found him exactly where the captain had guessed — deep in trance, folded into stillness in a quiet corner of the barracks, chasing the last of his spent strength back into himself hour by patient hour.

"Whatever it is," Jude murmured, still half lost in the working, "it can wait. I'm trying to concentrate."

It could not wait, as it turned out. Three in the morning would have seen him fully restored, but that was hours away yet, and Gouge was already here.

"Not quite an embrace, Jude," Gouge said, reaching out a hand rather than closing the distance further, "but you're worth more to me than a mere handshake, at least."

Jude blinked out of the trance all at once, disoriented. "Wait — what? What hour is it?"

"Half past eleven. We need to move."

"You couldn't have given me until one?" Jude said, something plaintive beneath the irritation. "I'd only a couple hours left in me."

"What became of the towers — West, South?" Gouge asked.

"Not the ones you came from," Paul said. "We spoke with the captain here — he means to move on the Western Tower, though the order hasn't gone out yet."

"The plan we settled with him," Paul went on, "runs one of two ways — along the coast and through a cavern into the Helm itself, or over the wall by levitation, straight into the sanctuary."

"Either way, we'd want to approach from the ocean side," Jude said, still gathering himself. "I can carry one of us up at a time — or we'll see how it falls."

"We're taking a convoy down to the cove," Gouge said. "From there, the cavern, or the wall — one or the other."

He turned the question over aloud. "Could you send the little clay scout ahead to search for the cave mouth? Save us an hour of hunting for it on foot."

"It's made of clay," Paul said. "If it's damaged out there, I lose it for good. It can't fly, only glide, though it climbs well enough. It could search the coastline, at least, if that helps."

"Then let it search the coastline," Gouge said. "My ranger and I can scout ahead in the meantime, clear the way as best we can."

"Going straight through the tower's front door and into the keep," Paul said, "that's near enough suicide."

"That's not the plan at all," Gouge said. "The diversion pulls every eye to the front gate. We take the wall from the coastline instead — the same stretch where we'd be hunting the cave regardless."

"While we're over there," Paul said, "could the little scout fly up and check something for us? The captain described a route along the wall that leads straight to the inner sanctum — if we mean to reach Fairwind, that's the way. Up the wall at that point, along to the tower, then it cuts off to the right."

Gouge studied Jude a moment. "I'm not surprised I caught you mid-trance — I'd guessed four hours was pushing it, and I'd hoped to arrive a touch later than I did. Any trouble reaching us?"

"None," Jude said. "The way was clear."

"Here's where things stand on my end, then," Gouge said. "Twenty or thirty hobgoblins fled the tower we took rather than fight to the last — they're still loose somewhere in the keep, holding the garrison house and the lighthouse. My man there keeps a signal running so our own people know to steer wide of it, and we're watching for any attempt to circle back on us from behind."

"So if we move ahead of the captain's own timeline," Paul said, working it through, "get into position first, then have him send the attack against the far beacon and demonstrate at the front gate—"

"The demonstration's only meant to pull the guards off the side walls," Gouge said, "so we've room to climb."

"Just so. His plan is to take the tower first, then send fifty men once we're set, and make a show of it in front — fires, a battering ram — without spending troops carelessly. That ought to fix the garrison's eyes on the gate and clear passage for us elsewhere."

"Good," Gouge said, then lowered his voice, glancing toward the household captain still nearby. "The rest of this stays between us. I don't yet know if Fairwind's clean or turned, and I'd rather not say it in front of the man."

"You think he's compromised?" Paul asked.

"I suspect it," Gouge said. "He may have broken the warding himself."

"Because of the sahaugin?" Jude asked. "Or something else entirely?"

"No proof of it either way — only suspicion, and instructions of my own on the matter."

"You never mentioned you had suspicions," Paul said.

"I've had them," Gouge said. "We all should, given his rank, and how convenient the timing of all this proves to be. We simply hadn't the chance to speak of it plainly until now."

"Were you sent to end him?" Jude asked.

"I was sent to bring him in," Gouge said, "not to kill him — that's the whole difference. Had it been the other way, I could've managed it easily enough while he slept. Just now, I only mean to learn what actually happened here."

"So — can he be taken alive?" Paul asked.

"We'll have to manage it," Gouge said. "If he's turned, that's a disaster whichever way it falls, and we need him breathing regardless — someone has to know how to mend what's broken, and set the warding right again. First step is clearing the sahaugin from this island entirely."

"Why would they even come here?" Jude asked. "That protection's held for centuries."

"That's the question that matters most," Gouge said. "He should know the answer, if anyone does — the working theory is some arrangement he struck with them once, that's since come apart. You wouldn't happen to know anything of merfolk working these waters?"

"No," Paul said, "though the ranger with you likely would — he knows this island better than I ever will. I don't swim well enough to have gone looking myself, and I've never so much as seen where they might live."

"Fair enough," Gouge said. "Let's move, and learn what we can as we go." He turned to Jude. "Give me a quarter hour to gather my gear — I'll meet you at the front gate."

"We'll stay close to the coastline," Paul added, "whatever cover it offers. If it comes to trouble, the ranger can throw up fog to buy us time."

"We haven't even seen these disguises of yours yet," Gouge said.

"They're rough," Paul admitted. "Hopefully you won't need them where we're going — but they're ready, if you do."

There was a brief, tired pause after that — a moment spent comparing how much of Jude's strength had actually returned before Gouge's arrival had cut the trance short, less than a full night would have given him, but something. A weary joke passed between them about an old mishap on some earlier job, half-remembered and long since healed, and then Gouge rose to see to his own people.

"I'll gather my crew and fill them in," he said. "Give me five minutes. Ten, at the outside."

And with that, he went to round up the rest of his company.

# Chapter Twenty — The Little Scout

The two companies met properly a short while later, close to midnight, gathering in a quiet corner of the barracks compound to settle the last of their plan before moving.

"I can send my little scout out ahead of us," Paul said, drawing the small clay figure from within his coat, "drop it somewhere with a clear view, then fall back and watch through its eyes from a safe distance. That's the notion."

"What is that thing, exactly?" Merrick asked, leaning in.

"This." Paul held it up — a small clay lizard, no bigger than a housecat, its wings folded neat against its sides. "I can send it up a wall, and it'll glide from one surface to the next. I see and hear everything it does, the whole time."

"That's remarkable," Merrick said, something like wonder breaking through his exhaustion.

"Is that — witchcraft?" Folsom asked, eyeing the little creature with open suspicion.

"It's a homunculus," Paul said, with the patience of a man who had corrected this same mistake more than once already. "Not whatever you were about to call it. A homunculus."

"We ought to write songs about it," Gouge said.

"Please don't," Paul said.

"The whole point," Paul went on, "is that I can see and hear through it while we're elsewhere — drop it somewhere useful, fall back, and keep watching."

"If we're sneaking in regardless," Jude said, "why not simply hunker down in the rocks and watch from there directly, rather than falling all the way back to the barracks?"

"I don't fancy making that walk twice, once we've started," Paul said.

"Fair — we can hunker in the rocks along the coast, then, and send the little fellow out from there. No need to double back."

"Can it be hurt?" Tyrus asked, studying the figure. "It's clay — is it even alive, properly?"

"It can be damaged. Destroyed, even," Paul said. "But it's not alive, no, and I take no harm when it goes."

"Could you throw it and have it stick where it lands?"

"No," Paul said, "though if it's lost, I can make another eventually. Not quickly, and not easily, but eventually."

Introductions went round while the plan settled — Gouge presenting Folsom to Merrick, a striking figure with more flair to him than any standard-issue sailor, carrying a longbow that sat oddly with the Marine uniform he wore over gear that plainly wasn't regulation issue.

"I am a Marine, for the record," Folsom said, before anyone could ask. "I was there, at the landing. Though most of us carry more than one trade besides soldiering — my father served in the navy, and I've done the same work for Lord Jamis myself, more advisor than proper Marine, on these waters. First impressions tend to be misleading. It wears off."

"And you?" Merrick asked, turning to Jude. "You don't dress the way I'd have pictured a wizard."

"I don't dress like a wizard, no," Jude said. "Standard adventuring gear, dark colors. No pointed hat, no flowing robe. Pouches, a dagger at my belt, hood and cloak. Understated. And armed, plainly enough."

"How do we ever hide you, though?" Folsom asked, tipping his head up at Tyrus. "Six foot four if you're an inch."

"Built like a cave troll, my uncle used to say." Tyrus took no offense at it. "I'll manage."

Gouge laid the final shape of the plan before them all. "We send men out as a distraction here. The six of us slip out along the coastline to this corner, and from there we decide — climb the wall, or hunt the cave mouth. Find the tunnels quick enough, that's our road. Too many hobgoblins in the way, or nothing to find after a real search, and we go up and over instead."

"Do we dress as hobgoblins for this," Tyrus asked, "or not? I'm not sure how well I'd sell it, at six foot four."

"My worry," Gouge said, "is that without disguise, a patrol sighting us near the water leaves everything to luck. We can't afford to be pinned down while we're trying to slip up on the fortress. But if a hobgoblin patrol catches us at a distance, near the shore, there's a fair chance they read us as more of their own and don't look twice."

"And if it does come to trouble," Jude added, "I can throw fog to buy us time — to fall back, or to reach the wall and go up. I know this stretch of coast well enough besides — there are good places to hide, if it comes to that."

"Then the vote's made," Gouge said. "We move fast for the caves, disguises on regardless, just in case."

At Paul's request, the barracks commander had several of the hobgoblin dead brought in from where they'd fallen, along with rope and stripped helmets, and Paul set to work with a small cosmetics kit, painting borrowed faces onto paper and paste by lamplight.

"These are rough," Paul admitted, working quickly, "but in the dark, they should at least buy a second look instead of an arrow on sight. What's useful is the armor itself — their leather and scale is near enough Marine issue, just different in the coloring. We noticed the same on the ones we fought earlier. Wearing their gear ought to let us pass at a glance — might buy just enough time to get the jump on anyone who does stop us."

"When do we want the commander moving on the front gate?" Gouge asked.

"We need to be well clear before that starts," Paul said. "It's going to pull every eye to the front, which serves us well enough, but we can't be caught crossing open ground while it's still quiet. Twenty or thirty minutes ahead of them, I'd think."

"Agreed," Gouge said. "Once they move, they'll light the braziers, make a show of assembling the ram. Call the attack for half past midnight."

"How long to reach position from here?" Paul asked, and was told roughly ten minutes, moving carefully, across some seven hundred fifty feet of ground.

"We'll want to signal the South Beacon when this begins," Gouge added, "so they don't mistake it for an attack on themselves and send men we can't afford to lose."

"I can send that now," Jude said, and did — word going out across the dark water that whatever they saw near the Helm tonight, they were to hold their position and wait for a separate signal before acting.

"Then let's move," Gouge said. "The six of us, out through the north exit, up the coast from there."

# Chapter Twenty-One — Over the Wall

They slipped out through the north exit in the deep hours between the second and third of Wealsun, six shapes moving into darkness that gave back nothing in return.

Merrick went first, peeling off wide of the cliffs, well clear of anything the walls might see, moonlight finding him for one bare instant before the shadows swallowed him whole. The rest followed in a loose, careful line — Merrick, then Paul, then Gouge, Jude, Folsom, and Tyrus bringing up the rear, thirty feet of separation held between each of them, trusting that a thousand feet of distance from the barracks walls would be enough to keep them unseen, disguises or no.

They came at last to a spot high above the coast, looking down on a stretch of beach far below — steep rock the whole way, waves breaking hard against a cliff face that offered no easy path down.

"Any notion where these smuggler entrances might sit?" Jude asked.

"Only that they're somewhere on this side," Merrick said, "high above the rocks. That's the whole of what rumor gives us."

What caught every eye instead was a ship riding at anchor offshore, lit by lanterns, foreign in its lines — not a corsair, not quite a proper warship either, but something built for speed, three or four hundred feet out from the cliffs.

"What are you thinking?" Gouge murmured.

"Any flag on her? Markings?" Paul asked, and got none for an answer — no colors he could place, though something in the hull's shape resolved itself under closer study into one of the newer Onwal trading vessels, fast-rigged, built to outrun even a corsair, which was no small thing in itself.

"Ours?" Paul asked.

"Not a chance," came the answer. "Foreign-built, and none of us know her by sight."

"Could be simple trade, caught out by the blockade," Tyrus offered.

"Possible," Paul allowed, "but I don't think we can afford to call it coincidence, given everything else tonight."

As they watched, a smaller vessel emerged from a fold in the coastline that hadn't seemed to hold any opening at all, and struck out toward the anchored ship.

"Where did that come from?" Gouge asked.

The coast curved out of clear sight at exactly that point — which, given everything they'd heard, left little doubt it marked the smugglers' cave.

"Do you know that skiff?" Jude asked, and the shape of it, once studied, resolved into a Monmurg smuggler's craft — small, built for bay and river work rather than open water, common enough in these waters to anyone who knew where to look.

"Then the entrance is compromised," Paul said slowly, "or someone down there is in on this."

"Or Fairwind arranged the whole thing himself," Gouge said, "and got in well over his head for the trouble."

"My instinct says this runs political," Paul said. "Ties to everything we already suspected, before we ever set foot on this island."

"What would anyone even smuggle out of a besieged fortress?" Tyrus wondered.

"Could be moving something in, not out," Gouge said. "Reinforcement, perhaps."

The ship sat low and heavy in the water — loaded, not unloading.

"I'd rather not chase whatever's on that ship," Paul said, "and lose sight of the people actually running this."

"If they're moving something out under a siege," Tyrus said, "that's worth knowing regardless, whatever else we do about it."

As they weighed it, a figure resolved on the ship's deck, directing hobgoblins as they hauled cargo aboard — stark white hair, skin pale even at this distance.

"That's Toli," Gouge said, flat and certain.

"No question of it," Paul agreed. "The same look as the ones we fought on the docks, a month back."

Just as Lord Jamis had suspected all along — Toli agents, tangled somewhere in the middle of everything unfolding here.

"Will that ship come back this way?" Paul asked.

"Once they've what they came for, I'd think," Gouge said.

"I want a closer look," Paul said, "alone, in the rocks."

"We get as close as we safely can without being seen," Gouge decided for the rest. "Better vantage on the ship, and it puts us nearer the wall besides."

They moved up along the coastline into deeper cover, close enough now to watch the skiff reach the larger vessel and begin unloading — barrels, the same rough size seen in any Monmurg warehouse. And then, from up on the barracks walls behind them, shouting broke out, hobgoblins running for the front gate in numbers.

The diversion had begun.

The pale figure on the ship's deck glanced once toward the commotion and went back to his cargo, unconcerned.

"Then we move, while their attention's split," Gouge said.

The captain's account held true — an entrance on this stretch of wall, reachable by a fifty-foot climb, with a tower door waiting somewhere above it.

"I'll go first," Gouge said. "Best suited for the climb." He scrambled up the broken hillside toward the base of the wall while the rest held in cover, watching thirty or forty hobgoblins stream past toward the fighting at the gate, none of them sparing a glance for the shadows along the cliff.

"Crossbow's ready, if anyone up top notices him," Merrick murmured, and watched Gouge cross the exposed ground — forty seconds, perhaps, in plain view — and reach the base of the wall entirely unremarked.

"I'll hold here for the rest of you," Gouge called back, low.

Merrick went next, the same route, and reached him without incident. "I'll hold where this tower meets the curtain wall," Gouge decided. "Enough cover, if more men come running past."

Jude readied the rope and spoke the words of Levitate. "Paul — start climbing, I'll carry the rest of you up." He glanced at the strange sight of it even as he worked the spell. "It's a little unsettling, watching this happen, if I'm honest."

"I appreciate the honesty," Paul said, and began the climb, the spell easing him the last stretch of the way in slow, careful pulls, until he stood at last on the wall walk and tied off the rope for those still below.

"Still pass for a hobgoblin, on my knees like this?" he asked under his breath.

"Near enough," Jude judged. "You'll do, against the bigger ones."

Tyrus climbed the rope cleanly and crouched beside him. Folsom followed with rather less grace, admitting to no great love of heights, but made it up all the same. Merrick came after, settling in to watch the distant fighting still raging at the front wall, and Jude came last, dropping into the shadows unnoticed.

"I'll hold the rope down here," Gouge decided, remaining below, "and keep an eye on that ship."

Six of them now crouched along the wall near the tower in the dark, the battle at the gate still burning below. Ahead, a door stood open into the tower itself, a single hobgoblin guarding it, and beyond that, a hundred feet off, a larger cluster of thirty or forty more, all facing the fighting, none of them looking back.

"I'll take the door guard," Jude said quietly. "Quick and quiet — doesn't matter who backs the play."

"I can draw a few eyes instead," Folsom offered. "Stumble out like a straggler, make some noise, give you the moment you need."

He broke cover and jogged into view, playing the part with a theatrical trip and a stumbling recovery, drawing exactly the attention he meant to. In the same breath, Jude closed on the door guard in absolute silence, and the man went down without so much as a gasp.

"I'll hold Darkness in reserve," Jude murmured, "no sense blinding ourselves along with them."

A second guard came around the wall's curve then, drawn by some half-heard sound, the click of a crossbow cocking carrying faint on the night air.

"I've got this one," Gouge said, and waited, still as stone, until the man had nearly closed the distance before striking for the throat. It ended before it began — no cry, no struggle, the body folding backward and away from the others without so much as a thud reaching the ground below.

Gouge searched the corpse with the same unhurried efficiency he'd used to kill it — a purse of coin, a ring of keys, a hobgoblin badge, a crossbow, and, curious among the rest, a flat steel disc stamped with a skull and some grim motto about the fleeting nature of life.

"Cheerful sort," Gouge said, pocketing it.

Beyond the tower door lay a stairway down into the fortress proper, and a corridor leading toward what the captain had called the inner sanctum — the most likely place, if anywhere, to find Fairwind or whatever remained of his loyal men.

"What lies through there?" Paul asked, studying the passage.

A long, fortified hall answered him, columned, twenty-five feet across, arrow slits lining one wall and all of them facing out toward the battle at the gate. Some twenty hobgoblins stood posted along it, every eye fixed on the fighting below, none turned toward the shadows behind them.

"Twenty's too many to take quietly," Jude said.

"Then we march," Gouge decided. "Disguises on, heads down, like reinforcements running late."

"I'll take point," Tyrus said. "I'm built enough like their bigger sort to sell it."

"I'll flank you," Folsom offered.

"I'll stay small and unremarkable near the back," Jude said.

"I'll take the rear," Paul said.

They fell into rough marching order and started down the hall at a brisk, purposeful clip, boots striking stone in a rhythm meant to say *sent for, in a hurry, nothing to remark on.* Halfway down, one of the hobgoblins turned and studied them a beat too long.

Tyrus waved him off without breaking stride, pointing back toward the gate as though ordering the man back to his post — and his sheer size did the rest of the work, the hobgoblin muttering something low in his own tongue before turning back to the wall.

They walked the full hundred fifty feet of that corridor, past twenty armed and watchful strangers, and no alarm rose to meet them. At the far end, the passage opened toward the deeper fortifications of the Helm — further into the fortress than any of them had yet set foot, with whatever waited for them there still unseen ahead.

---

## PART SIX: INTO THE HELM

# Chapter Twenty-Two — The Council Room

The towers marking the inner sanctum rose ahead of them now, two guards posted before its final door — one of them in a breastplate like their own borrowed gear, marked with a red badge of office that set him apart from the rest.

Folsom studied the distance, judged it at thirty feet, and let a small working of charm slip loose toward the officer as they closed the gap, walking with the easy, unhurried confidence of a man who owned the ground beneath him.

The guard's eyes drifted over the approaching party, caught for a moment on Folsom, and something in his bearing softened — not quite trust, but the absence of alarm. He said something in Hobgoblin that needed no translation to understand as a challenge.

Folsom walked straight up to him without answering it directly, close enough to crowd the man's space, and let the weight of his size and bearing do more of the work than words. "Pardon my general," he said in Common, low and dismissive, already reaching for the ring of keys taken off the corpse on the wall above.

"Why do you speak like a human?" the guard muttered, baffled, more to himself than to Folsom. "We are not among them—" He caught himself, shrugged the confusion off as not worth pursuing, and asked instead, plainer: "Why are you here?"

The rest of the party filed past while the guard's attention stayed fixed on Folsom, walking with the loose, unbothered gait of men who belonged wherever they were standing.

"Important business," Merrick said, when the guard's eyes finally found him.

"Are you speaking on behalf of the Toli?" the guard asked.

"As it happens," Merrick said, without missing a beat.

The name settled the matter. "He outranks me," the guard muttered, and waved them through, handing over the ring of keys along with the door itself.

They passed inside and set the bar down behind them, sealing off whatever might come from the corridor they'd left. The chamber beyond ran some twenty-five feet before opening onto a second barred door, and beyond that, Merrick judged from what little he could see through the gap, lay the inner sanctum proper — a place none of them had ever set foot before.

"If Fairwind's men are holed up in here with only this much standing guard," Merrick murmured, low, "that's not going to hold against fifty men, if it comes to that."

"Does this look worse for him the deeper we go?" Folsom asked.

"That's exactly what I'm thinking," Merrick said. "And that guard said Toli, plain as day — and we saw the Toli ourselves, on that ship."

The second door, when they reached it, gave beneath the key with a low groan, opening just enough to make one thing plain beyond any doubt: whatever the inner sanctum had once been, it was compromised now. Voices carried through the gap — low, unhurried, deep in conversation.

"Don't move," Folsom breathed. "Someone needs to listen first."

"My little scout can do it," Paul said, drawing the small clay lizard from beneath his cloak and setting it low near the crack beneath the door, where it crept forward with visible reluctance before slipping through.

He sent it on along the shadowed edge of the wall, and fell into a listening trance, watching through its borrowed eyes as it crept toward the sound of voices — down a passage, a turn, another turn, following the sound to what could only be a council chamber, a great table before it carved with the shape of the islands themselves, the kind of room where admirals gathered to plot the movement of ships and men.

And there, seated at its head, was Lord Fairwind himself.

"Don't trouble yourselves," they heard him say, addressing two figures across the table. "They'll never breach the defenses here. Your hobgoblins can hold them indefinitely. If you believe this little assault of theirs will succeed — it won't."

"We're not worried," one of the two answered — pale, white-haired, unmistakably Toli even at a glance. "We've taken most of what we need from the caves below, in any case. It seems, Lord Fairwind, you kept rather more of the compound for yourself than our arrangement allowed. We'll have the rest before we're through, and carry it home to our own island."

"Our arrangement still stands, then," Fairwind said. "You'll vouch for me before the council — press Jeon into granting me a fairer share of what his trade brings in from the other nations."

"Of course," the Toli said. "You'll be well compensated. Once the pressure on Jeon has served its purpose, everything returns to how it was. We'll return every barrel of the — pest control, shall we call it — and you may go about your business undisturbed. Until then, it stays with us."

Paul came back to himself slowly, the trance releasing him by degrees, turning the words over even as they still echoed in his mind. The compound. The yellow substance he'd stumbled across weeks before, back in Monmurg — could it be one and the same? He had no proof, no barrel in hand to point to, only the shape of an old memory surfacing: letters between his mother and father, half-understood as a boy, describing something that killed the sahaugin outright, poisoned the very water against them, the same weapon used a hundred fifty years past in the old wars, bought at cost from the Duke of Berghof because no one in the sea principalities had ever learned to make it themselves. His father had tried, and failed.

The truth of it, he understood now, was a secret buried deep enough that most of Monmurg believed the island's protection came from some ancient enchantment rather than a sack of poison sprinkled into the tide. Whether to let that belief stand or shatter it was a question that settled over him with sudden, uncomfortable weight.

He decided quickly enough.
