Whatever the compound's true nature, that was a secret worth keeping close — his uncle would want it kept close, given how thin a thread it hung by, tied as it was to a single foreign supplier. But Fairwind's dealings with the Toli were another matter entirely, and those he meant to report in full. The man was sitting not thirty feet away, trading in his own city's defense like a merchant haggling over spoiled grain.

# Chapter Twenty-Three — Blood in the Sanctum

"We've found him," Paul said, coming fully back to himself, voice pitched low with something between triumph and dread. "Fairwind. He's in there, talking with the Toli right now."

"What do they look like?" Jude asked. "Pale — casters, or blades?"

"No armor," Paul said. "Swords, daggers. Could be either."

A hobgoblin stood posted across the passage ahead, a corridor branching down and to the left beyond him. Paul eased his homunculus in closer for a better vantage, the little creature creeping unnoticed along the wall's edge, and the guard, sensing something at the periphery of his attention, nearly turned to look before some deeper instinct pulled his focus elsewhere.

"I want Fairwind alive, if we can manage it," Jude murmured. "Whatever else happens."

Paul and Jude signaled the strike together — a coordinated shot, dagger and bolt aimed as one at the unsuspecting guard. Jude's struck true and deep; Paul's deflected harmlessly off the man's armor. It was enough regardless. The hobgoblin cried out once in his own tongue and went down bleeding, and from somewhere deeper in the chamber, shouting answered the sound.

"Push forward, or hold?" Tyrus asked, blade already coming up.

They took their positions fast — Gouge at the front, Tyrus and the others close behind, Paul and Jude covering from range, Merrick flanking wide.

"Who goes there? Identify yourselves!" a voice called from within, sharp with authority.

"Sorry, sir," Gouge called back, stalling. "Wasn't feeling well."

"We have Lord Fairwind here," the voice answered. "Attack carelessly, and you'll kill him yourselves. State your names."

"Don't kill our lord," Jude muttered, half to himself. "We're only buying time."

"Show yourselves first," the voice demanded.

"You first," Jude said.

A hobgoblin edged around the corner instead, hand-crossbow rising, and loosed a bolt at Jude, who twisted aside at the last instant and took only a graze for his trouble.

The fight that followed came fast and ugly. Gouge closed on the pale, unarmored figure who could only be Toli nobility — fine clothing, fair hair, a rapier drawn with practiced ease — trading blows that neither man landed cleanly at first. Paul slipped past behind Gouge's shoulder and buried his dagger where the Toli's guard couldn't reach, the strike landing with the particular precision of a man who knew exactly where to aim. Tyrus and his own companion pressed a second noble hard from both sides, driving him back step by step. A third came at Merrick with the confidence of easy prey, catching him twice before Merrick could bring his bow to bear.

Jude, watching the stairwell for any threat still to come, turned his attention forward instead and loosed a burst of magic at the one directing the fight — a voice raised in command that marked him, unmistakably, as more than muscle.

"He's a mage!" Jude called out, and the warning rippled through the others as they adjusted.

The battle ground on in exchanges — Paul finishing his opponent with a clean strike and slipping clear before anyone could answer it, Tyrus weathering two heavy blows without breaking, Folsom lending healing where it was needed most, Gouge grinding down his own target with dagger and scimitar together until the man staggered on the edge of collapse.

Jude, weighing the last two standing against each other, drew a fireball's shape tight enough to spare his own people and let it loose between them. One of the two anticipated it — some trick of resistance or ward that snuffed the flame before it could properly catch — but the blast still tore through the space around him, and Merrick's arrow, following close behind, dropped the wounded mage to his knees.

"Grapple him!" Merrick shouted. "Don't let him cast again!"

A second fireball, shaped with the same careful precision, finished what the first had started. The mage went down for good, and only one Toli remained — the man Tyrus and his companion had been grinding down from the start, who broke for the door rather than stand and fight.

"Not happening," Gouge said, and between the two of them they ran him down before he'd gone more than a few steps, pinning him fast.

"Take him alive," Tyrus called, and switched from blade to fist, landing two solid blows in place of a killing stroke until the man dropped, unconscious rather than dead.

They stripped him where he lay, tearing strips from his own fine silk shirt to bind his wrists, and Gouge shoved him against the wall the moment he began to stir.

"Grubby elf hands," the Toli said, sneering, the instant he found his voice again, "ruining a perfectly good shirt."

"Shut up, you filthy pig," Gouge said, unmoved, and the man only looked back at him, entirely unbothered by the insult.

It was Tyrus who found the side room a moment later — a table, and beside it, bound to a chair and gagged, Lord Fairwind himself.

"Good thing he's gagged," Tyrus observed dryly, and went to see the last of the fight properly finished.

# Chapter Twenty-Four — The Prisoner's Bargain

They dragged him out and set him before them, Paul keeping his bow trained loosely on the bound man as a precaution more than an active threat.

"Just a precaution, I take it?" Fairwind said, eyeing the arrow with something close to amusement. "Not that I intend to attack."

"What are we going to do with him?" Paul asked the room at large.

"I'm entirely focused on this son of a bitch right now," Merrick said, not troubling to soften it.

"There's another set of stairs, off this way," Gouge noted, already scouting the room's exits.

Merrick turned to the practical matter of securing the chamber — no one else stumbling in on them unannounced — while Fairwind studied Paul with an expression that shifted, in the space of a breath, from wariness to something almost like recognition.

"Paul Rivera," Fairwind said. "You were beneath my notice, young man, last I knew of you."

"Fairwind, you're under arrest by order of Prince Jeon," Paul said, flat and formal. "I'm here as his representative. You've committed treason."

"Merrick — I know you," Fairwind said, turning. "Are you truly going to allow this?"

"I am," Merrick said. "Your word carries no weight with us right now. These are Lord Jamis's orders, and Prince Jeon's, under suspicion of treason."

"Suspicion," Fairwind said, something almost wry in it. "You can see well enough that I'm already your prisoner. I could still order my own men to cooperate, if you'd only let us—"

"Where are your men?" Paul cut in. "Where are they being held?"

"Taken prisoner," Fairwind said. "They're in the lower—"

"You're full of it," Paul said.

"We'll find them ourselves," Merrick said. "We're wasting time arguing."

"Keep your mouth shut, Fairwind," Gouge added.

"They told me you'd gotten too big for your boots, boy," Fairwind said to Gouge, something colder creeping into his voice now. "You'll regret this."

"I'll regret it?" Gouge said. "No threats from you — you don't know the first thing about me. We've enough Toli trash to deal with already without adding yours to the pile."

Paul turned back to Fairwind, pressing while the man was still off balance. "Speak quickly. How do we drive the sahaugin from these waters?"

"That's a state secret," Fairwind said. "I cannot tell you."

"They've already destroyed a ship full of Marines sent to this island," Paul said. "I might yet defend your honor before the courts, if it comes to that — but only if we can drive them off and reclaim this place first."

"I don't know how to simply make them vanish," Fairwind admitted, something in his bearing softening at last. "But the old protection can be restored."

"How?"

"There's a chamber below," Fairwind said. "Stairs, over there — they lead down to where the barrels are kept."

"Barrels of what?"

"That's sensitive information."

"I don't care how sensitive it is," Paul said. "What's in them?"

"The powder," Fairwind said. "It's already been loaded onto the ship."

"Keep going," Paul said, exchanging a glance with the others.

"I'm glad you're beginning to grasp the stakes," Fairwind said. "Though I believe the Toli have already made off with most of what we needed."

"Are you keeping something from us?"

"I serve Lord Jamis and Prince Jeon, same as you—"

"Keep talking," Paul pressed. "Honesty now will help your case at trial, whatever else happens."

"The hobgoblins aren't even the true danger," Fairwind said. "The sahaugin can be driven off — will be, in fact — unless what's stored below finishes being packed and shipped clear of this island first."

"You're not grasping how urgent this is," Paul said.

"Got any more of those fireballs handy?" Jude murmured, half to himself, eyeing the ship still visible somewhere beyond the walls. "Wait — that would take the barrels with it."

"That's the wrong move entirely, thank you," Paul said.

Fairwind pressed on, something almost pleading beneath the careful formality now. "Understand — the barrels hold a powder, poisonous to the sahaugin, an alchemical reaction rather than any spell, sprinkled into the water around this island. That's how the sea principalities have held them off for a hundred fifty years. The world believes it's an enchantment — a deliberate lie, meant to keep the true secret hidden. The Toli have learned it regardless, and they're taking our supply as we speak."

"Do the Toli suffer from the sahaugin themselves?" Paul asked.

"I couldn't say what drives them in this."

"We've long suspected Toli involvement in the blockade against Monmurg," Paul said. "And now there's a ship out there hauling off everything that keeps this island standing." He turned to the others. "Fairwind's case is for the princes to settle, later, properly. What's ours to handle now is the sahaugin, and reclaiming this island."

"Where are the hobgoblins even coming from?" Merrick asked. "Who's directing them?"

"The Toli," Fairwind said.

"Then let's take the dead one's head," Gouge said grimly, "and use it to put the fear of god into the rest — burn as many as we can manage besides."

They searched the wounded and the fallen alike while the argument settled — a purse, a set of keys, a hobgoblin badge, and among Fairwind's own belongings nothing that spoke to guilt or innocence either way.

"All that matters now," Paul said, "is protecting Monmurg, and that means reclaiming the Helm."

"Agreed," Merrick said. "We should make for the signal tower — it can be seen from the city, and they'll send reinforcement the moment it's lit."

"And we'll warn them of the sahaugin," Paul said. He turned back to Fairwind. "Is there any way to restore the island's protection, so we're not bleeding troops in that bay for nothing?"

"One barrel," Fairwind said, "will hold them off ten months."

"Then we move quickly to secure what's left below," Paul decided, "and we bring both prisoners with us."

"Can you blow up that ship, before it's fully loaded?" Jude asked. "It's leaving with the barrels."

"That ship's carrying the very substance we need," Paul said. "It's reactive to fire — badly."

"I could destroy it easily enough," Jude admitted, "but I'd take the barrels with it."

"Then before it comes to that," Paul said, "we find out what's still down there to recover."

"Forgive me," Fairwind said, watching them with something almost resigned. "I understand you must keep me restrained. I'll argue my case before the other princes, when the time comes."

"If we can secure even some of what remains," Paul said, "we retake this island without needing to burn the ship at all."

"Someone teach me the ballista," Jude said. "Could prove useful."

"Let's move," Paul said. "We're wasting time we don't have."

Gouge drew Paul aside briefly before they descended, his voice pitched for the two of them alone, though the rest could still hear well enough. "If we rush this, remember he's got a hundred fifty men of his own somewhere, imprisoned or otherwise. He might try calling out to them. We need him off this rock before his own troops learn he's alive."

"Agreed," Paul said.

"I'm thinking — the rest of you take the ship back to Monmurg if we can seize it, while I take Fairwind myself and try to rally his garrison directly, coordinate retaking the Helm from outside."

"You'd take both prisoners back yourself?"

"I'd sooner hand the interrogation to Jamis directly," Gouge said. "Unless you were told otherwise."

"That's what I was thinking as well," Paul admitted.

"We hood and gag him going down," Gouge said, "except he's the one who has to guide us to the lower level."

"We know nothing of what's down there," Paul said.

"I'd wager it's garrisoned the same as everywhere else," Gouge said, "and the Toli are striking some bargain with him he's at least partly agreed to. Whatever you overheard, Paul, we'll trust your account of it — you take the lead on him. I only want him off this island quietly, if the boat allows it. If he calls out and his men learn he's alive, everything gets harder."

"Definitely," Paul said.

"So we balance it," Gouge said. "He has to guide us, and if the charm's still holding, maybe he stays quiet on his own account rather than needing to be forced."

Gouge questioned him plainly once they'd rejoined the others. "Between us — how did the hobgoblins get here? How many are we facing?"

"They came aboard a ship the Toli brought," Fairwind said. "The Sea Ghost. Cain Toli's own."

"Anything more on the threat itself?"

"You'll face as much threat as you choose to," Fairwind said. "Or none at all, if you'd simply free me now."

"I respect my uncle a great deal," Paul said. "Though he considers himself first among equals, he's not sole ruler of this land."

"That hardly matters," Fairwind said.

"Why not? You're accused of treason, and I intend to see you before the courts of the principalities."

"I've never even heard of such courts."

"They're very real."

"What treason, precisely, do you believe I've committed? Am I not a lord in my own right, same standing as any here?"

"Not any longer," Paul said. "You're a prisoner accused of treason."

"Treason against whom?"

"Against us. Against Monmurg."

"What allegiance do I actually owe you, Paul Rivera?"

"You're fortunate I'm not pressing this further than I already have," Paul said. "No one means to usurp your authority — you need only speak with Prince Jeon directly."

"I will speak with him," Fairwind said, "but not under duress, and not as a prisoner."

"You'll speak with him as a prisoner, for now," Paul said. "We'll sort the rest later. What matters right now is how many men are below, how many we'll have to fight to retake this place. How many of your garrison did the hobgoblins kill, when they came?"

"None," Fairwind said. "I had them moved somewhere they wouldn't be harmed."

"To cells?"

"Not cells, exactly."

"I assumed they were prisoners."

"Gentlemen, I think you misunderstand the whole of this," Fairwind said. "There is an ongoing dispute among the princes of the sea principalities — a conversation, several of us party to it. It seems Jeon failed to fully brief his nephew, or Paul simply failed to pass it along properly."

"It sounds," Paul said, "as though what you're actually doing is siding with the Toli against us."

"I've been allied with Port Toli for hundreds of years," Fairwind said, "as have all the outer isles. Equal partners in this nation's decisions, same as Jeon."

"Does Toli suffer under this embargo the way Monmurg does?"

"No one suffers as Monmurg does, currently."

"Then your alliance with the Toli, against Monmurg specifically, is precisely the issue," Paul said.

"If I choose to side with the Toli against Monmurg," Fairwind said, "that's my own right to exercise."

"Not any longer," Paul said. "Not in this company. We've one of Lord Jamis's own agents standing here, and we hold one of your Toli spies besides."

"Do the rest of you even know these two men," Fairwind asked the room at large, "or have you only just met them, this week, this very day?"

"We're not here to answer your questions," Paul said.

"That's not how this works," Gouge said. "Can you guide us to the passage below?"

"Guide you where, exactly?"

"To wherever the barrels are being taken off this island."

"Ah," Fairwind said. "You mean to go down there."

"We'd like to recapture what's being stolen," Paul said. "We've seen the barrels ourselves."

"Wouldn't that be the surest way to hold this island against the sahaugin, at minimum?" Gouge pressed.

"I think it's the only way Monmurg survives this at all, frankly," Fairwind admitted.

"When does the boat carrying them leave?"

"It's come and gone several times already," Fairwind said. "What matters more is this — for months, I've been quietly moving as much of the substance as I could to Fairwind Island itself, to a place only I know."

"All the more reason to speak with Jeon directly," Paul said.

"I will."

"This is well above what we can settle," Paul said, "but we're still taking you down there."

"I don't see how that's possible," Fairwind said.

"You'll show us the passage," Gouge said, "and we'll try for the ship."

"What ship?"

"The one in the harbor."

"You mean Cain Toli's own vessel," Fairwind said, something almost pitying in it. "With his hand-picked men, his personal guard, hundreds of hobgoblins?"

"How large is the ferry that runs between it and shore?"

"A smaller ship moves between the two," Fairwind said. "That's the one presently in the cove. We're trying to get it back into open water, to help repel the sahaugin properly."

"We'll take that one, then," Gouge said. "And smuggle you back to Monmurg with us. Or negotiate something else, if it comes to it."

"Perhaps we discuss terms once we're seated somewhere safe."

"None of us much care for your terms," Gouge said. "You've already admitted to siphoning barrels for your own gain, full well knowing Monmurg needs this island's protection. That alone sets you against Jeon."

"Correct," Fairwind said. "I'll exercise my right to silence on the rest, then."

"You've crippled Jeon's defenses by taking those barrels," Gouge pressed, "which let the sahaugin overrun this place outright. At minimum, you've two hundred dead Monmurgian Marines in that bay to answer for."

"I respect your uncle a great deal, Paul," Fairwind said. "He's an honest man. But I don't think he fully grasps what's occurring among the principalities, and that's the whole of my disagreement with him."

"That'll be sorted at his office in Monmurg," Paul said.

"I don't believe you're leaving this island alive," Fairwind said, quiet now, "unless I allow it."

"Why's that? How would you stop us?"

"I wouldn't need to," Fairwind said. "I'd simply let the sahaugin handle it."

"You just said we'd leave only if you allowed it."

"Unless our business here concludes properly first," Fairwind said. "Do you need me to guide you to what remains of the stockpile?"

"Yes, please," Gouge said.

"Then, clearly," Fairwind said, "I retain some advantage here yet — a genuine bargaining chip."

"For what it's worth," Gouge murmured to Paul, aside, "the only reason he's talking to us civilly at all is that he's charmed."

"I'd guessed as much," Paul said.

"I don't know how long it holds."

"While you and the rest of the princes squabble over your petty grievances," Paul said, turning back to Fairwind, frustration finally breaking through, "the people of Monmurg suffer for it. Nothing improves until the sahaugin are repelled, order's restored, and this blockade ends. Let's go below, deal with this properly, and get that substance into the water."

"It's being moved out even as we speak," Fairwind said.

"He makes a fair point, regardless," Gouge allowed. "Monmurg's people are the ones who'll suffer most in any of this dragging on."

"This is only a preview of everything Jeon's been warning about," Paul said. "Can we go — I'm tired of this back and forth."

"There are more barrels being moved as we speak," Fairwind reminded them.

"Do you know where the remainder actually sits?" Paul asked.

"I know where the last barrels on this island are kept, yes."

"What's your actual play in all this, then?" Gouge asked.

"Very soon I'll have no bargaining chip left at all," Fairwind admitted. "The Toli will have taken the rest before long. For now, I still control access to a real cache. What I'd genuinely like, going forward, is to see all of you escape this island successfully."

"We escape together," Gouge said, "on that smaller ship."

"I will not be coming with you."

"You can't simply stay behind."

"I can, and I will."

"We'll settle that once we're clear of danger," Gouge said. "If we make it out at all — that's the priority now. We need our Marines back, our people back."

"Stay close to me, then," Fairwind said. "If the Toli notice their ferry's being taken, you'll need to move fast and slip away in whatever you've got."

It was past three in the morning by then, Gouge judged, weighing the hours already spent. "Could we still slip past the other lighthouse — did our troops ever secure it?"

"No," Fairwind said. "They staged a diversion there, but never held it outright."

"Then you'll take us to what remains," Gouge said. It wasn't a question.

"Yes," Fairwind said. "Let's go."

He rose, and the party fell in around him.

"Come along, then, friend," Gouge said, keeping a firm grip on the man's shoulder, something dark beneath the mock warmth. "Best of friends, you and I."

"There are hobgoblins along the upper shafts," Fairwind told them as they moved, "though not on the passage we're taking. There are sahaugin in the water caves near it as well — if we carry barrels through there, the substance should kill or drive off anything we pass."

"That's how we survive the crossing, then," Gouge said.

"You'll need to get near them regardless," Fairwind warned. "All the remaining barrels are staged close to the water caves now."

"I have no doubt you could negotiate with Jeon as something near an equal, in time," Fairwind added, as they reached the stairwell. "But you don't negotiate that way with me — not while you hold Jamis's agent and one of my own hostages both. Negotiate with Jeon himself, and I expect he'll deal with you fairly, given what you're bringing back to him. But to get off this island alive, past the sahaugin, you'll need at minimum enough barrels to secure the Helm and reach that smaller ship — and take it outright."

"One barrel secures the Helm a good while, you said."

"That would do it."

"Then that's exactly what we'll move quickly to get," Gouge said.

"If you'd wanted me assassinated outright," Fairwind observed, without much heat, "you'd have done it by now. Let's not pretend we negotiate as equals — we don't. But I won't grovel for it either."

"Fair," Gouge said, and turned to practical matters. "Where exactly are your troops, if they're not locked away?"

"On the lower level," Fairwind said, "with no access to the upper floors — bolted in from the outside, hobgoblin units guarding the passage. Effectively locked in, key or no."

"Do you have the key?"

"I do not. It's simply bolted."

"If we wanted to free them, could we reach that level through the sanctum, some other way down?"

"There are hobgoblins past the sanctum guarding exactly that passage."

"We already walked through there, on our way in," Gouge said. "Were those particular troops disarmed?"

"Yes," Fairwind said. "Those were the ones moved into the barracks, unarmed."

They fell into marching order — Gouge and Tyrus flanking Fairwind, Paul close at hand, the bound Toli held fast by the hair, Jude bringing light enough to see by.

"Are you certain about this?" Fairwind asked, as they reached the head of the stairwell.

"Yes," Gouge said.

"It's the only way down, then," Fairwind said. "Fine — I'll take you to the veils, as it were."

And they began their descent, a spiral staircase turning down into the dark beneath the Helm.

# Chapter Twenty-Five — What Folsom Learned

Folsom found himself walking near enough to Fairwind, in the shuffle of the descent, to risk a quiet word. "Can we whisper?" he asked. "In case anyone's near."

"What is your name, boy?" Fairwind asked, ignoring the question.

"Folsom. Marine bard, for what that's worth. I take it you're Lord Fairwind."

"I am," Fairwind said. "You seem a decent young man. Why have you fallen in with these cutthroats?"

"Following orders," Folsom said. "I was put on this mission by my captain."

"Orders. I wish I might speak with Paul Rivera alone, when this is settled — do you think that could be arranged?"

"I don't see why not."

"Perhaps once we're through this," Fairwind said. "You should know something, before it's too late to matter — Prince Jeon is being betrayed, by one of his own greatest advisors. You know the name Lord Jamis?"

"No."

"A famous man, you'd think — but no matter. Do you know he's kept contact with agents of Keoland?"

"I didn't know that either."

"Do you know his wife?"

"I couldn't say I do."

"Lady Azane, she goes by now — that wasn't always her name. Her brother is Lord Zafar Azane, a high noble of Keoland, loyal directly to the Duke of Gradsul. You know the Duke?"

"I'm sure I don't."

"The most powerful lord in Keoland," Fairwind said, "and the sea principalities' oldest enemy. His navy is the one blockading Monmurg this very moment. And Zafar Azane is Lord Jamis's own wife's brother."

"That's not the best news I've heard today," Folsom admitted.

"You didn't know it, did you," Fairwind said, something satisfied in the observation. "It's not common knowledge — though I make a point of watching it closely. Zafar has visited Jamis's castle several times these past months. I believe Jamis is betraying Jeon to the Duke himself. That's simply how they are, all of them."

"Jeon?" Folsom said, working through it.

"Yes. Do you know this Gouge, the one leading all of you?"

"A day, perhaps. No more."

"One of Jamis's own assassins," Fairwind said. "He's killed a great many people. Not a kind man — vicious, in truth. The elf's the same, one of Jamis's other creatures, both of them well-practiced in whatever dark work Jamis has needed done. It would go badly for you, if I were killed and you found yourself innocent in all this. Badly for your companions too — most of them innocent as well, I'd wager. That large, honest fellow. Merrick, whom I know to be an honorable man. None of you truly grasp what's at stake here."

"I appreciate the consideration," Folsom said, meaning it more than he expected to.

"Good men of the principalities shouldn't be dragged into the quarrels of princes," Fairwind went on. "And Paul Rivera — I hope he knows what he's doing, bringing you all into this. I believe he means loyalty to his uncle, nothing worse. What I'd ask of you is simply this: do what you can to help him survive his time with Gouge and the wizard."

"Absolutely," Folsom said.

"There's so much I'd ask, given everything you've just told me," he admitted, "though I don't know you, nor you me. But we are men of the sea, both of us."

"I'll do everything in my power to see you survive this," Fairwind said, "so that you might be properly questioned, in time."

"I'm not so sure I'll be the one facing questions."

"Understand me," Fairwind said. "You will survive, if I have any say in it — you and your companions both. Gouge, I can't speak for. I believe him an agent of Jamis, the elf as well — but if you don't resist, you'll come through this whole. And know this: if I don't survive what's coming, carry word to my family of what I've told you. Tell Paul Rivera of my concern as well — that I believe Lord Jamis a traitor, in league with Zafar Azane, who leads the Office of Forbidden Magic in Gradsul. I know little of this may mean much to you now, but understand: there are secret orders of wizards whose whole purpose is the ruin of this principality."

"I understand," Folsom said, quiet now. "These are dangerous times."

"And the course I'm taking, I take for the survival of all of you," Fairwind said. "I know we're no friends, not truly, not yet — but I promise you can survive this, and be dealt with fairly after."

"I appreciate your honesty," Folsom said. "And your concern."

"No," Fairwind said. "Thank you. For reversing your witness against me, if it comes to that."

# Chapter Twenty-Six — Into the Caverns

The stairs gave way at last to a rough-hewn passage, and from somewhere in the dark ahead came the sound of whispering — scattered, indistinct, impossible to place among the echoing stone. They pressed on regardless, three hundred yards down a narrow ledge cut along the wall of what could only be an underground river, the tunnel curving and descending as it went, the water below audible but unseen.

Then, rounding a turn, torchlight appeared ahead — perhaps a hundred feet off, marking the mouth of some chamber beyond, and a figure crossed briefly through the light before vanishing into the passage beyond it.

"We should let the others know," Tyrus murmured.

"I'll go ahead," Gouge said, and covered his shield's light before creeping forward alone, feeling his way by touch along the wall in the total dark — slower than any pace he'd have chosen, testing each board with his foot before trusting his weight to it.

Behind him, in the confined stone, the others' hushed voices carried further than any of them meant them to. Gouge pressed on regardless, closing to within forty feet of the torchlight — near enough to make out two voices, human, speaking Common, though the words themselves stayed just beyond his hearing. Not hobgoblins. That alone was enough to trouble him.

He turned back the way he'd come, but as he crept along the platform, feeling his way in the dark, his footing slipped at the edge, and he began to fall toward the water below before Tyrus's hand closed hard around his arm and hauled him back onto solid planking.

Somewhere far below, something surfaced — a tail slapping the water, unmistakable even at a distance.

"Something's down there," Merrick murmured, straining to listen. He was closer to the water than the others, closer even to the bound Toli prisoner still gagged and stumbling along beside him.

"There's something we need to talk about," he told the others quietly, drawing them back within earshot. "There's something big in the water — I heard a tail slap against the surface."

"How did you hear it and we didn't?" Tyrus asked.

"Ranger's ear, that's all. Doesn't matter — it's maybe thirty feet below us now, and we've been descending toward it the whole while." He studied the dark water as best he could, drawing on everything he knew of the sea, and made out at last a shape moving beneath the surface — something built along the lines of a shark, but far larger than any shark had a right to be, patrolling a slow loop through a channel wide enough, here, to swallow it whole.

"How big?" Tyrus asked.

"Forty feet, near enough."

"That's a massive creature."

"And it's not noticed us," Merrick said. "Just patrolling."

Gouge, still shaking off the fall, cut through the murmuring with a low, flat order. "We keep moving. If it comes to a fight, whoever reaches a barrel first cracks it and gets the powder into the water — sprinkle it, dump it, whatever it takes. It's either going to work, or we're all finished, Fairwind included. In that case, I'd suggest pushing him in first."

"Understandable," Tyrus said, then turned to Fairwind, gagged and helpless beside them. "Any magic dust in your pockets, by chance?"

Fairwind, unable to answer past the gag, only stared back.

"I might have a little of the yellow stuff left," Jude offered, though a moment's thought convinced him otherwise — they'd already spent what they'd carried.

"Long as it stays in the water and doesn't come crawling out on legs," Tyrus said, "I'm not overly worried."

"It won't," Jude said, with more confidence than he entirely felt. He'd heard the old fishermen's tales of such things — beasts forty feet long, capable of cutting a man clean in half — but had never laid eyes on one himself until tonight.

"Then we keep moving," he said. "Shaking in my boots, but moving. If it comes to a fight, whoever reaches a barrel opens it and starts sprinkling."

They pressed on in careful, deliberate silence, Gouge creeping ahead alone once more despite every attempt to rein him in — "Gouge does Gouge," as Tyrus put it, with the weary resignation of a man who'd already given up trying. Merrick, unwilling to risk the dark passage himself, reached Tyrus instead by a whispered thread of magic carried straight into his mind.

*Normally I'd have a joke ready for this,* the message ran, in Folsom's voice, carried the same way Folsom always got a man's attention across a crowded room. *Not this time. There's a forty-foot shark directly beneath us — one of the sahaugin's own, by the look of it. We're all of us standing near water it could reach in a heartbeat. Move faster if you can, or at the very least, don't fall in.*

"Don't have to tell me twice," Tyrus murmured, and pressed forward with rather more care than before.

# Chapter Twenty-Seven — Voices in the Dark

Gouge crept the platform's length alone, one hand trailing the wall, testing each board with painstaking care despite the noise carrying from behind him — hushed, urgent whispers that echoed further through the caverns than he'd have liked. He reached the edge of the torchlight's reach and held there, in the last true shadow before the light would betray him outright, and listened.

Two voices, unmistakably human, speaking Common — though not what they said. Not hobgoblins, whoever they were. He held a long moment, confirmed no one followed, and turned to make his way back.

He found Tyrus waiting at the edge of hearing, and as he approached, the sound of a dagger being drawn greeted him from the dark — Folsom, startled by the footsteps, weapon already in hand before he recognized the shape moving toward him.

"Why'd you pull that?" Tyrus asked.

"Didn't know it was you," Folsom admitted, sheathing it again.

"There's a shark down there," Gouge said, low and urgent. "Don't go in the water. And humans, up ahead, talking — couldn't make out what."

"Let's get off this platform," Folsom said. "If we go over the edge here, we go in right where it's patrolling."

They gathered the rest and pressed on, Gouge taking point again near the torchlight, watching the passage ahead through the flickering glow — until, without warning, a voice rolled out of the dark toward them, echoing off the stone.

"Who's there?"

Folsom seized Fairwind by the arm. "Do you know these men?"

"Yes," Fairwind said.

"Speak for us, then. Please."

"It would benefit you," Fairwind said, weighing it, "if I spoke for you — and for Merrick."

"I'll blow your head off if you don't," Folsom muttered, low and hard, and shoved him forward. "Tell them — 'This is Lord Fairwind.' Just that."

Fairwind raised his voice, calling out toward the dark ahead. "It is I—" And then, breaking from whatever script Folsom had hoped for, his voice rose sharp and clear, carrying every bit as far as he could throw it: "I am being held prisoner, by agents of Lord Jamis! Cover your minds!"

The warning rang off the stone in every direction at once.

"Go, go!" Gouge shouted, already moving. "We don't want to fight — go!"

Jude's answer came without hesitation — his wand rising, a burst of pure force streaking through the dark and catching Fairwind full in the skull, the man dropping where he stood before the echo of his own warning had finished dying against the walls.

For a long, ringing moment, nothing else moved. Then the caverns erupted into motion on every side, and the fight that had been building since the moment they'd descended those stairs finally broke wide open.

# Chapter Twenty-Eight — The Chasm Fight

Tyrus surged forward first, closing thirty feet at a dead run, a guard rising ahead of him with shield already raised. "We'll kill him if you don't give us passage!" Tyrus shouted, more for effect than truth now that Fairwind lay senseless on the boards, and held his ground rather than press the advantage blindly.

Merrick, firing from the deep shadow at the rear where no enemy eye could find him, put an arrow into the nearest guard with the ease of a man shooting at something that couldn't shoot back. "Under attack!" the guard cried out, and the alarm spread through whatever chamber lay ahead.

Folsom moved to secure what mattered most in the chaos — Fairwind himself, unconscious but not dead, a dagger pressed to his throat the moment Folsom reached him, more to keep the man from anyone else's hands than from any real fear he'd wake and flee. "Cease fire! Hold!" Folsom called out, and held the position while the fight broke out around him.

Gouge loaded and fired into the dark ahead, his bolt finding a guard's shoulder with brutal effect, then slid past Folsom and the fallen Fairwind along the narrow ledge with the careful grace of a man who knew exactly how easily any misstep here ended in the water below. As he passed, Fairwind — stirring, some flicker of consciousness returning even through the daze of the blow — kicked out at Folsom's leg, catching Paul as well in the scramble that followed, though both of them kept their footing by the narrowest of margins.

Paul was not so fortunate a moment later. Whatever hold Folsom had kept on the prisoner broke as Fairwind thrashed free, and in the chaos Paul lost his footing entirely and went over the edge, plunging down toward the black water below — toward, he realized in the instant before he struck the surface, the pale shape of a Toli corpse already floating there.

Jude, watching Fairwind break loose and turn his violence on whoever stood nearest, weighed a fireball against the risk to his own people and held it back, watching instead as Fairwind — no longer bound by any lingering charm, all pretense of cooperation gone — struck out at Gouge with a flurry of blows, catching him hard across the back of the skull.

"I want credit," Gouge called out, half delirious with the pain of it, "if that shark down there eats a Toli before it eats me."

"Everyone clear of this catwalk," someone shouted. "It's not stable enough to fight on."

Fairwind pressed the attack, driven now by nothing but the animal need to escape, shoving and striking at anyone in reach rather than fighting with any purpose beyond flight. Gouge staggered under a second blow, caught himself at the very edge of the platform by the barest margin, and turned on the man with something past reason in his eyes.

"I've got this bastard right beside me," Gouge said, and seized Fairwind bodily, wrestling him toward the edge of the walkway in a contest that ended, decisively, in Gouge's favor.

"Go drink with the fish, then," Gouge said, and threw him over.

The splash carried up from thirty feet below, and for a moment nothing else happened. Then, further out in the dark water, something surfaced — a violent thrash, a shape rising and dragging something under before the water stilled again. Whether it had been Fairwind or some other unlucky soul caught in the current, no one watching from above could say for certain.

Tyrus pushed on toward an open doorway ahead, readying a burst of divine fury for whatever waited there, and found two guards in breastplates rounding the corner to meet him, swords already drawn.

"Jude!" he called out, even as he engaged them. "Get to Paul — pull him out of that water!"

Jude was already moving, casting his sight across the dark surface below with the clarity only elven eyes could offer in such blackness, and found Paul there, flailing weakly, something unseen brushing past his leg in the current. The spell caught him and lifted him clear in a single smooth pull, twenty feet up out of the water in an instant, hanging now above the platform with the rest of the climb still ahead of him.

The fight at the doorway ground on in the meantime — Tyrus taking two heavy blows from a guard plainly more skilled than his fellow, disarmed at last by a strike that knocked the sword clean from his grip. Jude, dashing into range, flung a hand axe at the man's head instead, catching him hard enough across the ear to send him reeling without quite finishing the job.

Gouge closed the gap and ended it properly — a clean stroke across the throat for one guard, and for the second, a near-perfect finishing blow that dropped him where he stood, badly wounded but still clinging to consciousness enough to take one last swing at Gouge as he fell, catching him hard across the legs and very nearly putting him down as well.

Tyrus finished what remained with a knife thrown true and a follow-up strike that ended the last guard for good, and in the sudden quiet that followed, they turned at last to hauling Paul the rest of the way up onto solid ground — soaked, shaken, but alive.

"Look for Fairwind!" Paul called out, the moment he had breath enough to shout it, and the cry went up from more than one throat at once, echoing uselessly into the dark water below.

Nothing answered. No sign of the man surfaced, not then, and not in the long minutes that followed while they searched the black water by whatever light they could manage — until, further along the current, something pale drifted into view.

An arm. Nothing more attached to it, no other feature to mark whose it had been.

"I don't care whose arm it is," Jude said, when the argument over it flared briefly among them, "if someone's bleeding to death down there—"

"It matters," Paul said, "because either we're still chasing Fairwind, or he's already dead and this tells us nothing worth chasing at all." He raised his hand and cast a small working of light directly onto the floating limb, close enough now to make out the markings clearly.

Toli. Not Fairwind's arm at all.

"Then he might still be alive," Paul said, something almost like relief in it, though relief was hardly the right word for what still lay ahead of them.

# Chapter Twenty-Nine — What the Water Gave Back

They searched what remained of the water they could reach, Paul lowering himself on a length of rope while the others watched from above, the arm's faint residual glow giving them just enough light to make out shapes in the black current — sharks circling back into view, and among them, unmistakably, the pale shifting shape of a sahaugin.

"We need to keep moving," Tyrus said, watching the water darken further with movement. "We've got to reach those barrels."

They pulled Paul back up and pressed on, deeper into the cavern system, until the passage opened without warning into a vast grotto — five hundred feet across if it was an inch, wide enough that a proper ship might have sailed into it whole. A wooden walkway descended from the platform where they stood down to a landing far below, docks visible in the gloom, and mounted on a ledge overlooking it all, a ballista — a siege weapon, plainly meant to cover anyone approaching from the water.

No ship rode at the docks below, but tents stood there, and beside them, a stack of barrels waiting to be moved.

"I want that ballista on the barrels," Tyrus decided. "Not to destroy them — just to pop one open near the water, let the current do the rest. I'll need someone to help me man it."

"I've got crossbow training," Gouge said. "I'll take the other end."

"The rest of you, down to the landing," Tyrus said.

Merrick weighed the ballista's range against his own spells and found himself well outside it either way, and chose to hold the higher ground instead, watching the passage behind them for anything still following from the direction of the chasm — Fairwind, the sahaugin, whatever else the night still had in store.

"I'll stay up with him," Folsom decided, close enough within sixty feet to keep his voice a weapon in its own right if the moment called for it.

Jude chose to hold the line as well, and the remaining three — Tyrus, Gouge, and Paul — started down the long wooden walkway toward the barrels waiting below, the dawn still an hour off and the cave lit only by scattered, guttering torches.

# Chapter Thirty — The Poison Sea

Sunrise was still an hour distant when the four who'd gone down reached the beach proper, and the water answered their arrival almost at once — pale shapes breaking the surface near the barrels, three sahaugin rising from the shallows in a rush.

"Right by the barrels," Gouge said, grim.

The ballista spoke first, Tyrus's aim true enough to skewer one creature clean through and stop the bolt lodged in a second body behind it — a devastating opening blow that dropped the first outright and left the second reeling. Jude, watching from the platform above, put an arrow into a third before the fighting on the beach had properly begun.

Folsom's voice found its mark next, a cutting verse aimed at the nearest sahaugin that left it staggering, disadvantaged and rattled, easy prey for the crossbow bolt Gouge put through it a moment later.

Paul weighed the distance for a fireball and held off, waiting for a better cluster to present itself, while Merrick's arrow found its mark on the creature nearest Tyrus with a force that nearly finished it outright. Gouge closed on a wounded sahaugin and ended it with cutlass and dagger together, a blade across the throat and a second through the eye, and turned to find another already closing on him in its place.

"I want a barrel," Gouge said, disengaging despite the opening it cost him, taking a solid hit for the trouble but reaching the stack regardless. Paul dropped his own sword and shield to haul a second barrel free, staggering with the weight of it toward the waterline.

The sahaugin pressed their advantage while the two men struggled toward the water, sensing blood and closing in, until Folsom's rapier and Jude's follow-up strike from behind finished the nearest one cleanly.

"Nice — way to go, bard," Gouge called, even as he fought to keep his footing under the weight of the barrel and the wounds already gathering across his arms.

Jude, weighing his position at eighty feet and finding it enough, shaped his last fireball with the same careful precision he'd used before, sparing the two men wrestling with the barrels while the blast caught the three remaining sahaugin square. Two died outright, incinerated where they stood; the third, catching only the edge of it, collapsed into the shallows regardless, badly burned and finished off a moment later by Merrick's arrow.

Gouge reached the water at last and pried the barrel open with his bare hands, no weapon free for the work, and the lid gave way to reveal a pale yellow powder that carried the faint, unmistakable stink of sulfur.

"Gods," he muttered, flinging the contents wide into the shallows around him, "we could've set this whole cave alight without meaning to, this entire time." He heaved the rest of the barrel out into deeper water for good measure, dousing himself in the process — his own skin, where the powder had touched it, shifting slowly toward a sickly, greenish yellow.

The last wounded sahaugin lunged at him with spear and claw both and missed on every count, and a moment later let out a strange, wet cough, blood and water bubbling from its gills before it collapsed face-first into the poisoned shallows and did not move again.

"I should wash this off," Gouge said, eyeing his own discolored skin.

Paul finished the last standing sahaugin with a dagger driven home while it was still reeling from the powder's effect, and then there was nothing left to fight — only the four of them standing on the beach, watching the water for further movement.

It came, in its own way, ten seconds later. A bloated shape surfaced first, then another, then dozens more scattered across the open water of the grotto — sahaugin corpses by the score, forty or fifty of them at least, and among them the massive carcass of the great shark itself, floating belly-up in the tide.

"That's the same one from before?" Gouge asked, studying its size.

"Looks to be," came the answer. Between the sharks and the shark-folk both, near a dozen more corpses than the sahaugin themselves littered the water — a number that would have overwhelmed them entirely, had it all come at once rather than in the scattered handful that had actually attacked.

"Any sign of Fairwind, among all that?" Paul asked, scanning the drifting bodies.

No sign of him surfaced. Whatever had become of Lord Fairwind, the poisoned water gave no answer, and the grotto settled slowly back into silence around them — the barrels secured, the sahaugin driven from these waters for what might yet prove to be a very long while, and the fate of the man who had once ruled this island still, for now, an open question.

---

## PART SEVEN: ESCAPE FROM HELM ISLAND

# Chapter Thirty-One — The Empty Racks

Morning of the third of Wealsun found Jude retracing his own steps up from the grotto, moving slow and careful along the ledge, watching the stone floor for anything the water might have left behind. No dripping trail marked the passage back the way he'd come — no sign that Fairwind, if he'd survived that fall, had hauled himself out anywhere along this stretch.

He reached the stair and considered the lower levels, the barracks, the armory, before recalling there was only the one route through — the chamber where they'd fought the Toli, no side passages branching off it anywhere he'd seen. That left the climb upward, toward the inner sanctum, and the bar still sitting fast across its door exactly as they'd left it, sealed from the inside by their own hands hours before.

He unbarred it and stepped through into the familiar hush of the council chamber — the dead hobgoblin still slumped where he'd fallen, the dead Toli beside him, neither disturbed. No wet footprints anywhere near either body. Whatever had happened to Fairwind, he hadn't come back through here.

Jude eased the outer door open next and looked out on the passage beyond — hobgoblins still posted along it, the same troops they'd slipped past on the way in. He drew the door shut again without a sound, invisible as he was, and barred it once more behind him rather than risk contact.

He turned instead to the two rooms he hadn't yet searched — a library of sorts on one side, records and almanacs and old trade charts, nothing that spoke to sorcery or secrets; a council room on the other, the map-table where they'd first overheard Fairwind's voice. No other doors, no other passages. This inner sanctum sat sealed off from the rest of the keep entirely, built more like a lord's private tower than any proper fortress wing.

From the library's window he could see clean across the island — the army that had staged its demonstration at the gate the night before had pulled back now, the assault abandoned rather than pressed. And further out, where a ship had ridden at anchor when they'd first climbed these stairs, the water sat empty. The Sea Ghost was gone.

Thirty minutes, he judged, turning the timing over. Long enough for a strong swimmer to make the distance from shore, even through waters that had lately held sharks — though those same sharks, he reasoned, would have scattered the moment that first barrel went into the grotto, drawn off by the reek of the compound rather than toward any struggling man in the current.

He left the library as he'd found it and made his way back down, checking the floor at every turn for water that wasn't there, until he reached the one passage he hadn't yet explored — a barred gate, standing open, leading into a wider cavern system beyond. He slipped through.

Racks lined the walls inside, empty now, stretching back further than he could easily judge — space enough, by the look of it, to have held tens of thousands of barrels in better days. Whatever this place had once been, it had served as the island's great warehouse, and someone had been quietly emptying it long before this night's chaos had ever begun. No wet footprints marked the stone here either.

Beyond the racks, a second tunnel opened onto a second barred door — this one locked fast, no key at hand to open it. Through its grate, though, morning light spilled in from a small cove beyond, sand pale in the new sun, and there, unmistakable even at a distance, footprints crossed the beach.

He studied them as long as the grate allowed him to, but there was nothing more to learn from the shape of them alone — only the confirmation that someone had come this way, and gone. He turned back at last, retracing the full length of the passage, and made his way toward the others to report what he'd found, and hadn't.

# Chapter Thirty-Two — What Fire and Iron Decide

The party had gathered on the docks by then, the fight behind them and the grotto's dead settling slowly beneath the surface, and the talk had already turned from recovery to reckoning. Paul counted the barrels twice to be certain — twenty-five, stacked and waiting — while Gouge went hunting through his own gear for a flask of red-eye and Tyrus turned his attention to the tents pitched near the water's edge.

The tents gave up their answer quickly enough: a staging ground, weapons and armor matching what the hobgoblins had carried, plainly where they'd geared up before ever setting foot on the island proper.

"The ship's going to come back," Gouge said, low and certain, "and we have to be ready to take it when it does."

"We don't have time to fully rest, then," Tyrus said, weighing the twenty-five barrels still sitting exposed on the beach against however long that reckoning might take. "Not with this much sitting out here for the taking."

"We could move them up by the ballista," Paul offered. "March them up while there's still time."

Jude shook his head at that. "If the ship comes and we're still hauling barrels, we lose the only advantage we've got. Better to load the ballista, post an archer, and cover this beach from above if trouble comes."

They weighed the ground itself — how far a man could climb and still keep the cove entrance in view, whether the ballista's arc could cover the water approach as well as the docks. It could, Gouge confirmed, having sighted along it himself. The bodies scattered across the sand gave everyone pause a moment longer — whether to drag them out of sight, into a tent, out of decency more than tactics — but in the end it hardly mattered. Anyone coming in by daylight would see the dead floating regardless.

"Well," Paul said, the frustration finally breaking through as he looked out over the grotto, "we've lost Fairwind entirely. Do we burn the bridge behind us, then, and be done with it?"

"I think we can hold this ground from up top," Tyrus said. "Without Fairwind to chase, we could spend the time searching properly instead — thirty minutes, maybe, to sweep what's left of this place."

He turned the larger question over aloud, working through it as he spoke. Without Fairwind, they could still strip what remained of the stockpile, destroy what they couldn't carry, and work to free his locked-away troops — a hundred and fifty men, by his own account, shut in unarmed rather than truly imprisoned. Kill the interior guards standing over them, and the island itself might be held — twenty-five barrels ought to buy fifty days against the sahaugin returning, longer if rationed properly, long enough certainly to summon reinforcement from Monmurg.

"The whole island?" Paul said, doubtful. "We've barely cleared this stretch of it."

"Fairwind said himself one barrel does the job for the better part of a season," Tyrus reminded him. "We likely overdid it dumping what we did into this one cove. Spread thin and rationed, twenty-five could hold the whole approach for two months."

The question of readiness settled over them next — who had strength left, who needed rest, how many spells still sat in reserve. Jude had little enough remaining, a single working of any real weight and whatever cantrips still answered him. Merrick sat at nine hit points by his own count, bruised but standing; Folsom claimed himself fit enough; Tyrus shrugged off his own wounds as no great matter.

"If Cain Toli's still out there with the Sea Ghost," Tyrus said, "we won't manage much of anything until we know the numbers we're actually facing. Call it three hundred men, all told, between the outer garrison and whatever's held within the keep."

"There's still a hundred and fifty of Fairwind's Marines out there too," Paul said, "minus what we've already accounted for. He didn't want them harmed — only kept from the armory."

"Then we burn the bridge," Tyrus said, "haul the barrels up top, and bring his outer garrison over to our side. They never knew about his treachery — the same way they trusted us readily enough once they understood we spoke for Prince Jeon."

"What separates them from the men he kept locked in the castle, then?" Paul asked.

"Those he kept in the dark on purpose," Tyrus said. "You heard how he carried himself in there — arrogant enough that he'd never have looped his own household guard into a scheme like this. Otherwise we'd have had to fight through them outright, rather than find them shut behind a bolted door."

Paul turned the whole account over once more, weighing what his homunculus had overheard against what made sense of a proud man's pride. Perhaps Fairwind had meant to strike some quiet bargain — spare his people, hand over the barrels, let the blame land elsewhere. Tyrus wasn't buying it; the man had been sitting free and unbound when they'd first overheard him dealing with the Toli, the ropes and gag only a costume donned once footsteps sounded in the corridor. Fully complicit, Tyrus judged, and likely playing both sides at once — angling to shortchange the Toli even as he betrayed Jeon.

"That's how I read it too," Paul admitted. "Undercutting Monmurg while keeping a hand in every pot, letting the rest of the principalities tear at each other while he slips clear with the profit."

"I don't much fancy dying so he can walk away pleased with himself," Jude said.

"He walks away either way," Tyrus said. "The Toli agents in there with him are already dead. Sitting here debating it buys us nothing."

The plans multiplied faster than any of them could properly weigh — hide the barrels and wait out the sahaugin's return, or press on to find Fairwind's locked troops and turn them, or seize the resupply skiff when it came and simply leave. Each option carried its own price. To hide was to gamble that six or ten days would be enough before the sahaugin found their way back in force — though the tally of hours told a gentler story than that: it had been scarcely a day since they'd first set foot on this island, the second of Wealsun to the third, and Jeon's reinforcement might already be moving by midday.

"What if we took the signal tower instead," Paul said, "and warned Monmurg directly — sahaugin in these waters, watch the strait?"

That, at least, drew general agreement, though it left the matter of the barrels themselves — split the party to guard them, or risk the whole stockpile unwatched while the rest pushed toward the tower. Merrick, weighing both plans, judged the vantage point they already held as strong enough to serve for both purposes — a clear line on anything approaching by water, and cover over the rear passage besides.

"Why do we need to hold onto every barrel at all?" Paul asked. "Monmurg can't easily replace what it's lost under this embargo — but neither can whoever's been quietly draining this warehouse for months."

"That's precisely Fairwind's own arrangement," Tyrus said. "Skimming barrels off to some private stockpile of his own. There's more of this supply chain we haven't found yet, and it clearly moves through here on a schedule."

They turned the ballista in its cradle to test the angle up toward the stairway they'd come down by — fully adjustable, as it turned out, capable of covering that narrow approach as easily as the open water. One shot at a time, one man at a time forced to come through it. Not a wall, but a very costly door.

"We could always fireball the bridge outright," Tyrus said, "burn our own way in behind us if it comes to that."

"If I had the hour," Jude said, turning the problem a different direction, "I could raise Tenser's floating disk and haul the lot of it out myself. But we don't know this island well enough yet to trust any hiding place — given time enough, whoever comes looking will find it regardless."

In the end the argument circled back to the same fork it had started from: hunker down and heal, or push forward and gamble on seizing Fairwind's men and the inner keep both. And even that plan carried its own flaw, Jude pointed out — if Fairwind himself surfaced somewhere unlooked-for, would those same men follow him over strangers wearing borrowed loyalty?

"Then one of us kills him," Tyrus said simply, "if it comes to that."

"Hopefully not in front of his own troops," Jude said.

They settled, in the end, on the short rest — an hour, no more, with Folsom's flute lending its own quiet measure of healing to whoever needed it most. Merrick came back from his hit dice nearly whole; Folsom, untouched by the fighting himself, played on regardless, filling the cove with something gentler than the violence that had so lately filled it. Jude took his own dice and found himself down only a single point when the rest was done.

"I'll take my nine and go," he told Folsom, when the bard offered to share what little healing remained unclaimed.

"You want company?" Folsom asked.

"No," Jude said. "I'll go alone."

He cast invisibility over himself and slipped away from the group without further ceremony, bound for wherever Fairwind might have gone — the outer garrison, if he'd made the beach; the inner troops, if he'd somehow slipped back inside; or nowhere at all, if the water had already claimed him.

# Chapter Thirty-Three — What the Shark Gave Up

While Jude searched, Merrick turned his attention to the carcass floating in the grotto — the great forty-foot shark, belly-up now, a ballista bolt still lodged fast in its hide with a length of rope trailing from it into the water. He worked the rope hand over hand, hauling the massive body in against its own dead weight until it rode close enough against the ledge to climb aboard.

Two-thirds of the bulk sat submerged even now, but what remained above the waterline was mass enough to stand on, and Merrick set his feet against the pale, ruined belly and began to cut — carefully, enough to open a passage into the creature's interior without so much weakening the whole that it might sink beneath him.

The anatomy proved rougher going than any ordinary shark he'd dressed before, the hide thicker, the proportions all wrong for anything he'd trained on, but the work went forward regardless, and after a long while he began drawing the contents out into the daylight, one grim discovery at a time.

A head came free first — pale hair, sharp features, unmistakably Toli, unmistakably the man who'd fled the sanctum fight and never made it further than the water. Merrick set it aside without ceremony and kept working.

Half an hour passed in that grim excavation, the rest of what the beast had swallowed surfacing piece by piece, tossed overboard as it came free. No sign of Fairwind turned up among it — only, near the very end, a shirt far too large and fine to have belonged to anyone still living on this island, which gave Merrick a moment's pause before he cast that overboard as well.

Down at the beach, meanwhile, Gouge had already turned his attention to what remained of the tents — canvas and lumber both, torn apart and hammered into something that might, with enough charity, be called a raft. The hobgoblin gear scattered among the wreckage was good quality, worth a look, but the more pressing question was simpler: could this island's leavings carry six people and whatever barrels they judged worth saving, out past the tide and home to Monmurg?

The tide itself, he reasoned, would do most of the work — the currents ran strong through this stretch of water, in and out with the hours, and a raft riding that outgoing pull needed only paddle and a scrap of sail to make the five-hour crossing.

# Chapter Thirty-Four — Watch the Waves

Jude returned a little before eight that morning, dropping his invisibility as he crossed back to where Gouge stood waiting.

"Where did you go?" Gouge asked, some sharpness in it. "We'd barred that door behind us."

"I needed the time to get a spell back," Jude said. "The Sea Ghost's gone — not anchored where she was, not anywhere I could see. The hobgoblins who were demonstrating at the front gate have pulled back to garrison." He recounted the rest of it as they walked — the empty racks, the second barred door looking out on the little cove, the footprints in the sand beyond it, no wet trail anywhere to suggest Fairwind had come back through the sanctum itself.

"I've got lockpicks," Gouge said, turning that last detail over. "Good to know there's a door worth opening."

"If the Toli had come through there in the last half hour," Jude said, "they'd have left the floor wet behind them. I checked. Nothing."

"There's clearly more of this place we don't know," Gouge said, and turned the timing of it over aloud — whether the whole operation ran only by night, under cover of dark, to keep merchant eyes from ever spotting a ship where none should be. It would explain a great deal, if true — why Fairwind might not have swum clear the instant they'd taken him, why the powder dumped into the grotto had likely killed one of his own Toli partners before his eyes and left him scrambling.

"I don't know that he'd have swum that whole distance himself," Jude said, "sailor or not, sharks or no sharks. But I need to rest properly if a skiff does show — I'll take whatever I can get in the meantime."

They found Merrick down among the tents, sail rigged from canvas that had lately kept the rain off hobgoblin heads, and the raft itself taking shape plank by plank under Gouge's direction.

"What's all this?" Jude asked, eyeing the construction.

"Getting us home," Gouge said. "I sent Folsom up to the sanctum to signal Monmurg — on our way, watch the waves. Should meet us partway if the winds favor us. Five hours, maybe less."

"And the sharks?" Jude asked.

"We're bringing enough of the barrel powder to keep them off," Gouge said. "That's our whole ticket through open water."

"My concern's the same as yours," Jude admitted. "I don't care what else is happening on the rest of this island right now — there's a hundred messes we could chase down here, but the only sensible course is getting back, reporting what we've learned, and letting Jamis decide the rest from there."

"That's the whole of what we're doing," Gouge said.

They gave the work two hours in all — Merrick lashing the sail, Gouge and Folsom tearing free what lumber the docks would spare, Tyrus lending what strength he had between stretches of trance. Twenty-five barrels sat counted on the sand; only four would make the crossing, the raft judged too slight to carry more without risking the whole venture. Twenty-one would stay behind, enough — barely — to hold this stretch of shoreline against the sahaugin's return until reinforcement arrived to make the count matter less.

Before they launched, Folsom climbed to the inner sanctum, the dead hobgoblin and the dead Toli still lying undisturbed where they'd fallen, and worked the signal lantern at the window with the practiced hand of a man who'd learned the art properly. *On our way. Watch the waves.* Then Gouge's name, spelled out letter by letter across the strait toward a city he could not see but trusted was watching all the same.

They loaded the raft at last — Tyrus carried down bodily and settled to finish his rest aboard it, Gouge taking a rope-lashed hobgoblin corpse in tow as proof of what had happened here, Merrick working the crude sail while the outgoing tide caught the hull and drew it clear of the cave mouth.

"Keep sprinkling the powder as we go," Gouge said, cracking one of the four barrels loose and working handfuls of the pale yellow dust into the wake behind them at intervals. "Worth more than gold to us right now."

"And still just as ready to catch fire, given the chance," Jude reminded him, and Gouge only grinned at that, unbothered.

The crossing ran a full hour before the island fell properly behind them, the raft holding a slow but steady mile-an-hour pace under Merrick's sail, the tide doing the rest of the work. Another hour on, past ten in the morning by then, three corsairs broke the horizon ahead, cutting out from Monmurg's harbor toward them.

"Finally," Jude said, something in his shoulders easing for the first time since the fighting had ended.

Half an hour more brought the first corsair alongside, and willing hands hauled the six of them — and the barrels, and the grim, roped proof of a dead hobgoblin — up onto her deck. Merrick gave the account to her captain in broad strokes: the sahaugin, the fighting, the island reclaimed in part and still contested in the rest. No word passed to the ordinary crew of what the yellow powder truly was, or why it mattered so much more than its weight in gold — that account, all of them agreed without needing to say so aloud, belonged to Lord Jamis alone.

"Back to Monmurg," Gouge told the captain, when the question of course came. "Send the other two on to the island — the cove we cleared is secure, but the Helm itself still needs retaking, and our people at the barracks need reinforcing."

The captain gave the order, and two corsairs peeled away toward Helm Island, full of Marines bound for whatever remained to be done there, while the third turned her bow toward home, carrying six exhausted, salt-crusted survivors and four barrels of a secret that had already cost more lives than any of them yet fully understood.

---

## PART EIGHT: DEBRIEFING IN MONMURG

# Chapter Thirty-Five — What Gouge Told Jamis

Evening found the corsair docked, and the party scattered to separate summonses before they'd so much as washed the salt from their skin. Paul and Jude were led up toward the Royal Palace and Lord Jamis's own offices; Gouge and Merrick were taken instead to the Harbor District, to the chambers where Jamis conducted the quieter half of his business. Jamis wished to speak with each of them apart, and Gouge went first.

He knew the room well enough to help himself without asking — crossed straight to the sideboard, poured from the cheapest bottle in reach, and settled into the chair across from Jamis's desk with the easy unconcern of a man who'd sat there many times before.

"Report," Jamis said, without preamble.

"Paul made it back safely," Gouge said.

"Thank you for that." Something in Jamis's bearing eased, fractionally, at the words.

"Barely," Gouge admitted, "but safely."

"Was his performance in question?"

Gouge turned the glass in his hand, weighing his answer. "He's got a sense of entitlement to him — fair enough, given who he is. Overall he did well. Interesting young man, gaining experience fast. Whether that chip on his shoulder wears down with more time in the field, I couldn't say for certain."

"I think it will," Jamis said.

"I'd agree — a few more reality checks ought to chisel it down. I didn't push him too hard, this time, given you had me watching him as much as fighting alongside him. I expect I can work him harder next time, now I know what he can take."

"Was there conflict between the two of you?"

"None. He seemed respectable to me, and I'll take that. He had insight into events out there worth reporting — though I'm not entirely sure how far to trust his account of exactly how things unfolded."

"There was clearly more going on out there than even you'd suspected going in," Jamis said.

Gouge gave the barest shrug. "His somewhat lax view of the whole situation struck me as questionable, though I don't care to overstep my bounds saying so."

"No mystery in that," Jamis said, folding his hands before him. "He and Fairwind are, in truth, very good friends. He wouldn't wish to think ill of the man. That's simply the shape of it, Gouge. My own eye has to stay entirely objective in these matters — I've had Fairwind on my radar some while now, his jealousy of Prince Jeon, of Monmurg's prosperity generally. There are ongoing negotiations between the Toli and the outer princes, and Fairwind's the most outspoken among them. Jeon's inclined to give him some part of what he wants — certain outcomes he means to see happen in this country, and he's willing to spend real political capital chasing them. Personally, I don't think it's worth the cost. He's something of an idealist, and that's the trouble in this case. The other princes of the outer isles are not idealists, by contrast."

Gouge set his glass down and gave the fuller account — the landing near the southern beacon, the thirty men put ashore, the bridge tower taken and held, the standstill against the hobgoblins garrisoning the barracks beyond it. The push north with Merrick to the Helm's own barracks, the garrison captain there, the secret entrance they'd learned of but never used. The diversion that let them circle to the Helm's blind side. The strange corsair-style ship, eastern in its lines, loading barrels from a skiff that came and went beneath the fortress.

"The Sea Ghost," Jamis said, before Gouge had finished naming it.

"I've had reports of a ship by that name. Cain Toli's."

"Bought from Onwal," Jamis said. "Not common construction for us, though our own yards are beginning to copy the lines. I believe it was purchased specifically by Cain Toli."

"That tracks — the Toli were aboard her." Gouge went on: the climb into the Helm itself, the hobgoblins holding it under the same diversion, the creep to the inner sanctum, Fairwind found at last through the eyes of Paul's homunculus. "He was in there dealing with the Toli — not under any duress that we could see. Making an active bargain with them. We caused enough of a ruckus getting in that we engaged them all at once — killed one Toli, took a second alive, and took Fairwind himself, bound and tied, not at all pleased about it. Demanded to know why."

"And what did you have to go on, besides Paul's account?"

"That's the whole of it," Gouge admitted. "No one else witnessed the conversation directly — only Paul, through that creature's eyes. Fairwind tried to play it off once we reached him — tied up, claiming he'd been forced into it. We believe he'd bound himself, to make it look that way, rather than being taken by force."

He laid out the rest — the passage down through the smuggling caves, the barrels of granular substance, not spell nor enchanted fog but a manufactured compound, sprinkled into the water to keep the sahaugin at bay. The Toli hauling it away by the shipload. Fairwind's admission of his own private stash, kept back at his own keep, even as the rest of the island's stores were stripped bare. The dead Toli prisoner, killed in the scramble and dumped into shark-crowded water; the confirmation, later, of an arm floating free. Fairwind's own fall from the ledge, thrown loose in the chaos, gone without a trace once the search was done.

"So Fairwind directly attacked Paul Rivera?" Jamis asked, something sharpening in his voice at that.

"Not Folsom, who had him under a charm at the time — Fairwind was cordial enough to speak with, arrogant as ever, conversed with me readily. Said something like, 'I've got information, I've got a role to play here — I'm no captive in this.' Very unhappy about being dragged back against his will. He admitted to the barrels outright."

"So he did remove them from Helm Island."

"Yes. I believe he was lying to cover the rest — denied being in league with the Toli on it, though we had to weigh that against Paul's own account, given he's Jeon's nephew, and frankly I don't think the lad's skilled enough yet to be part of anything this calculated against us."

"That thought crossed my mind as well," Jamis said.

"You sent me to watch him, so I wasn't working off assumptions. I don't think he needs an agent watching for betrayal — his general attitude might give that impression more than any real evidence does, but I sense nothing genuinely wrong there."

"With Fairwind, though," Jamis said, something colder settling over his face, "I believe there is something. He's clearly up to more than we understand yet. Why strip the island's protection at a time like this, with the blockade already cutting off supplies to Monmurg — and no apparent concern for it?"

Gouge finished the account as plainly as he'd begun it — the fall into the chasm, Jude's levitation pulling Paul clear before the sharks could reach him, the search that turned up no trace of Fairwind but soft footprints on a passage leading east. "Jude scouted that himself, under invisibility, while we readied to leave. We couldn't bring Fairwind back for proper questioning in the end — I don't know how that would have gone politically, frankly, keeping him prisoner and bringing the matter to you. In the end we didn't have him to worry over either way. We sprinkled what we could on the way out, and Merrick built us a raft that got us home in one piece — fine work, that."

"The Navy men with you were quite capable too, from what I understand," Jamis said. "They told me you returned with twenty-three of the barrels."

"Four on the raft," Gouge said. "Twenty-one left behind, out of twenty-five total found."

"Twenty-one remaining, then." Jamis turned that over. "I didn't tell anyone precisely what the substance was, or how it works — not sure whether anyone briefed the crew returning to the island on its use, or simply told them to use it without explanation. I don't know who ended up minding the barrels left behind — wasn't me."

Gouge finished his glass. "To summarize my own part — I protected Paul, and observed what I could. He saw considerably more directly, and no one else can independently verify the conversation between the Toli and Fairwind, or confirm he wasn't simply bound the whole time as he claimed. We're going entirely on Paul's word for that. If Jeon sent him with instructions not to reveal too much, he seemed angry and on edge enough that he may have exposed more than intended, confronting Fairwind directly. I read that as more genuine than Fairwind's calm denials — a young man who couldn't hold back his anger struck me as more honest, in the moment, than practiced deception would look."

"The simple fact that Fairwind admitted to removing barrels from Helm Island at all is telling enough," Jamis said. "That substance, and its dwindling supply, is one of our most closely guarded state secrets. His admission suggests he transferred at least some of it to Fairwind Island itself."

"He mentioned a personal stash there," Gouge confirmed, "and seemed content to let the Toli take the rest. Suggests he's looking chiefly to his own island's interests just now."

Jamis rose and paced a slow turn behind his desk before he spoke again, his voice settling into the cadence of a man laying old history bare.
