"A hundred and fifty years ago, the sea principalities formed as a unified body because of an alliance between the Duke of Berghof and the pirates of the outer islands. The greatest scourge of the Azure Sea in those days was a vast hobgoblin kingdom, its stronghold just north of our own coast — raiding everything from here to the Dragon Isles, to Red Shore, to Monmurg itself. The trade we enjoy today would have been unthinkable under their dominion; they were the true rulers of these seas then.

"There's a book Merrick's carrying, which I read on the crossing home, covering how that hobgoblin kingdom devastated the undersea ecosystems too, as part of how all this came about — Admiral Flotsam wrote it some fifty years past, as a warning, around the time the Duke of Berghof first stopped giving us the yellow substance."

"Why'd he stop?" Gouge asked.

"Claimed it was no longer necessary. We held a vast reserve on Helm Island then, and the outer islands their own substantial stores besides. There was a fortress, once, in the middle of the bay to the north, that held the greatest concentration of the substance anywhere — the staging ground for our war against the hobgoblin kingdom. Ships sailed out regularly and dumped barrels of the stuff into hobgoblin waters. It didn't take long. Hundreds of thousands of hobgoblins died a hundred and fifty years ago, and that slaughter is what let our nation prosper afterward.

"Keoland benefited indirectly from our labor. But later, once we'd become a unified nation, conflict arose between us, and they turned our own historical tactics into political ammunition against us — because the powder isn't lethal only to the sahaugin and the hobgoblins. It strips clean everything in the affected waters: fish, wildlife, the very seabed. The elves and the other peoples of the Ulek states saw our methods as far too destructive, and we pulled back from large-scale use, maintaining only limited salting around Monmurg and a handful of strategic points, enough to keep the sahaugin from returning.

"Within the last decade, though, so much of the substance has grown unavailable to us that we've had to scale back even that limited use — which has forced us to reconsider our coastal defenses entirely. This is precisely what we've been negotiating with the Duke of Berghof about, though he's shown no real interest in resuming the supply.

"And recently, the lease on that fortress in the bay — where most remaining stores were held — expired, and Cain Toli took the island outright. At first he didn't deny us our usual shipments. This past winter, though, he cut us off entirely, claiming the Duke of Berghof had instructed the Toli that the outer islands were no longer to receive it, since it's been our lifeblood for coastal defense all this while. This coincides suspiciously with the Duke's own blockade of Monmurg — and I've heard there's been direct communication between the Duke and Prince Toli, which historically is bizarre, given the two have had essentially nothing to do with each other in hundreds of years."

"Who's actually producing the substance, then," Gouge asked, "if not Gradsul?"

"Not Gradsul, no. Even though Gradsul maintains what it claims is a very advanced magical academy — the Duke of Berghof's own school of transmutation among them — none of their transmuters rival the Duke's actual knowledge of how to produce it."

"I'm just wondering," Gouge said, "why the Toli would need Fairwind at all to take barrels off Helm Island, if they've already got direct access through Berghof."

"Nearly a direct provocation, isn't it." Jamis's mouth thinned. "I think Fairwind's actions stem from jealousy of Monmurg's success, specifically. Of all the modern principalities, Monmurg's benefited most from our alliance with the outer islands — the wealth flowing through us, the taxes, the trade volume, far beyond what raw piracy alone could ever provide the others, even though they still engage in it; it's simply not nearly as lucrative as the legitimate trade Prince Jeon's cultivated. Perhaps, from Fairwind's own view, he doesn't see himself as working against the principalities at all — perhaps he frames it as, 'I stand with all of you; Monmurg is the one truly working against the rest of us.'"

"Did he actually say that?"

"Not explicitly — I'm extrapolating, since he's never shown much warmth for Monmurg specifically, despite his long friendship with Jeon. I believe there's something colder underneath — jealousy of our success, combined with his objection to Jeon's political pressure, both at play together.

"And, to be fair, Fairwind likely has a legitimate grievance in the negotiations with the Toli and the Duke of Berghof. My read is that the Duke restricts our supply specifically because of Jeon's efforts to eliminate the slave trade out of the Mediko Jungle — he's petitioned some time now to halt it throughout the sea principalities, which would limit Prince Toli's ability to raid the jungle tribes for gold, ivory, and above all slaves. That's the bulk of Toli wealth, in truth — raiding those tribes, trading captives as human capital across the region.

"Jeon finds the practice distasteful, and would end it in Monmurg outright if he could. But both Berghof and the Toli profit too much from that trade to surrender it easily, and they're apparently willing to risk our broader alliance over it — even courting the Duke of Gradsul's influence as leverage, to bring Jeon back in line.

"I think Fairwind's underlying point is understandable enough — he's wary of provoking Berghof needlessly, and he's argued this position many times in council. Unfortunately, this latest move endangers everyone, especially with Gradsul already gaining ground against us militarily. Cain Toli is a separate and far graver matter — his betrayal runs deeper than anything Fairwind's doing. Fairwind, as far as I can judge, is simply angling for personal gain — gold, mostly, is my read on his true motive. His actions still constitute a betrayal of the sea principalities and of Jeon, and I understand the motive even where I don't condone it.

"What I don't yet fully grasp is how far the Toli, through Cain Toli specifically, are truly aiding the Duke of Gradsul in this war against us. About a month and a half past, a wizard visited our docks — you'll recall him; one of his associates hired you and Karmirg to stand guard."

"I don't recall that one directly," Gouge admitted.

"No matter. I'll summarize. What I wanted from that wizard was information on what the Toli sought along the coast near Saltmarsh. It seems they've located a powerful artifact there — one I believe grants control over turtle dragons. Our failures in certain naval engagements trace directly back to this, and it's a state secret, so this goes no further than this room.

"I tell you because the artifact must be recovered, if it can be — we need an opening to try. But Cain Toli, I believe, is our chief enemy in this. If he commands turtle dragons, that explains a great deal — when our corsairs engage Keoland's warships, storms often form at a distance, the classic sign of dragon activity nearby, and shortly after, a few of our ships simply vanish.

"Ordinarily the dragons don't trouble us directly — for years we've paid an annual tribute to keep them from interfering with our fleets passing through their waters. Greedy creatures, but gold buys their cooperation easily enough, and they've no particular taste for men, whom they find rather unappetizing — hobgoblins and sahaugin suit them far better, more like sardines to them, comparatively. But I believe Cain Toli is directing them against us specifically now, or at least that's what I've pieced together from conversations with Lord Phranck and others familiar with such matters.

"I think Fairwind something of a witless fool in all this — he doesn't grasp the true depth of the game being played, or how dangerous it truly is. But his meddling cannot simply be ignored."

"Absolutely not," Gouge agreed.

"Should we stop here for now?"

Gouge set his empty glass aside and rose. "Yes."

# Chapter Thirty-Six — What Merrick Told Jamis

Merrick came in after him, an empty glass already waiting on the table before the chair he was given — Jamis picked it up himself, set it aside, before Merrick had even settled.

"Ah, Merrick," Jamis said. "Excuse the mess. So — what happened over there?"

"A great deal to report," Merrick said, and found himself momentarily at a loss for where to begin. "Overwhelming, honestly. The sahaugin have returned in force — we watched them overrun an entire ship from the tower. Once we made port on the south side, we found sharks in the water, and tracks I didn't recognize on the shore, which left me uneasy from the start. When we moved to take the south tower, hobgoblins ambushed us — we lost men left and right, but held it in the end, with Gouge, Folsom, and Tyrus's help.

"After that we pushed for the Helm itself, made it there safely, found Jude and Paul Rivera along the way. From that point I made it my charge not to let Paul out of my sight, as you'd asked, and to keep Gouge in line and following orders." He recounted the rest in careful order — the sneak through the hobgoblins in the keep, the search for survivors in the barracks, the fight, and at last Fairwind himself, found tied up, accused by Paul of dealing with the Toli.

"So you didn't directly witness this business between Fairwind and the Toli?" Jamis asked.

"No — only Paul's account. He sent his little creature in ahead."

"The homunculus, is that what he called it?"

"Yes. No witness to actual words spoken, beyond what happened once I entered the room myself — he acted very defensively, he and Paul went back and forth. He did seem guilty, especially once we'd taken him prisoner and he fought his way through the keep against us, on a very narrow bridge. I watched him attack Folsom, push him back, then trip and shove Paul into the water during a scuffle with Gouge, and end in the water himself. He didn't strike me as innocent, given his dealings with the Toli.

"We killed one Toli, took a second prisoner, and Fairwind himself had to be thrown into the water, for our own survival — so he's dead too, I believe. Saw what was left of him after, in a shark's belly."

"He 'had to be' thrown into the water?" Jamis asked, something careful in the repetition.

"Not by my hand — I was toward the back, Jude was up front. Once we were spotted on the bridge by a patrol, Jude reacted — anger, shock, I couldn't say which — and threw him in."

"But it was Jude who killed him."

"I don't know that throwing him in was what killed him, exactly, or if the shark below did the rest of the work. No idea whether the second prisoner got away either." Merrick pressed on to the barrels — the powder that killed the sahaugin outright the moment it touched the water, the four he'd brought back himself, one already opened for use along the crossing, and the twenty-one still waiting on the coast. "We found around twenty-five in total. I could only load four — had to build a raft from what materials the coastline offered, to get us back as quickly as possible. We were badly outnumbered, completely overwhelmed. I judged it best, per your orders, to see Paul to safety and return as fast as we could — staying longer would have left us sitting for whatever came next, more sahaugin, more hobgoblins, or another Toli ship."

"Commendable work," Jamis said, something like genuine warmth in it. "Everything I'd expect of you, and more. I don't think Fairwind's involvement should be spoken of just yet — I need to speak with Paul myself before making any accusation, and the matter with Prince Jeon will be delicate. He never believed Fairwind capable of this. Perhaps Paul can convince him otherwise. But if I were you, I'd avoid mentioning Fairwind's part in this for now, especially not to his men."

"Is there anything I need to worry about, going forward?" Merrick asked. "He knows my face, likely my name — could that trace back to you, or should I look to my own protection?"

Jamis considered him a moment. "You actually know Fairwind, don't you — met him at the Helm before, though never served directly under him. He knows who you are, and respects you, if it helps."

"That's good to know."

"I wouldn't worry overmuch — I don't think he'll move against you or us just now; it wouldn't serve a man pleading innocence. He knows you'll report to me, and to Prince Jeon, as your duty demands. What he's doing, as I see it, is angling for political leverage — not something you need fear personally. You've done your part, reporting what happened, his involvement included. This is a matter between princes now. Whatever Paul wishes to accuse him of is his own business. Say nothing of it — especially not to other soldiers, or to Fairwind's own men — and you'll be fine."

"Absolutely. As for the powder — I didn't tell the corsair crews who picked us up what it actually was. I may have given them a sprinkle of it; I don't fully recall."

"Why does that matter — did you already know what this powder was, before today?"

"It's a state secret," Merrick said, "and needs to remain one."

"I'll tell you this much, in honesty," Jamis said. "We've only twenty barrels left, all told. If our enemies knew that number, they'd know exactly how thin our defenses have grown."

"Is this something my father knew of?"

"He was an admiral. Of course he did."

Merrick turned that over a moment. "I know it may not be my place, but if this is the only thing that's kept the sahaugin at bay this long, wouldn't Monmurg keep a reserve here as well, in case things truly went wrong? The number of sahaugin we saw was extremely overwhelming — once we sprinkled it in, dozens surfaced at once."

"We did keep storage here, in fact a good deal," Jamis said, "but it isn't being replenished. We receive no new shipments, and we've run through most of what we had. In truth, we were actively shipping barrels from Helm Island to here when this all occurred."

"What could the Toli possibly want with it, then — is it valuable enough to resell, or do they need it for their own territory?"

"I don't think they benefit from having it," Jamis said. "They benefit from our not having it. There are political negotiations underway right now, between the Duke of Berghof, the Toli, and Prince Jeon, and I believe this is a bargaining chip Berghof and the Toli intend to use. Not something you need concern yourself with — hopefully these maneuvers resolve soon enough that we needn't worry over it for long. I want to stress: tell no one of the powder, or of the Toli's part in taking it. I'm glad you've confirmed it was the Toli — but say nothing of Fairwind's involvement either."

"Understood," Merrick said. "There's still a great many hobgoblins holding the keep, and we only sprinkled enough to last a day or two in the water. Our men there are sitting ducks if support doesn't reach them."

"Would it help matters if I sent a couple of corsairs?"

"Yes."

"With your report, and the barrels you've brought, I believe we'll retake Helm Island within a day or two. It might even resolve through negotiated settlement with the hobgoblins — they're Berghofian, in truth. The Duke employs hobgoblin mercenaries fairly often, kept mostly in the mountains — not common knowledge, but he maintains small units, even small armies, of them. Why they were brought here, I couldn't say for certain, though my guess is they've no compunction about killing fellow principality men, which helps hide his hand in it — 'just hobgoblins,' nominally. A clumsy route for him, honestly, but better, from his angle, than risking actual Oeridian troops — and Toli rats would have proven far less effective; mostly slavers and scum. Hobgoblins are decent soldiers, and I suspect he needed professionals to pull this off."

"I won't say a word of the powder, or Fairwind's part in it," Merrick assured him.

"Do you still need to keep an eye on Paul Rivera?"

"That's a matter of trust yet to be seen," Jamis said. "I won't say one way or the other — but he's Jeon's nephew, and carries that privilege. From what I understand, he acted reasonably. A little hotheaded, stressful to manage, but no liability. He made the best judgments he could, perhaps a bit emotionally driven, but with guidance that will improve. He's a prince — what would you expect? No military man."

"Of course."

"I'll speak with Paul myself, in Prince Jeon's presence, and get to the bottom of it. Gouge was a good partner on this — worked well, followed orders. Have you spoken with him yet about any of it?"

"That was his glass on the table, I believe," Merrick said. "I could smell the red-eye on it."

"He did what he was paid to do."

"He was very helpful along the journey. Tyrus performed well too — but when it comes to trustworthiness, can you trust Gouge not to speak of this powder, or of working with you, to anyone?"

"There are more people who already know of this than just you and me," Jamis said, "but yes — I'll keep my own mouth shut, and I expect the best from you regarding Monmurg's interests."

"As for Gouge, or the bard Folsom — I don't know that they're the most discreet."

"Gouge will stay quiet; don't trouble yourself over him. I expect you to handle Tyrus and Folsom on this, and see they understand exactly how much it matters that they stay quiet."

"I can do that," Merrick said. "I did see Folsom — he had Fairwind under escort, in transit toward custody, essentially — and I witnessed some side conversation between the two of them. I don't know what passed, but shortly after, Fairwind broke free. I don't know what happened there exactly. Folsom had been a great help to me before that; I don't think he was at fault — but I don't know what Fairwind might have said to help engineer his own escape."

"Find out what Tyrus and Folsom actually know," Jamis said. "Discuss with Folsom what was said between him and Fairwind."

"I'll report back."

"I'll trust you on this — the same with Tyrus."

"It sounds as though he was never in Fairwind's presence for very long."

"No."

"All right."

"I don't think anyone fully trusts him — Tyrus, I mean."

"Oh," Merrick said. "Understood."

"Just caution them both to remain quiet, and see what you can draw from Folsom about that exchange, if anything passed between them at all worth knowing."

"I can do that. And one last question — I'm told Jude killed one of the Toli, or at least threw him into the water."

"Yes, that's true," Jamis said, and studied him a moment. "What's your evaluation of Jude?"

"A great ally, a great asset to the mission. Without him, I don't think we'd have gotten out alive — he got us through faster than I could have managed alone, with his levitation, his sword work, very intelligent. Hotheaded, certainly, and he seems to operate purely on his own judgment. The Toli were strong; it took three or four of us together to bring one down. When things were about to fall apart and we were badly exposed, he did what he felt he had to do to stay safe, and I understand that, even if it's not the path I'd have chosen myself."

"I want to warn you about those two," Jamis said. "Gouge and Jude. Both very dangerous. Gouge you can trust, as long as you never cross him — he'll stab an enemy in the back, but if you're an ally, he'll tell you he's about to stab you before he does it."

"I'll keep my distance," Merrick said, "and won't anger him — especially not when he's been drinking."

"As for Jude — he's entirely amoral. Incredibly effective, as you've said, but don't believe for a moment that he holds any loyalty to you. His only loyalty is to himself, and his own advancement. I control him through advancement and favors."

"Absolutely."

"But if he ever thinks he'll gain an advantage at your expense, he'll take it without hesitation."

"I hope you understand I'd never place trust above what you'd sanction first."

"I understand," Jamis said. "But what I'm telling you is, I don't fully trust either of them myself."

"I'll keep my guard up as best I can," Merrick said. "I trust Gouge to be Gouge. Jude's motivations I can't fully grasp." He hesitated, then asked it plainly: "Would you like me to report anything further, if I have dealings with him?"

"If you see anything that strikes you as treasonous, or any clandestine meeting, yes — I'd want to know. But I don't want you risking yourself over it."

"If you don't trust him, as you say, and you want Gouge and me protecting Paul as best we can — why was Jude allowed so close to him at all, if you don't mind my asking?"

"Prince Jeon requested it," Jamis said, "and I gave Jeon exactly the warnings I've given you. But Jeon takes a genuine interest in magic, and Jude's one of the more powerful wizards in the principalities — I think Jeon hopes he'll tutor Paul in the arcane arts. I've warned against it, for several reasons, but Jeon believes it Paul's best path forward, and that homunculus you mentioned suggests he might be right."

"Understood."

"But tell no one of this — it stays between us. I want you to know it, in case you find yourself in danger around any of these people."

"Understanding the relationships, and what they mean, while they don't know mine — that gives me an advantage," Merrick said.

"Absolutely." Jamis turned the conversation at last toward what lay ahead. "You won't be part of the group going back to retake the Helm. Your cousin Owen is back from Red Shore — smuggled some goods into the city, turned a profit on it, cider and other goods for the barracks among them. Go find him, down in the Harbor District, and ask what he's seen. I believe Red Shore is where the traders from your part of the sea are being funneled by the Duke of Gradsul."

"Understood." Merrick hesitated once more. "I do have one favor to ask — any chance I might get a small amount of the powder for my own use, if I'm out and about and run into sahaugin? Just enough to pull myself out of a bad spot."

"I'll consider it," Jamis said. "We can't spare much, but I'll see what I can do."

Merrick thanked him and rose to go, turning over in his own mind, as he crossed toward the door, the shape of his cousin Owen Black — a decent man, once Navy, drawn always to profit, smuggling goods into Keoland before the blockade and now smuggling food the other way, out of Rechor and into a starving Monmurg. Whether his loyalty ran deeper than his purse, Merrick couldn't yet say for certain — but Jamis, at least, seemed unconcerned. Screw Gradsul, the man had said, plainly enough, and Merrick found he couldn't disagree.

# Chapter Thirty-Seven — Cider at the Barracks

While Gouge and Merrick sat with Jamis, Tyrus and Folsom found themselves left to their own devices, and made straight for the bar.

The Marine barracks in the Harbor District were livelier than either of them had seen in weeks by the time they reached it — men gathered outside with cups in hand, more cheerful than the season had any right to allow.

"These are all my guys," Tyrus said, studying the crowd with something warm settling into his face. "Half my own unit's still out on the island, but these are still my people — men I know."

"A lot of them died, though," Folsom said, quieter.

"Not mine, specifically — but plenty of Marines did. Still people I know. Let's grab a drink before we hit the real bar properly."

"Agreed."

Tyrus clapped a hand on the shoulder of the nearest man, who turned with a mug already raised. "Folsom!" the Marine called out, delighted. "What's the occasion — Tyrus! Owen Black came back today, from Red Shore — smuggled food back for the city. Brought a couple of barrels of cider too, good stuff, not watered down like everything's been these past weeks. You can actually get properly drunk on this."

"I love it," Tyrus said.

"Go get yourselves one."

"Got to follow orders." Tyrus turned to Folsom as they crossed toward the bar. "Owen Black — I'd know the name?"

"A bit," Folsom said. "Used to be a Marine, not anymore — knows the waters extremely well. Kin of Merrick's, actually. Bit of a pirate — more of a smuggler, really. Too wild for the Marines in the end, though it wasn't a dishonorable parting. Bigger ambitions than the uniform allowed."

They found him at the counter, holding court in the middle of some tall tale, a splint bound around his left arm. He hadn't been seen in these parts since winter, and had been keeping himself scarce for reasons neither of them knew — but he was his usual self now, gregarious where Merrick ran serious, quick with a smile where his cousin ran duty-bound. He caught sight of them approaching and broke into a grin.

"Owen!" Tyrus called.

Owen turned, drink in hand, eyes sweeping the two of them. "Oh, awesome! How are you, lad — looking good. Who's this giant beside you?" He punched Tyrus lightly on the arm. "I understand you're responsible for all this."

"I am responsible for it," Tyrus said, grinning.

"Going to be great, especially in these dire times."

"I owe you, for sure — not a decent drink to be had in this district otherwise."

"Not for me either," Owen admitted, "but appreciated nonetheless. How'd you get past the patrols coming in?" He waved off the question before either of them could answer it. "To be honest, I didn't even know we were having trouble with them out there."

"Where have you been?" Tyrus asked.

"A little here, a little there — not a good idea to ask me that directly." He softened it with a grin. "Not a big secret, though — Red Shore's where all the ships are headed now."

"The one blockaded by the Duke of Gradsul?"

"That's the one. A mess in that harbor — you've never been, either of you?"

Neither had. Owen leaned in, warming to the telling. "It's a whaling town — docks fit for fishing boats, never built to handle fifty merchant ships with no dockmaster who knows his own business, no navigator worth the name, no proper lanes. Total chaos. Merchant ships languishing at anchor, merchants grumbling because they can't unload a thing. I sail in with my little cutter, buy up what I can straight from desperate men — they give me fine prices just to move the product — load up, and come home. I don't even double the price for you boys, just a fifty-percent markup. In the open market I do a good deal better. Food, oil, things this town actually needs — doing my small part, and making decent coin doing it."

"Worth it either way," Tyrus said.

"Money's not bad, no."

"Interesting way to make a living right now."

"There's opportunity in it, Tyrus, if you're looking."

"I'm always looking for ways to help Monmurg through this."

"I thought you were Marine golden-boy material," Owen said, something teasing in it. "Wouldn't want to run with a smuggler, would you?"

"It's not about running with a smuggler," Tyrus said. "It's about helping Monmurg through the blockade. That's the whole of it, for me."

"Fair enough — I can respect that." Owen glanced past him, scanning the room. "Where's Merrick?"

"Who knows — brooding, off somewhere, probably hiding in a corner."

"Honestly, I was looking for him myself — he knows these waters as well as anyone, maybe better than I do. Only other man besides me who can slip the Red Shore patrols and make the trades there."

"You'll be in town a few days?"

"A few, before I head back."

"We'll see him — maybe all get together, figure out what he wants to do."

"That'd be excellent." Owen's eyes narrowed, some sudden calculation crossing his face. "You're not talking to any authorities about any of this, are you?"

"I like my ale too much for that."

"There you go." Owen leaned closer, dropping his voice a fraction. "But there might be real opportunity here — bringing more supplies into Monmurg. That's what I'd actually be interested in. The city's dying out here."

"It does seem dire."

"Very dire. If we could bring in more, that's worth pursuing." Owen paused, weighing something. "I heard about Port Torvin."

"That it was burned to the ground," Tyrus said. "Big part of why we're in such straits — most of our supply ran through there. Some call it the work of the 'Toli Rat,' or the 'Scarlet Rat.'"

"I've heard that rumor too."

"Any truth to it, you think?"

"Hard to say with rumors. But I actually know the Scarlet Rat. Met him myself." Owen's grin turned wry. "He's a fraud, is what he is. Between the three of us — about two months back, I was in Saltmarsh, smuggling weapons in and out. Had dealings with a certain Cain Toli on a job he was running, scouting to make sure no patrols were about. There was a sailor under him then, a gangly Toli lad, barely more than a cabin boy. Lo and behold, a couple weeks later, I run into that same rat right here in Monmurg, not three doors down from where we're sitting — and he attacks me. Recognized my face.

"Turns out he's tied to a wizard called Lord Phranck, connected to a noble of Keoland — some local appointee in Saltmarsh, a military commander they call the 'power' of Baywen. I'd had dealings with Baywen myself before." He raised his splinted arm. "See this? He's the one who broke it. Captured me, questioned me in the Appleyard shell-keep."

"You're not pulling my leg?"

"That's the strange part of it — there were bugbears down there too, captives, and a couple other prisoners, some of Toli's crew or connected to him. We broke out together, got the better of one of Baywen's guard dogs — some crazed dwarf out of the mountains — and got clear. Found a skiff and made for open water."

"Lucky turn."

"It was. Ran the coast down to Saltmarsh, then on to Seton — picked up a couple old friends who helped me back to Monmurg, and I found a new ship. Had to make coin fast to pay it off, and Red Shore's the place for that right now, since that's where the trade's all sitting, going nowhere. Nobody buying, nobody able to unload — if you've a ship and the will to go get what you need, you can find nearly anything there."

Tyrus studied the map in his own mind's eye a moment. "So Saltmarsh sits up here, Red Shore there?"

"Right about there — that's why everyone's funneling through, thanks to the blockade."

"Makes sense. Real opportunity in it. Might use more supplies for the Marines, or leave the Marines outright and work for you, make real coin."

"You too," Owen said. "Helping the city helps us. What was Monmurg built on, really, but smuggling, piracy, profit. Helping her is helping ourselves. Aren't we her children too? We feel a duty to see her endure — this is where we make our coin, free trade and commerce, as it were."

"I have to let Merrick know what's happening at the Helm," Tyrus said.

"I've heard rumors it's in jeopardy — still is, from what I hear," Owen said, watching him closely now. "You're not telling me everything, are you. I know you were there — heard your company shipped out, and you came back without most of them."

"There was quite a situation," Tyrus admitted. "We nearly didn't make it. Escaped on a homemade raft after the place got overrun."

"You haven't seen sharks in the water lately, have you?"

"Actually, yes."

"So did I, coming into the bay today — surprised me, honestly. They attacked us, but not enough of them to overwhelm us. My men handled themselves well with a blade — lost one or two, but made it back."

"I heard it was worse at the Helm," Tyrus said. "A whole ship lost, from what I understand."

Owen let that sit a moment before pressing on with his own questions — how many ships he'd seen heading out since he arrived, whether it was the hobgoblins that got them, how long since Tyrus had last seen hobgoblins active at all. "I'll tell you this," he said, when Tyrus had no ready answer, "hobgoblin activity's been increasing off the coast near Salinmoor, around a small island they call Turtle Island — some call it Wizard Island. I've sailed through there myself. Seems that's where they're regrouping, near the true marshland, close by a small town called Appleyard."

"Any idea why?"

"None."

They talked the politics of it a while longer — whether the Admiralty already knew, whether one good naval victory would break the whole blockade, whether it was princely self-interest rather than incompetence keeping the fleet in dock. "Winning one battle would do wonders," Owen said. "Why our corsairs haven't gone out and torn the Keoland navy apart, I have no idea. There aren't even that many warships at Red Shore — three, maybe, plus two at Seton and one in Saltmarsh. I've seen them myself."

"We could take the whole armada with a coordinated strike."

"Easily. Burn Red Shore to the ground with the right force — wouldn't take more than a fraction of our fleet, and that city would be decimated. Plenty of captains stationed there don't even want to be. Why it hasn't happened, I couldn't say."

"Some princely politics behind it, likely."

"That's for sure."

"Which prince, though?"

"Maybe all of them."

"You think they'd want to look this foolish to their own people?"

"I think whatever lines their pockets matters more to them than how they look," Owen said, and grinned when Tyrus laughed despite himself.

Owen turned then to Folsom, who'd been quiet through the whole exchange. "But, Folsom — you got a song for us?"

"I do, in fact," Folsom said, and looked about for something to stand on.

Someone pointed him toward the counter, and he climbed up onto it with a showman's flourish, calling out over the crowd. "Ladies and gentlemen — for your entertainment, I bring you... Folsom!"

The room roared its approval, cups raised. Folsom grinned down at them, riding the warmth of it. "Tyrus, I won't be needing an announcer for once — everybody's already happy! Well — before I get into the tale of our recent adventure, let's give it up for Owen, for this grand festivity we're all enjoying tonight!"

Cheers rose again, and Owen lifted his cup in acknowledgment.

"But I do, in fact, have a song for you all," Folsom went on, "about our recent adventure." He drew himself up, weighing which of several songs he'd composed on the crossing home might best suit a room full of off-duty men still raw from three hundred lost comrades — and chose, in the end, not the gentler, safer one, but the sharper.

He called it "The Brewing Storm," and began.

*Oh, Monmurg, you're a jewel on display,
but your leaders have led you astray.
Princes and powers in their gilded halls
can't see the writing on your walls.
They could grasp the news, if they'd only try,
but it's your own leaders who are truly blind.*

*Oh, princes of the sea, what have you done?
Your squabbles and schemes have blocked out the sun.
While you bicker and fight, the city's in plight,
and the Duke of Gradsul laughs while you fight.*

*The navy's strong, or so they say,
but storms and whispers have led you astray.
The enemy's fleet, the weaker and slow,
has somehow managed to steal the show.
Is it magic, or treachery — who can tell?
But incompetence might be the cause as well.*

*For Torvin in ashes, the food's running low,
you sit in your castles, putting on a show.
The Prince of Port Toli, with his ships delayed,
plays his own game while the city's betrayed.
Internal strife, a festering sore,
while Monmurg starves, you all plan for more.
The traders are stuck, the markets are bare,
and the people of Monmurg are in despair.
The Duke of Berghof, with his meager supply,
leaves you to wonder — is this goodbye?
Your allies are distant, your enemies near,
and you do nothing but stroke your own fear.*

*Oh, princes of the sea, what have you done?
Your squabbles and schemes have blocked out the sun.
While you bicker and fight, the city's in plight,
and the Duke of Gradsul laughs while you fight too.*

*So here's to the leaders, the princes and powers,
who've turned Monmurg's fortune to scars.
While you play your games, the city's in pain,
and the Duke of Gradsul wins again.
Monmurg, your fate's in their hands,
but it's their own folly that tightens the bands.*

The words themselves were sharp and well-turned, the product of real craft laid down over long hours of the crossing home — but the delivery came out rough despite it, his voice cracking once on the high note of the second verse, and the room, once his final line faded, did not roar back the way it had for his introduction.

It went, instead, very quiet.

The crowd was patriotic to a man, off-duty Marines who'd just watched their fellows die by the hundreds, and a song naming their own princes' folly as the true author of the city's suffering did not sit well in a room like this one — not tonight, not from a stranger's mouth, however true the words might ring.

Gouge arrived at the barracks door in that same silence, took one look at the room's mood, and crossed the floor without hurry, setting down his own drink along the way. He drew his dagger halfway from its sheath, let it catch the lamplight a moment, and slid it home again — a small, deliberate gesture, meant to be seen.

"These words aren't welcome here," he said to Folsom, low but carrying, and took him firmly by the arm. "Sit yourself down." Then, louder, to the room at large: "He's had a bit too much." And with that, he hauled Folsom bodily out of the barracks and into the evening air, leaving the crowd to mutter and slowly return to its cups.

Tyrus lingered a moment longer, awkward in the sudden quiet. "I'll come down in a second," he told Owen, reaching for his own cup. "Next round's on me."

Owen raised his glass in thanks, and Tyrus followed the others out.

# Chapter Thirty-Eight — The Gift in the Alley

Gouge walked Folsom into the nearest alley and sat him down against the wall, the noise of the barracks fading behind them.

"You know what you were trying to do back there?" Gouge asked. "Did you even like the song?"

"Loved it, actually," Folsom said, still riding some of the high of the performance despite everything.

"I saw it wasn't landing — reading the room, I took it on myself to get your backside out of there the best way I knew how. Everyone was stunned, staring at you, so I made a bigger scene of it — pulled the blade, grabbed you, hauled you out. Gave Tyrus a look too, since they'd have known he was with you, told him to sit down — so it's understood this is being handled, in case there was bad blood over it. Nearly blew up in there. This was me getting you clear before it got worse."

"And here I thought I was making a life for myself," Folsom said, something rueful in it.

"You do have a bit of a preconceived notion of who I am, or what I am — all of you do. Remember when I shouted out and you thought I was the villain of the piece? That's Jude, not me. I follow the law. You've no reason to fear me, unless you cross somebody I'm loyal to. That's the only thing worth fearing from me."

"Fair enough," Folsom said. "I do appreciate you getting me out of there."

"I do — and I want the others here for what comes next." Gouge sent word for Tyrus, who arrived a few minutes later to find the two of them in the alley's shadow, Gouge's arm draped easy over Folsom's shoulder, talking to him in a tone that gave no clear sign of whether this was a good turn or a bad one.

Tyrus threw up his fists in mock readiness at the sight of them. "Uh-huh. This is the part where I get stabbed."

"Great song, Tyrus," Gouge said, unbothered, "but that room was crap for it, brother. I read it, pulled him out — told them it was all a joke, and that a round of drinks cures more fear than anything else does. I think it will."

"You two don't have business in that barracks tonight, do you?"

"No," Tyrus said. "Let's meet over at the bar instead."

"Go grab Merrick for me — I want a word with him. We'll wait here, then clear these two out after what just happened, and go have a couple ourselves."

Tyrus went, and returned a few minutes later with Merrick in tow, catching him just as he stepped out through the barracks door, shaking sand from his boots.

Gouge drew the two of them aside without further preamble and produced, from somewhere beneath his cloak, a cloak of his own — worn, plain, and set with a small green stone at the collar.

"It's a gem," Gouge said, holding it out to Folsom. "I don't use it as it ought to be used. But this thing's probably going to make your songs just a little bit better."

"Understood," Folsom said, accepting it with real care.

"But it comes with a price, if you take it. If we're together, in a fight, and things are happening, and you're about to play your tunes or do whatever you mean to do to help somebody — I want you to look at me first, before you decide. I'm fair, and you can trust that, whether you believe me on it or not. But when you're going to throw something out to help another man, look my way. If I give you a nod like this" — he tipped his chin — "that means I don't need it, and it's all on you where it goes. But if I look at you like this" — his eyes went hard, deliberate — "then whatever you're doing, you'd better be helping me. Understood?"

"More than okay with that," Folsom said.

"I owe you one, then. Or a few."

"We'll see," Folsom said, something lighter creeping back into his voice. "Hopefully not — or maybe just some red-eye, if it comes to it."

"Forges it is, then," Gouge said, and something like a grin broke across his face at last. "All right. Good kid."

# Chapter Thirty-Nine — What Merrick Found at the Bar

Merrick had come into the barracks just in time to catch the tail end of the disaster, and stood a long moment in genuine shock at what he'd walked into.

His first thought, once words came back to him, was plain frustration — this was precisely what Folsom had been told to avoid. When Gouge hauled the bard bodily out through the door, Merrick half expected to find him stabbed and left in some alley by the time he caught up.

Instead he found Owen Black leaning against the bar beside Tyrus, laughing and drinking as though nothing at all had happened.

"What happened with Folsom?" Owen asked, when he caught sight of Tyrus's face. "Must have been rough out there."

"Oh — it's a farce," Tyrus said. "Meant to be one, anyway. A joke."

"He always try to crack jokes like that?"

"I keep telling him it's not working."

"You should," Owen said, grinning. "Marines don't much care for jokes, especially about themselves. You know what they do like? Beer." He raised his cup as if to make the point for him.

Tyrus set gold on the counter. "Come here, all of you — looks like I came in at the right time."

"Was that for my benefit?" Merrick asked, arriving at his side. "Put on the moment I walked in?"

"No, I don't know," Tyrus said. "You made it here at all — that's something."

Merrick shook his head slowly. "I'll see you later. Good to see you, Owen." He turned toward the door, still shaken. "I've got to report to my captain. I don't know what—" He didn't finish the thought, only left, the door swinging shut behind him.

Tyrus lingered with Owen a while longer, nursing his own cup. "That was quite a show," he said at last.

"Strange circumstances, absolutely," Owen agreed. "Good to see you, though — hope your travels went well."

"Good to see you in one piece too. How's it been?"

"Good — busy. Just got back from Red Shore." Owen leaned in, warming again to the subject. "You wouldn't believe it — fifty merchant ships there, and they can't unload a one of them, no docks fit for the purpose."

"Sounds like it — good for your profits, though."

"To my profit, absolutely." Owen studied him a moment, something shifting in his tone. "Talk to me about the sahaugin in the bay — did some of them attack you out there?"

"Have you seen them all the way out there yourself?" Tyrus asked.

"Five miles from the city, one mile out from Red Shore — they hit us there. Some of my men had run into them before too, which is strange; we hadn't seen them in years. They're coming back."

"Something's going on, that's certain," Tyrus said. "I've seen them on the coast more lately too, up near Red Shore. Any of your men hurt?"

"One or two died. Bad encounter." Owen shrugged it off, though something in his eyes stayed troubled a moment longer than the gesture suggested. "You know I'm almost as good a pilot as you are — managed to avoid the patrols on the way in."

"Your navigation's probably the best there is."

"Patrols have been increasing lately, from what I can tell. I think the only other person who can make it through them to Red Shore besides me is you — we both know it."

"What would you say to going into business," Tyrus asked, "a private fleet, something outside the Marines? The city needs you."

"True enough, and the politics around here are getting old." Owen's mouth twisted. "According to your friend over there, the princes are all fools."

"You know I'm not going to give you a satisfying answer to that."

"Fair. You know I'm just going to have a drink and catch up with my cousin, then go get him out of trouble after that performance." Owen glanced toward the door Gouge had dragged Folsom through. "Think you could get him on my crew? Gouge, I mean, for the right coin, or the right cut."

"You'd hire Gouge?"

"He'd be very useful out there — I'd been thinking of it myself. Know him by reputation, certainly. In fact, about a week ago I picked up a compatriot of his — an elf — at Red Shore, and dropped him at a small fishing town on the north coast. Tell him to get in touch with me. If we hire on a few of these mercenaries, we could do well for ourselves. Dangerous work, but—"

"What are you smuggling nowadays?"

"Food's the most important thing right now, as you'd expect — and then there's the sahaugin to worry over too, if they catch the scent of what you're carrying. It's dangerous."

"Very dangerous."

"Absolutely." Owen studied him a long moment. "Well — you've got me intrigued, for sure, and you know I always have your best interest in mind." He called over toward the door. "Now go tell that boy that's what I'm here for." Then, quieter, back to Tyrus: "I'm worried about Gouge — want to make sure he doesn't do anything rash."

"Absolutely," Tyrus said.

"Propose it to him, though, and I'll get you another boat. We can do this right, and make a great deal of money."

"That sounds good," Tyrus said. "Thank you."

# Chapter Forty — The Confession at Ferd's

Merrick and Tyrus found Gouge and Folsom sitting together a while later, Folsom looking a shade sheepish, as they returned to compare notes.

"So — what did you learn?" Tyrus asked. "Do we need to go back to the island?"

"Before any of that," Gouge said, "I was brought here specifically to keep this one's mouth shut."

"What did I do?" Folsom protested. "I just walked through the door—"

"You were spilling the most horrendous nonsense I've ever heard."

"Great voice, though."

"Great voice, I'll give you that — but do you not know how to read a room?"

"Can't help it."

"Clearly not. You did great helping with the ship, building the raft — good hand to have. But right now, I don't know how I feel about you." Gouge turned businesslike, laying out the terms plainly. "When it comes to what we saw and learned on Helm Island, you stay completely silent. There were no barrels. There were no political revelations. As far as anyone else needs to know, I don't even know if you got off the boat."

"We did get a boat," Folsom said. "I didn't actually spill anything specific, if you think about it."

"I'm only saying there's foul play afloat here, and you need to watch what comes out of your mouth."

"Maybe run your lyrics by an advisor next time," Tyrus offered. "That's all I'll say."

"Don't run them by me," Gouge said. "You hit the nail on the head with that one, kid — just at the wrong moment."

"I did some decent damage control after you left," Tyrus said. "Didn't seem that stunned by the end of it."

"Maybe just don't go back to that barracks for a while — would be a kindness to yourself. Marines are simple creatures; feed them enough drink and they forget things fast."

"You should know by now," Folsom said, some quiet steel in it. "Yes, I'm privy to all this. My mouth stays shut because I'm smarter than you're giving me credit for. This isn't a conversation you need to have with me — I'm not policing what you do."

"So — the barrels, everything we found out there," Gouge said, turning to the group at large.

"I reported what I needed to," Folsom said. "I'm done with it."

"Nobody else heard anything from us about any of that," Tyrus added. "We said we got stranded, made a raft, sailed back on our own, separate from the group."

"That's the cover story?" Gouge asked.

"Supply run, ship wrecked at the docks, had to improvise a way back."

"That's all anyone else knows?"

"That's all we gave Owen. Didn't tell him the rest — just hoped the protective spell gets restored."

Merrick, arriving in time to catch the tail of it, spoke up. "I did mention to Owen, just so you know—"

"I don't want you saying anything to Owen," Gouge cut in, sharp.

"We just said he—"

"What in the world, Owen?"

"He's cool," Merrick said. "Owen wants to give us a chance. My cousin, Owen."

"Okay."

"Did he ask you about the job?"

"That's what I overheard," Tyrus said. "I was offered work by Owen. He asked me to talk to you, Gouge, about it."

"From Owen?"

"From Owen."

"Does Owen know who I am?"

"No," Merrick said. "I think he only really knows Tyrus, a bit."

"What's his position in all this?"

"He's been smuggling for Monmurg's benefit these last few years — real motivation's coin, sure, but done in a way that helps our people. Food, mostly, right now, Red Shore to Monmurg."

Gouge traced the route in his own head. "And that's where we already were."

"Right."

"Did he tell you how he hurt his hand?"

"Said he was attacked by hobgoblins on his way back."

"That's it?"

"Well — the actual reason is, he was kidnapped."

Gouge's eyes sharpened. "By the wizard? The Scarlet Rat?"

"No — he was attacked by the Scarlet Rat separately, at another point." Merrick worked to get the sequence straight in his own head. "He was taken captive in Saltmarsh, then made his way to Seton, I believe."

"Sounds like he's smuggling to help us," Gouge said, "and left out the full story so as not to invite military attention."

"That tracks," Merrick agreed. "Smuggling to keep the city alive, basically."

"But who — my main concern is why my name comes up in any of this."

"I didn't bring it up," Merrick said. "He asked who you were, since you're my cousin's contact, I suppose."

"How does he know who I am?"

"No idea. Seems you've got a bit of a reputation."

"Wouldn't make my head any bigger," Gouge said, "but I'd rather know. If you talk to him again, find out why he knows me specifically. That said — I do support what he's doing. If it brings more food and supplies into the city while we sort the bigger picture, I'm fully behind it. Might have to report this properly, though — I don't like a man knowing who I am while I know nothing of him. Not saying it's a problem. Just something I want clarity on."

"Good to know," Tyrus said.

"We hear you," Merrick said.

"What I need to figure out," Tyrus said, "is whether I'm actually going with this man — that means taking leave from the Marines to do it. Are we really taking orders from a smuggler now?"

"I'm not saying take orders from anybody," Gouge said. "But right now, I take certain orders, from certain people."

"You mean I'd need to take a leave of absence?"

"Exactly that."

"I'm not doing this alone, that's for certain."

"Understandable. Might be worth talking to Jamis about it directly — he could let you take a crew, run this as reconnaissance. Getting eyes on Red Shore's valuable on its own; he's used people this way before, agents posing as smugglers, that sort of arrangement."

"You could probably work it so Tyrus takes leave," Merrick offered, "brings Folsom along for a few days — might not be such a bad idea."

"Don't worry about what the brass thinks," Gouge told Tyrus. "I think it'd genuinely do all of us good to get out of here a few days. Especially you." He paused, weighing something else. "But — what did the two of you actually learn from your briefing with Jamis? Anything you can share?"

"I told him everything we saw," Tyrus said.

"I'm curious about his reaction."

"Have you met with Jamis yet?" Tyrus asked.

"No — only from a distance. Has he?"

"Of course."

"Was he shocked by any of it?"

"Hard to say — he's difficult to read. There's clearly more work to be done here, though, politics and military matters both."

"I think it might be in everyone's best interest to take this job with Owen," Gouge said. "The military angle can stay quiet — you needn't worry about the leave of absence complicating things. Could work out in our favor long term, and be good for the city besides."

"Do I go through my captain," Tyrus asked, "or straight to Jamis?"

"Straight to Jamis, and sort it with him directly."

"All right." Tyrus set his cup down, and his voice dropped into something graver. "But before we go any further — I need to know what you and your little friend here actually learned from Fairwind."

"As a matter of fact," Gouge said, "I was about to bring that up myself. Convenient timing."

"You're treading on real thin ice with me right now."

"I hear you — not trying to step on anyone's toes."

"Continue, then."

"So — Fairwind." Gouge set his own drink aside and folded his hands together. "I'll tell you exactly what he told me, word for word as best I can, and I want to be clear I'm neutral on whether any of it's actually true."

"Was he under a charm at the time?"

"There was a charm involved, yeah."

"Could that make him say things that aren't true?"

"A charm works more like talking to a trusted friend," Gouge said. "Your friend can still lie to you, but there's a good chance he told the truth as he understood it. None of us actually know Fairwind's honesty at bottom — lawful, neutral, chaotic, who's to say — he could still slant things to benefit himself regardless."

"So we shouldn't dismiss it outright. It might be true."

"He was talking about — let me get the names straight. There's Prince Jeon, and there's Lord Jamis."

"Right."

"Jeon's Paul's uncle."

"Right, Paul."

"Lord Jamis was apparently the subject of a good deal of what Fairwind was going on about — his alignments, his ties to everything that's been happening." Gouge drew a slow breath. "Everyone knows Lady Jamis, of course. Her brother, according to Fairwind, is Zafar Azane — who works for the Duke of Gradsul, and runs the Duke's Office of Forbidden Magic."

"Not a great look," Tyrus said slowly, "given everything happening right now."

"The Duke of Gradsul is the one blockading Monmurg."

"Let me get this straight." Tyrus set it down carefully in his own mind, piece by piece. "The Duke of Gradsul's the traditional enemy of the sea principalities — one of his ancestors, Lucian the Fourth, attacked us and died at the Siege of Westkeep. The Duke's own line periodically produces the King of Keoland, and whenever it does, Keoland tends to go to war with us. So his bloodline's essentially our oldest enemy."

"That's the shape of it."

"So who's in league with the Duke right now, specifically?"

"His brother-in-law," Gouge said. "Lady Jamis's brother, Zafar Azane."

"Hold on." Tyrus set his cup down entirely now, all levity gone from his face. "Lord Jamis's own wife's brother is Zafar Azane, who runs the Duke's Office of Forbidden Magic?"

"That's right."

"That's... not great."

"Works directly for the Duke, runs that office, could even sit close to the Duke's own inner circle, for all we know."

"So — given all this — are your suspicions shifting?" Tyrus asked. "From Paul and Prince Jeon, down to Lord Jamis himself?"

"Let's be honest — I don't think any of us really know what's going on for certain."

"That's fair."

"But if Jamis asks either of us anything directly, I think we say nothing of this particular thread."

"What you're telling me right now," Tyrus said, low and careful, "if there's real suspicion pointed at Jamis — you're treading on thin ice with me too. My loyalty runs through him, but my true loyalty is to Monmurg itself. I'm not saying Jamis is complicit, or pulling strings behind everything — I'm saying what Fairwind described sounds like a web of connections that all lead back toward him, even unintentionally."

"Lord Jamis, Lady Jamis," Gouge said, "and we all know she works some influence behind the scenes."

"Quite the connection. Or just coincidence?"

"I don't believe in pure coincidence with something like this."

"Jamis told me himself he doesn't keep close friends," Tyrus said. "So I'm not bringing any of this to him directly."

"I think the best course is taking the job with Owen," Gouge said. "I can tell Jamis we're running reconnaissance — easy enough. Owen needn't know we're doing it for the crown at all; let him believe we've stepped away from duty for our own reasons. Safest thing for him to think."

"Tired of the politics, whatever shape it takes. Just looking out for ourselves and the city."

"Exactly. Best interest going forward — and the fastest, cleanest way to handle this." Gouge held Tyrus's eye a moment. "Like I said — especially with Jamis — I don't think you should bring any of this up to him directly."

"This is a little shocking to me, honestly, but—"

"Obviously, if I've got to play the part of a man keeping an eye on you all for him, that's the part I'll play. But I'm not going to distrust what you're doing just because of what you've told me. And I'll take it with a grain of salt regardless — this is coming from the same man who tied himself up to fake being a hostage. Nobody outside this circle knows any of it — that's the whole of the trust we're placing in Paul's account."

"Paul?"

"He was pretty heated about it — young, genuine emotion, which inclines me to believe him, unless he's that fine an actor."

"I don't think Paul's in on anything."

"Jamis could be playable too, in all this," Gouge said. "He sent me to talk to Owen discreetly, to learn more of Red Shore."

"Sounds like Owen's heading back there anyway."

"He is — so our going along might reveal exactly why Jamis wanted that information in the first place."

"That's where everything under blockade is actually ending up."

"Right. And if, in the meantime, we help get supplies flowing to the people here, that eases tensions too. Feels like a two-birds situation."

"Agreed," Tyrus said. "And if you talk to Jamis about any of this — about me being involved going forward—"

"I won't mention you specifically. As far as he knows, he's cut me loose on this end — did my job, got paid, had a drink in his office, walked out. That's the last contact I've had."

"I want to stay on his good side. Have a drink for me when you see him next."

"For the most part I'm off the hook at this point, unless you need me pulled back in — which I don't think you should, unless it's necessary."

"I think you'd be a real asset on a journey with Owen," Tyrus said. "He did ask that I run this job by you specifically, try to bring you in."

"I'd like a meeting with him directly, then."

"I can arrange that."

"I understand," Gouge said. "I just don't care for people knowing about me without my consent." He turned to Folsom. "You — count in. You're coming on this job too."

"Yes?" Folsom said, brightening.

"You need to get your backside out of Monmurg for a while, and I need a break from that mouth of yours."

"I was bringing him along anyway," Tyrus said.

"Let's go, then — all of us."

"Wherever Tyrus goes, I go," Folsom said.

"Well, Tyrus — I trust you'll have a good time on this one," Gouge said, something almost fond beneath the dryness.

"Oh, yeah."

"Maybe rein him in a little, though. No booze for him a while."

"We'll see how that goes."

"Good thing Marines are simple-minded," Gouge said. "You really didn't say much that'll stick, if you think about it." He rose, brushing off his coat. "I'll leave the two of you here — if you want to talk with Owen, he's probably still at his bar."

"If you want, I can come along," Tyrus offered.

"No problem with that."

"I think you two should head out instead," Tyrus said to Merrick and Folsom. "He's not going back into that barracks."

"Definitely not," Folsom agreed.

They made instead for a place Gouge knew, deeper into the foreign district than any of them had ventured before — a melting pot of cultures and travelers from every corner of the region, foreign traders and merchants renting rooms, second only to the Great Market in its cosmopolitan mix, but also home to the locals of Monmurg who couldn't make ends meet any other way, through begging, or theft, or worse.

"This is where I grew up," Gouge said, something wry in it, as they crossed deeper into the district's rougher corners, past an alley marked by a crude sign reading *Blood Alley*, and another beside it.

"What's that one called?" Tyrus asked.

"Murder Alley."

"Subtle."

The bar itself sat across from both, its clientele a scarred and battered mix of mercenaries from every corner of the world — dwarves with old wounds, down-and-out pirates from Port Toli. Tyrus mimed a dainty sip as they crossed the threshold. "Very refined in here."

Gouge only laughed and led them to the man behind the counter — Ferdin himself, who looked the three of them over with a raised brow.

"What do we have here?"

"At least they smell better than Karmirg," Gouge said.

"True, that," Ferdin said. "He's been missing the last week or two — mumbled something before he left. You haven't seen him?"

"Fortunately and unfortunately, no." Gouge leaned in, dropping his voice. "Say nothing about us to the elf, if he comes around asking. Pour us four red-eyes, and just say 'Gouge sent them,' if anyone asks."

"I'll do it," Ferdin said, and set to pouring.

Some months later, when the tale of that night had made its rounds through the taverns and barracks of Monmurg more than once, it was Folsom's disastrous performance of "The Brewing Storm" that people remembered longest — not the fighting, not the barrels, but the song itself, and how a room full of patriotic Marines had grumbled and bristled at hearing their own princes named as authors of the city's suffering. As the weeks rolled on, though, and rumors of Fairwind's treachery at Helm Island spread, and the Sea Ghost was sighted again, and the Toli's hand in the whole affair became harder to deny, those same Marines began to realize that Folsom had seen something coming before any of them had. They grudgingly granted him the wisdom of what he'd told them.

There was a storm brewing, and it was not the Duke of Gradsul's doing alone — the jealousy and infighting of the sea princes fed the confusion, and the country's troubles came not only from its enemies, but from those it called friends. In time, the Marines who'd once wanted him thrown out of the barracks began to wish they could sit and talk with him, to learn what else he knew. And so began Folsom's career — and his infamy — as a bard whose name would carry rather further than that single rough night suggested it might.

# Chapter Forty-One — The Prince's Charge

Paul, meanwhile, had been led up a private stairway past several guards, into the palace that was as much his own home as it was Jeon's seat of power, and brought to his uncle's office. Jeon stood there already, bent over a map of the outer islands, Songmor Bay marked to the north, Jerly Bay to the south, and something drawn and troubled in the set of his shoulders that Paul had rarely seen in him before.

"Paul," Jeon said, without looking up at first. "Sit down, please."

Paul sat. "I heard there was some trouble on Helm Island."

"I've had reports the ship we sent you on was overwhelmed."

"Three hundred Marines on that ship," Paul said, the weight of it settling into his voice as he said it aloud for the first time. "All gone. Maybe a hundred made it off."

"And it was the sahaugin?"

"Yes — and hobgoblins had taken the island besides."

"Tell me more of that."

Paul drew a breath. "Fairwind is a traitor."

Jeon's eyes came up sharply from the map. "How do you know this?"

He was alone with his uncle, Paul realized as he glanced about the room — no Jamis present, no witness beyond the two of them, which told him plainly enough that Jeon wanted his account first, unshaded by anyone else's version of events. Paul laid it out in full: the landing at the southern beacon, the ship overrun by sahaugin behind them even as they fought ashore, the barracks and the Marines still holding there who knew nothing of what had become of their lord. The plan to scale the wall while a diversion drew every eye elsewhere. The inner sanctum, and what he'd seen and heard there through his creature's borrowed senses.

"You said Fairwind was a traitor," Jeon said again, when Paul had laid the ground of it.

"Here's where it gets interesting," Paul said. "His own garrison didn't know how the hobgoblins had gotten in, or what had become of him. They'd just been holed up, holding the line, under siege themselves. We linked with them, made our plan, and snuck around to where we figured he'd be."

"The inner sanctum."

"Yes. I scouted ahead — could see and hear him. Dealing."

"Dealing with whom?"

"The Toli."

Jeon's face went very still. "The Toli — there were Toli on the Helm, at that hour?"

"I saw them myself. We captured one."

"How do you know they were Toli?"

"I saw them," Paul said, "and we took one alive."

"Go on, then. You witnessed Lord Fairwind speaking with them, walking the same room, making deals. What were they discussing?"

"Removing the powder from the Helm," Paul said. "The substance that keeps the sahaugin away. The Toli said something like — 'We noticed you took most of it for yourself.' I think Fairwind was splitting off a share for himself, that was his angle. He'd already removed most of it from the island."

"And that's how the sahaugin were able to swarm it."

"Right. They said he had four barrels of the stuff. Once we'd taken him, I placed him under arrest — he made a great fuss, claimed innocence throughout. We took one Toli alive as well." Paul recounted the rest — the secret passage down through the cave system, the fight on the ledge, the dead Toli thrown into the water, Fairwind's own fall — pushed, or falling, in the same chaos that had nearly taken Paul with him. "Don't know what happened to him. Could be dead. Could be alive. We fought our way down, killed the hobgoblins there, built a raft, sailed clear. Took four barrels with us, enough to keep the sahaugin off for now."

"How many barrels were there, originally?"

"Twenty-five, by our count. We could only take four — the rest stayed behind."

"Who else witnessed Fairwind speaking with the Toli agents?"

"Unfortunately — nobody else. Just me."

Jeon studied him a long moment, something weighing behind his eyes. "So you're the only one with a direct, definitive account of this betrayal. Your companions — how much have you told them of it?"

"That he's a traitor, that he was dealing with the Toli. That's the whole of it — I didn't go into specifics. But they believed me over him."

"It doesn't entirely make sense," Jeon said slowly. "What he apparently told all of them was simply that he'd had his own men give up their arms and be held, cells or otherwise."

"We never found his Marines," Paul admitted. "But they'd basically let the hobgoblins walk right in."

"That doesn't sound very fair to him," Jeon said, "but it is your opinion that Fairwind allowed the hobgoblins to take the Helm."

"The hobgoblins were working with the Toli, we believe."

"Strange, even given the Duke of Berghof's known dealings with hobgoblin mercenaries — so there may be some involvement from him as well." Jeon's brow furrowed. "These hobgoblins — were they red-skinned?"

"I couldn't say — never seen one before."

"I have. They live in the Crystalmist Mountains, which the Duke's duchy backs against, so he has dealings with them. Easier to deal with than most humanoids, in truth — but disconcerting, that they'd be used here."

They went over the rest of it together — the signal from the southern beacon that had warned the fleet off, the strange ship glimpsed through the fog, the Sea Ghost and Cain Toli's supposed command of it. "No doubt that's where the hobgoblins came from," Jeon said, "or the likeliest explanation, at least — we'd seen ships moving back and forth to the caves before. That's where they were storing the powder?"

"Loading it, yes."

"I'll have Jamis send a flotilla to Helm Island," Jeon said, "and we'll retake the caves, recover whatever remains. The area's blockaded for now." He turned then to a gentler line of questioning — the men Paul had traveled with, Merrick and Gouge and Jude among them, whether any beyond that small circle knew of Fairwind's treachery.

"Almost all the surviving Marines in the barracks witnessed parts of it too," Paul admitted.

"How many were with you, specifically?"

"Five. Merrick, Gouge, Jude, Folsom, and Tyrus."

"They'll need debriefing too, quietly — I'll leave that to Jamis, though they answer to him regardless." Jeon considered a moment. "Merrick could be commended for this, I think. Honorably."

"What do you mean?"

"He's obviously loyal to me. Did you choose him?"

"Jamis recommended him."

"They're loyal — I don't think they'd cause trouble. What about Gouge? I worry about him."

"Gouge is fine," Paul said. "Makes quick decisions under pressure. Not much of a threat unless he's threatened himself. Professional, I'd say."

"And this Jude?"

"A good wizard. The job you gave him was to protect me, and he did that well enough. They're all very capable."

"Good." Jeon's tone shifted, settling into something more deliberate, more carefully weighed. "Now, Paul — there's something I want to speak to you about, given what you've told me of your own investigation into your father's history. I've been thinking on this a while. Take it as a suggestion for your own path forward, and how you might best serve your family in these difficult times.

"I've noticed you've taken to something like the ledger domain — spycraft, in a way — and I think that useful. But I knew your father, Grayson, when we were young men, and I was very impressed by his intelligence, and your mother's besides. Both brilliant people. He spoke often of the yellow substance, and how our state would only be truly free once we could produce it ourselves. Are you aware the compound was given to us by the Duke of Berghof, and that he's since stopped supplying it? We've been running low for decades. What remained on Helm Island, even before it was taken, would only have lasted another three years. We've been desperate for it, and now that it's gone, we've neither the time nor the means to produce it ourselves — we simply lack the knowledge. Your father recognized this two decades ago, and meant to change it. Unfortunately, the greatest alchemists all belong to Keoland—"

"Or the Duke of Berghof," Paul finished.

"Exactly. What I'm asking, Paul, is whether you'd dedicate yourself to solving this. I believe you're capable of it. Would you study the making of this substance — pursue it as your own quest, and find a way to produce it?"

Paul turned the question over, feeling the weight of what it would mean. "This would divert me from my present path entirely — I'd have to delve properly into the arcane arts. Is that what you're asking?"

"Most of Oeridian stock are reluctant to go there," Jeon said, "but I see now our neglect of the magical arts has brought us to this point. We need wizards of our own."

"We do."

"Which brings me to my second point. Do you feel Jude is someone you'd be willing to study under?"

"He's a good wizard," Paul said, "very powerful. He could teach me."

"Excellent — I've already asked Lord Jamis to propose the arrangement to him. Jude's a transactional man, from what I understand, but we have things he wants right now. If he could share his knowledge and teach you the arts, it would prove very advantageous to our family — and, honestly, having a new prince of Monmurg with a wizard's abilities would be advantageous in itself.

"So I'm charging you: look into the arcane arts, as your father and mother did before you. I'll give you full leeway in it — do as you wish, so long as it brings no harm to the city or our family's name. There are magics illegal in Keoland and in Berghof both — necromancy comes to mind first — but so long as no one else learns of it, I won't restrict your knowledge, or where you seek it. You'll have full use of the Royal Library, and whatever esoteric components you need, I'll help you acquire. The Great Market still draws wizards from around the world, even in this difficult year, and I expect we can find most of what you'll require.

"I'll have Jamis speak to Jude about tutoring you, and I'd like to see that begin within the fortnight. I'm charging the two of you together with unraveling this yellow powder — how to make it, where it comes from, everything we need to know. We have months before our supply runs out entirely. There may be other stores to look into — if Fairwind did indeed betray us, he likely holds a large amount of it somewhere on his own island. But I do not wish to go openly to war with him over this."

"Why?" Paul said. "He's a traitor."

"He's a prince of the principalities, with a large force of men and ships."

"So do we."

"True — but we're at war with Gradsul right now. Showing him our internal weakness, our division, would be disastrous."

"So we're just going to let Fairwind get away with it?"

"I did not say that," Jeon said. "The first thing I'd like you and Jude to do is find where he's stored his stockpile, and then we strike. He can play his games; we'll play ours. But there's a clock running on us regardless — it's only a matter of time before our own supply runs dry, and Monmurg's more exposed to the sahaugin than any other principality's holding."

"That makes sense," Paul said. "If the sahaugin could destroy this city, it'd only be a matter of time before everything collapses." He paused, turning something over. "How long does one barrel last, once it's in the water?"

"Not even a week, from what I understand," Jeon said. "The salting ships go out every couple of days with a fresh barrel, though a small one covers a wide range, miles across — the entire Monmurgian Strait, from a single barrel at a time, dumped up near the channel and let the current carry it back this way. We've done it this way for a hundred and fifty years."

"I'll start looking into it."

"There's a political dimension too — I don't want word of Fairwind's treason spreading to the other princes just yet. I'm hoping we can negotiate with him instead, find a way to recover the supply, or at least some part of it."

"You might want to have those Marines stay quiet, then," Paul said.

"I will — I'll leave that to Jamis." Jeon's face eased fractionally. "If I can avoid a war with Fairwind, I very much would like to. I don't want Keoland to see weakness among us — we already look weak enough to that country as it is. Any sign of division among the princes invites direct attack, and I won't risk that.

"There's another matter too — we've looked very weak at sea, and I believe it's tied to some magical talisman Cain Toli holds. We've lost ships we shouldn't have, and until we know what's behind it, I don't want to risk an outright strike on the Keoland fleet. Jamis has ideas on what's causing it; I'll let him share them with you in time. Your own arcane abilities, once developed, may help us counter whatever advantage the Toli hold there. Any knowledge you gain will serve us greatly, and I want you to be my interpreter into that world. I don't trust anyone else with it."

"Not even Jude?"

"Has he given you reason not to trust him?"

"Of course not."

"But he's not family. I've no reason not to use him as an advisor — I would simply rather you be the expert in this."

"That would probably be most effective," Paul admitted. "A wizard-spy."

"Yes — that's what these times require. Good captains and good fighters aren't quite enough anymore." Jeon studied him a moment longer. "Is there anything you wish to discuss with me?"

"Let me keep these Marines under my own command," Paul said. "My own small group."

"I'll consider it. If you wish to stay in contact with them, I understand — though Merrick holds a fairly high rank, and Jamis has plans for him; he can be drawn on when needed. Gouge is another matter — allocated by Jamis as needed, but if you've a mission requiring his particular skills, I'll see he's made available. Come to me when you've an actual mission, and those four are yours."

"Absolutely."

"Good. In the meantime, continue the work you and Jude were already pursuing — research into the powder, and where Fairwind may have taken his share. That will likely mean sending spies to his island, hiring informants. You've the funds you need for it."

"Got it."

"A good master knows how to delegate — learn that skill too. Let your agents bring information to you; cultivate a network."

"I don't really have agents yet."

"You can get them. Jamis tells me Jude's already quite skilled at it."

"We have one informant," Paul said, "but that was three days ago."

"Find more. Jamis says Jude's particularly gifted at bending people to his will, through various means — learn that from him too, and use him as your cutout, your front man. Tell me what he does, what agents you recruit, and report back what you learn."

"I will."

"Excellent. Go get some rest now — we're done here. You can meet with Jude tomorrow and lay your plans."

Paul rose, was led back out the way he'd come, and found himself, once alone in his own rooms at last, glad simply to be there — peeling off clothes still damp from the shark-infested chasm, singed at the edges from the tower that had gone up in flame behind him, and glad, above all else, to be alive to feel the weight of everything his uncle had just asked of him.

# Chapter Forty-Two — What Jamis Already Knew

Jude, meanwhile, had been led by a different route — not up toward the palace, but down a back stairway he'd never walked before, into one of Jamis's private offices in the Harbor District. He noted the path carefully as he went, aware it was likely the man's own private route between residence and office, and aware too, from the care taken to keep him unseen, that Jamis did not wish this visit known.

He'd pocketed a portion of the yellow powder himself before leaving the island, meaning to study it at his own leisure, and it hadn't escaped him that Fairwind now knew him for one of Jamis's agents — reason enough to keep half an eye on every shadowed doorway between the docks and this office, in case some rival chose tonight of all nights to settle a score.

Jamis was waiting when he entered, and rose to pour the wine himself rather than send for it. "Jude — please, sit. I need a drink myself. I assume you're not partial to Gouge's red-eye?"

Jude's expression answered for him.

"I have something better, then — wine, unless you'd prefer something harder."

"Just wine."

Jamis brought the glass over himself. "A vintage from Keoland — I hope you like it."

"Wait," Jude said, studying the bottle. "Was that carried on an Oeridian ship?"

"An Oeridian ship, yes — far beyond what Keoland itself could produce."

"Have you spoken with Paul yet?"
