"I won't be — the prince will brief him directly. I may speak with him later, but I wanted you first, before Gouge or Merrick's reports reach me. Start from the beginning, if you would. I already have a fair picture of what happened out there — I had observers embedded among the Marines as well."

"I met some of the ones with Gouge, at least."

"Yes — and at least two of them answer to other people. I don't know if you're one of those."

"I don't need to know who they answer to," Jude said. "On Gouge's crew, they answer to me."

Jamis studied him a moment. "To my understanding, Merrick's one of my agents. Gouge is one of my agents."

"What of the other two? The bard?"

"Where's he from?"

"One of the men in Gouge's crew."

"I can't recall his name offhand, which irritates me. I'll assume the rest were simply regular Marines with you, not my own, though I'll look into it."

"Interesting," Jude said. "I know the bard and Merrick both sent word to have corsairs meet us — that we'd be leaving by skiff."

"Likely men Merrick trusts personally. He did ask to bring along some of his own. I'll speak to him, update what I know of them." Jamis settled back. "What is it you'd like to share first?"

Jude gave his account in careful order — the homunculus returning agitated, the fight with the two Toli guarding the approach, the powerful counterspell or resistance they'd carried alongside their enchanted blades, Fairwind found bound to a chair once the fighting ended. "In the conversation that followed, trying to get him off the island and back to Monmurg for proper interrogation, he admitted to siphoning off the barrels for months. I saw the storage room myself — entirely empty, save the twenty-five we recovered, minus the four we brought home. The rest had already gone, loaded onto Cain Toli's ship, the Sea Ghost.

"So it sounds as if the Toli took a share, and were negotiating with him — and he played it off as business as usual, the kind of jockeying that happens between princes all the time." He caught himself. "He did say — sorry, I may be mixing something in there that only Paul would have heard directly."

"That's fine," Jamis said. "You wouldn't know that part — that was specific to Paul's account."

"But I did hear him admit to siphoning the barrels, and he wasn't surprised the Toli were there. Playing it cool, or genuinely unbothered — either way, they were there. It sounds like Fairwind's out for his own benefit — believes that siding with the Toli against Jeon gains him leverage, more power, more control. A political play, purely."

"I think it was too," Jamis said, "and that's part of why I was trying to bring him back to you — figuring you might still negotiate with him, or send him to his own people, whatever made sense."

"My sense of the Toli, from the short time I've been back after a year abroad, is that if they're involved in this, they're playing for Keoland's benefit. That worries me. But I'm glad to hear he admitted, in full honesty, that he was playing politics for leverage."

"I believe so," Jamis said. "You should know — Fairwind and Jeon are childhood friends, known each other their whole lives. Jeon's mind will change now, I think, especially since it was Paul who brought the accusation. I'm glad it happened that way, because Jeon could never have imagined his own friend capable of this — that's part of why you were sent in the first place."

"He admitted, plainly, to siphoning the barrels — and may hold the largest surviving stash of any of them, which is treasonous to the principalities, if not directly to Jeon himself. Talking with the Toli alone wouldn't ordinarily be improper, but removing barrels of that substance is treason outright. Even knowledge of it, outside the admiralty, is treasonous. The Toli agents shouldn't have been allowed into that area at all."

"So they'd have come up from below as well?"

"Right — we hadn't penetrated the main citadel through the front. We climbed one of the outer walls near the coastline — I levitated us up, Gouge managed on his own — under cover of darkness. His troops weren't aware of us. A sizable portion of his garrison had retreated to the barracks, apparently under cover of that fog a few nights past — that's when the hobgoblins came, dressed in Marine-style gear, though not any color scheme I recognized as belonging to any prince. Interesting, that.

"His garrison captain had no understanding of what had actually happened, beyond the hobgoblins holding the inner citadel. He'd staged a show of force that gave us our diversion to scale the outer wall. But when Fairwind led us down, from the inner hall—"

"The sanctum," Jamis offered.

"Yes, straight down. That's where we fought the Toli, who likely came up from the Sea Ghost."

"So they know not only of the barrels," Jamis said slowly, "but of the inner sanctum's layout too."

"Fairwind said he didn't want to see his men killed — made sure they were disarmed and locked away elsewhere before we arrived. I meant to stay behind with Paul, but Gouge and Merrick judged it wiser to pull back and call for help. That's why they signaled the corsairs Jeon had already sent." Jude turned the rest of it over. "I'm not certain whether the hobgoblins came in under cover of the fog alongside the sahaugin — there were sahaugin there too, which I assume is exactly what the barrels were meant to prevent. We lost the whole complement of two hundred Marines who'd come ashore with us — I saved twenty who were with me directly, retreated into the garrison beside Fairwind's own men.

"To my knowledge, his outer garrison wasn't aware of his duplicity — I never spoke with anyone from the inner sanctum, but it didn't sound as though there'd been a massacre of his own troops. My feeling is he was trying to maintain a ruse — his theft of the barrels going unnoticed, the blame falling on the Toli instead."

"That would make sense," Jamis said, and something in his voice shifted, grew more careful. "There are a couple of things you should know now, and I'll tell you plainly — don't speak of this to anyone. Not Gouge. Not Paul."

"Not even Paul."

"No. Jeon's affection for Fairwind blinded him once, and I'm afraid his emotions could blind him again in this. When we act, we have to do it without those blinders, which is why what I tell you now stays between us."

"Of course."

"This isn't because I trust you completely, Jude," Jamis said, something almost wry in it. "But I understand what patronage means in desperate times, and mine will be worth a great deal to you."

"Do you have another magic ring in that drawer?" Jude asked, only half joking.

Jamis paused. "What did you say?"

"You got another magic ring in there?"

Jamis considered it a long moment, then something crossed his face that suggested he'd landed on something better. "Actually, I have something you'd appreciate more. Real estate's quite cheap in Monmurg right now, and I know where you live — you may have your reasons for staying there, various nefarious ones, I don't judge. I understand you're keeping a thumb on the pulse of the foreign district, and that has its uses. But there's a tower overlooking the sea that would suit you far better. And, of course, access to the Royal Library — not the Archives of the Arcane Order, that's a separate matter, but a fine collection all the same. Better than what you've had, in that half-ruined tower you're keeping now. We'll arrange it, so long as you're willing to act as my agent in this."

"Of course."

"I don't think Fairwind fully understands what the Toli are truly up to," Jamis said. "I don't think he understands the Scarlet Brotherhood at all, or the danger it poses."

"He doesn't."

"I believe he thinks he's simply playing politics—"

"Playing politics with people who are playing for keeps," Jude said.

"Exactly — and he believes the Toli ultimately want the sea principalities united as one nation, that their negotiations with Jeon are purely over the slave trade — the Duke of Berghof, the power of Westkeep, the interior principalities all bound up in it. Fairwind thinks Jeon's objection to that trade is the whole of the Toli's interest. It isn't — and unfortunately, I don't believe Jeon himself fully grasps what they're truly doing either. I have information that the Scarlet Brotherhood isn't only infiltrating the sea principalities — they're infiltrating Keoland too, and the current conflict between the two nations is being stoked, to their own benefit."

"Remember when I spoke with that baron from Red Shore?" Jude said. "He seemed surprised when I told him Jeon opposed slavery — as though he'd been led to believe otherwise."

"That belief comes straight from the Duke of Gradsul," Jamis said. "He's always claimed the sea principalities morally corrupt for the slave trade — which existed in Berghof long before we ever came into being, back to the fall of the Suel Empire itself. We had nothing to do with its origin, though yes, slaves are traded in our own marketplaces, and Jeon would end the practice if he could. It's something that predates our nation entirely."

# Chapter Forty-Three — The Tower and the Storm to Come

"What might also help me, going forward," Jude said, turning the wine glass slowly in his hand, "is knowing whether you can give me a kill-on-sight list. Would have been helpful to know one existed before I acted on my own account, bringing Fairwind in as I did."

"Fairwind is not a kill-on-sight, Jude," Jamis said, firm. "I do not want him assassinated. He isn't fully aware of the political situation — which means, in the future, he could still come around. Realize where his true loyalty lies, that the Prince of Toli is more dangerous to him than Jeon ever was, and that the game he's playing could destroy him along with everyone else. I don't think any of the princes survive what the Toli mean to bring, if it comes to that."

"I agree with that." Jude turned to the city's more immediate troubles. "How does Monmurg stand, with the embargo on food? Are we suffering mass desertions? Holding together at all?"

"Our stores are going down greatly," Jamis admitted. "We get some food from smugglers running to Red Shore — you've seen this yourself, and brought me word of it. My own spies have confirmed what you reported. It's interesting, someone from Red Shore approaching you directly — I may have more to say on that in the future. There may be an opening there; I may send you as an envoy to speak with him again.

"But I believe Fairwind and Jeon aren't the only ones being played by the Toli. I have a feeling the sea principalities, and Keoland itself, are being played by other, more dangerous forces still. Do you know what's happening in Keoland right now? A civil war may be imminent there."

"Between whom?"

"The Duke of Gradsul, who by rights should have been crowned king some twelve years past, and was denied it — has been chasing the throne ever since. The man appointed instead, an obscure Oeridian from some back province, has recently died. There is no king currently sitting the throne."

"Any suspicion he was assassinated?"

"The royal alchemist died a month before the king did," Jamis said. "Very suspicious, what's happening there."

"Is the Duke the greater problem for the sea princes, or for Jeon specifically?"

"When I say 'the sea princes,' I mean Jeon specifically, right now — we're on the verge of civil war with the Toli ourselves, depending on where everyone falls. It's already begun, if I'm honest with you. When you speak with Jeon, let him know — Fairwind made a comment, taking the island back from the hobgoblins being a necessity for Monmurg, and Jeon answered that he thought Monmurg would fall without the Helm. So he understands the gravity of it, at least, exactly — and I think, again, he's playing a very dangerous game, for reasons beyond me. He's always been jealous of Monmurg's prosperity, and outspoken that the negotiations over slavery with the Toli and Berghof are unnecessary, idealistic, an attempt to end something older than our own country. Minor things, in the end. Why he's gone to such lengths now is beyond me. Very dangerous — and something I'd like you to try to ascertain, if you can.

"There's a second matter too. The salting ships did their work before the Helm was overrun — why didn't it take effect? Was some magic cast to keep the substance from working as it should? Could someone have been replacing the barrels as they were taken, with something ineffective? I need to know. If someone swapped the barrels for a substitute, that's even more treasonous — more evidence of what's been done. Or is the substance simply growing less effective on its own, the sahaugin adapting, some counter-agent overcoming it? I want to know, and I think you're the one to find out."

"Is the tower equipped with anything," Jude asked, "or is it simply housing?"

"Whatever you want brought there, I'll see done — and of course you may take books to and from for your own research." Jamis paused, something more delicate in what came next. "There is another matter, and I confess I'm hesitant to raise it, but Prince Jeon has insisted. He believes Paul Rivera will make an excellent wizard, and wants you to tutor him. He means to push him in that direction."

"I can see he's got a natural talent for it," Jude said. "Made that homunculus entirely on his own."

"He did?" Something like genuine surprise crossed Jamis's face.

"Yes."

Jamis studied him a long moment before continuing. "So — yes, you're fully cleared to research the substance, and I'd like you to research what possibly negated it at the Helm as well."

"Definitely. And I'll bring Paul in on this."

"That's what Jeon wants — he'd like someone in his own family with knowledge of the arcane, and this seems the way to it. The yellow substance was never created by any reigning prince — the Duke of Berghof supplied it to us."

"So you need me to reverse-engineer it."

"Essentially, yes — we've no idea how to produce it ourselves. The Duke began supplying it some twenty, twenty-five years back. The largest stores — the only real stores — were kept on the Helm."

"Do you have any here, in the city?"

"A few barrels in Monmurg. The Helm was built as a storage container, historically — that's where it was kept. But there's another store as well, a significant one, in Selmer Bay, between here and Saltmarsh."

"Still secure, to your knowledge? Does the Toli have any claim on it?"

"It's rotated in possession over the years — the princes trade custody of these stores among themselves." Jamis's expression sobered. "But before we look into that, we need to understand how the Toli are destroying our ships. That's the other thing I want you to investigate — the artifact Gouge tricked Lord Phranck into revealing. Did you hear of this?"

"No."

"An agent from Keoland came to see me last month, around the fifteenth — spoke with an alchemist in the foreign district, then wished to speak with me directly, and was attacked by Toli agents for his trouble. Gouge was hired by one of Phranck's men to guard him; I'd warned them the Toli were watching. What Phranck is, it seems, is what's called an Inquisitor — have you heard the term? A secret society, in truth a government body in Keoland, called the Office of Forbidden Magic. Not widely known, but a kind of magic-policing organization, seeing that wizards don't practice what's forbidden — necromancy, certain conjurations—"

"Demonic conjuration," Jude said.

"Exactly — probably all the things the Scarlet Brotherhood excels at."

"From what I understand, yes."

"Phranck's part of that organization — my own agents confirm it — and he was here trying to learn what the Toli had actually taken. The agents who attacked him, unbeknownst to them, were up against Gouge and Karmirg, my own men acting as his guards. They were defeated before they could finish him, and Phranck tore something free of their minds in the aftermath: the Toli have taken possession of a very powerful, ancient artifact — one I believe grants control over turtle dragons. Our ships vanishing at sea trace back to this, and it's the reason we haven't been able to fight the Keoland fleet effectively."

"Can we still maneuver to Red Shore, or to Jerly Bay, without being molested by them? Is it only open-sea shipping that's at risk?"

"The Dragon Isles sit close to both Red Shore and Monmurg, so anything north of here carries the risk of Cain Toli and his dragons. Jerly Bay's untouched, but only because the Toli control it themselves — their own thoroughfare to the jungle, unaffected in the least."

"Do you believe the Toli capable of a direct assault on Monmurg?"

"Absolutely not. Our defenses are impenetrable to their armies — there's no way even Keoland's forces, significant as they are, could take this place by storm."

"With the sahaugin's help, if we lack the material to hold them off?"

"There's no true alliance there," Jamis said. "The sahaugin simply go where they can feed — sharks being chummed, more than anything, adding to the chaos rather than serving any real plan. The Toli mean to disrupt us, nothing more, though you can be sure they've no wish to see the sahaugin win outright either — and yes, they do have the means to produce the yellow substance themselves, unlike us.

"So: research the substance, discover whether it's been diminished or thwarted, and why. Try to learn where Fairwind stored his own barrels, if you can. Help Paul in all of this, since that's the charge Jeon means to give him — and take his measure, see what rudiments of the arcane arts he might already grasp."

"Yes, exactly," Jude said, and hesitated a moment before adding, almost idly, "Few people know who Paul's father truly was. I've been mildly curious — though if it's a state secret—"

"I don't think his father lacked for intelligence," Jamis said, choosing his words with visible care. "From what Jeon's told me, and my own dealings with the lad, he's plainly capable himself. His father's own mother was remarkably intelligent — and magical besides."

"Did he suffer some disfiguring injury," Jude asked, "or is he simply shy? I don't need the details — only curious, since I'll always be dealing with him. Should I even ask?"

"You should," Jamis said. "And to be honest, it has nothing to do with disease. He was burned terribly as a young child, in one of his father's great magical experiments gone wrong."

Jude sat with that a moment. "The hell. That's... quite a thing to carry."

"So — the artifact," Jamis said, gathering the threads back together. "Continue researching its nature, and how they're using it against the dragons. The yellow substance. Where Fairwind's hidden his stores. Help Paul develop in all this work, see what he's capable of, and report back to me. Do not let Prince Jeon's loyalties blind you in any of it."

"His loyalty to Fairwind, you mean?"

"His loyalty to everyone. These are dangerous times, and I don't fully trust his judgment, if I'm honest with you."

"Suspect everyone," Jude said.

"Exactly. That ship would never have been lost to the sahaugin, if I'd had my way with it — there'd have been a barrel of our own aboard. Jeon's eyes are opening, slowly, but for us to survive, they need to open a great deal faster than they are. Don't let on to Paul about any of this."

"Of course." Jude turned one more matter over before rising to go. "I have in mind — Paul commanded the contingent of twenty Marines, the only ones to survive that ship. Could you give me their names? If they're reassigned back to Monmurg, or held at the Helm — in the sense of the network you mentioned — they'd know both myself and Paul, and carry some sense of solidarity from having survived together."

"I can give you the name of their commander," Jamis said, "and he can speak to the men — though I'd have Paul speak to them himself."

"If you want him to develop in that work, yes — absolutely. Helping men who survived the sahaugin attack is solid ground to build on. And there's money for bribes, if it comes to that."

"That's probably the only thing we have in abundance right now."

"Those men, if they have families," Jude added, "would likely appreciate simply being able to feed them. Unfortunately, food's something none of us have much of either."

An attendant slipped in a moment later to inform Jamis that Gouge had arrived and was waiting to be seen.

"I'm sure you have things to attend to," Jamis said, rising.

"Absolutely," Jude said, and rose as well.

"My attendant will take you back the way you came. Use my residence to collect your things from the foreign district, if you like."

"Will there be keys to the tower made available?"

"Tomorrow — come back to my residence tomorrow, and I'll see you have them."

"Thank you for the wine," Jude said, and let himself be led out, turning over as he went the full weight of everything he'd just been told — a tower, a library, a mission tangled in dragons and forbidden magic and a boy who'd been burned as a child by his own father's ambition, and a city that was, by every account he'd just heard, standing a good deal closer to the edge than anyone within it yet fully understood.

---

## PART NINE: THE FOURTH DAY

# Chapter Forty-Four — What Paul Wanted

Some days after the return from Helm Island, Jude and Paul found a quiet hour to sit together and turn over everything each of them had learned, and everything still left undone.

"I'm not sure we should have killed Lord Fairwind," Jude said, turning a cup slowly between his fingers. "Though it may be moot now. I don't know that Jeon and Fairwind could have mended things any other way."

"Did you want him dead?" Paul asked. "I wanted him in custody."

"When we lost him, I wasn't sure what we'd even have done with him beyond arrest — a traitor, guilty of treason plainly enough, but we hadn't the time to sort any of that properly while fighting our way through the castle." Jude studied him a moment. "What exactly did you hear him say, that night? I never asked you outright."

"You already know most of it — he was speaking with the Toli, about disposing of evidence, or selling something off. The Toli answered him, more or less, 'We noticed you took most of it anyway.'"

"Of the powder."

"Yes. He admitted as much himself, once we had him bound."

They picked apart the tangled last hour of the fight together — who had said what to whom on that narrow bridge, whether Fairwind had truly meant to strike Gouge or only stumbled into him in the chaos, what he'd whispered to Folsom in that last moment before breaking free and shouting his warning to the guards below.

"Have you ever actually toured his holdings?" Jude asked. "His own island?"

"Attended a feast there once, at the main port. Never seen the rest of it in any depth."

"So if we need to find where he's stashed things, you'd have no idea where to start."

"Pretty much none."

Jude weighed the shape of the man they were hunting. "Is he the sort who'd keep his valuables close at hand, or scattered across separate islands, far from anything obvious?" He turned the question over in his own experience of pirate lords and outer-island princes, and answered it himself before Paul could venture a guess. "Old-school, through and through — pirate tradition to the bone. Men like that live for secret treasures, hidden coves, smuggling routes. If he's true to type, he'll keep several caches, never all his eggs in one basket — one well-defended, obvious decoy, and a handful of genuinely secret stores besides."

"There must be hundreds of little islands out there," Paul said. "Impossible to search them all, even within a day's sail of his main holdings."

"Not infinite," Jude allowed, "but enough to make any search without better information a fool's errand. We'll need shipping logs, records of where he's sailed — official business or otherwise."

"I'll check the library."

"I'll check too, though you'll need to bring me along for proper access." Jude paused. "Do you know what Jamis actually wants from us now? First and plainest — find the powder, recover what we can, recreate it if there's any way to. And he wants me to teach you wizardry properly."

"He said that outright?"

"He did."

"I'm fine with it," Paul said, something settling in him at the admission. "It closes a real gap in the family — we've no spellcasters otherwise."

"He's addressing the weakness directly, then." Jude glanced sideways at him. "What's your intelligence, out of curiosity?"

"I don't actually know, offhand."

"We're probably about equal."

"Doubtful," Paul said, dry.

"Whatever, then — you're not exactly making homunculi out of blood and clay in your spare time."

"I didn't even know that was how it was done."

"You already knew I was teasing you about it."

"That's not an eighteen-intelligence trick, surely."

"What's yours, then?"

"Twenty, apparently."

Jude gave a short, unbothered laugh. "Fine — give me a level or two, I'll catch up. You've got a real propensity for it, at any rate, and the curiosity to match, which matters more."

They turned, more soberly, to the question of timing — fifty days, perhaps three months at the outside, before the last of the powder ran dry, longer still if the twenty-five barrels left behind on Helm Island held secure. Whether they'd be called back to help clear the last of the hobgoblins, or left to their research here in the city; whether the gills Jude had cut from the dead sahaugin might yet hold some trace of a counter-agent, some clue as to why the powder had failed when it should have held.

"There's an alchemist," Paul offered, "just off the square in the foreign district. I've been trying to build a rapport with him — good reason to visit now, between the gills and whatever else we might need."

"Lead the way, then," Jude said, "before these robes start smelling like the docks. I haven't even had the sense to wrap the things properly."

"They have no idea what they're in for," Paul said, shaking his head, "working with you."

Before they parted for the alchemist's door, Paul offered one piece of advice unprompted. "The twenty men who survived the Helm with us — if we hadn't been there, they'd be dead on that ship. I'm not saying hold it over their heads. But find their sergeant, see he's not hoarding what little coin comes his way, and let him pass some of it down to feed the men's families. In times like these, that buys real loyalty."

Jude turned the thought over and found it sound. "And the thief from the guild — the one we didn't put to the poker."

"He already knows who I am."

"Then someone will eventually pay him well to put a knife in a member of the royal family. Could be you. But if he wants to rise faster than a whole life of waiting would get him, that could also be you, if you play it right." Jude considered the whole shape of what Paul had just offered him, unprompted, and found it better counsel than he'd expected. "Good instincts. Start with the soldiers — find out how many of that twenty are still on the island, whether Fairwind's own three hundred are being handled fairly or simply left to rot in whatever hold he penned them in. Then keep cultivating your thief. Even a man who once tried to kill you can be made an asset, with the right patience."

"To be fair," Paul said, "it wasn't a particularly skilled attempt."

"No — I put an arrow clean through his companion's neck for the trouble."

"And then you saved him."

"Out the other side, more or less. We're even, in a way."

# Chapter Forty-Five — The First Cantrip

The following afternoon, in a quiet corner of Lord Jamis's residence, the two of them sat down properly to begin.

"So," Jude said, studying him across the table, "where do you see yourself? You've met Gouge — if you go on entirely as you have been, that's the road you're walking. I like the man, worked with him a long while, and I'll keep on working with him. But he's a killer, plain and simple, nothing more."

"Is that what you see for yourself," Paul asked, "or what you want?"

"Not exactly. I think I'd like to be something of a mage as well."

"What draws you to it, as far as you understand it? Reading scrolls, or something more?"

"Every new initiate to the arcane comes in carrying their own baggage, if I'm honest."

"So what do you actually want — to compete with others who wield real influence? Power?"

"I like that word. Influence."

"Power for what, though?"

Jude considered the question longer than Paul expected. "The last time I stood against those rogue mages, I wasn't nearly as useful as I'd have liked to be. Nothing I could do to counter their spells directly."

"So you felt ineffective."

"There's a limit to how far cutting a man's throat gets you. Gouge is fine in a pinch, but against someone who truly commands the arcane, it's an entirely different contest."

"Any natural talent for it, that you know of?"

"I think so," Jude said. "I made that little lizard, after all."

"The homunculus — you made it yourself, truly?"

"Truly."

Paul studied him with fresh interest. He knew enough of the craft, from his own reading, to know that most who claimed the title were charlatans selling little more than painted trinkets to credulous nobles — real homunculi were rarer, and harder-won, than the popular fashion for them suggested. "Tell me how you did it, then. Don't spare me the details — I'll know if you're spinning me a tale."

"I've the actual papers on it, if you want proof," Jude said, and described the working plainly enough — blood, clay, a working drawn from an old tome he'd found among a stranger's effects years before, one he kept close and shared with no one. He didn't offer the book itself, nor the name written inside its cover, and Paul, sensing the boundary, didn't press for it.

"Can you shape it safely, the way you want?" Paul asked instead.

"You're good," Jude said. "Yes."

The conversation wandered, as such conversations do, into stranger corners — why a man raised in comfortable circumstances would describe himself as having been "locked up" for twenty years, what lay behind the elf's careful reserve, how much of what Jude said of himself was even true. Jude turned the questions aside without much heat, more amused than offended, and pressed in turn for whatever hidden currents ran beneath Paul's own composed exterior.

"What are your hang-ups?" he asked eventually. "Every man carries something from before he started down this road."

"I want to prove myself," Paul admitted, after a moment's thought. "Earn my place. Be valuable."

"I understand wanting that." Jude leaned forward. "Now — how does that very desire get you killed, in this line of work? Take it further. You don't want to fail. What does failure cost you here, specifically, studying the arcane?"

"It could kill me."

"Worse than that. If, in your hunger to prove yourself, you reach for things you can't yet control, believing you can — you might unleash something on the world that neither of us can put back. Summon what you can't command, thinking you're ready when you're not. I want to be valuable too, Paul. I want power, same as you. But if you're dead, none of that matters, because you're dead. You'll have to check yourself constantly, especially once I can't watch over every step you take, and you're teaching yourself instead. Keep that in your bones: don't overreach. If you forget it, you will overreach, and the cost may run far beyond your own life."

"I understand," Paul said, and meant it.

They turned, at last, to the practical matter of what Paul might actually learn first. He described his own instincts — disguise, misdirection, moving unseen through a crowd rather than cutting a path through it — and Jude nodded slowly, recognizing the shape of it.

"That maps to illusion, in the arts. Being unseen." He considered the six spells owed to a new initiate, and the three cantrips besides. "Mending and Light, to start. You already know how to kill a man — these will teach you the rest of what the job actually requires."

"Mending," Paul said, unconvinced. "How does that help me, exactly?"

"Break a window getting into a place, mend it again on your way out, and cover your tracks entirely."

"That, I can use."

"And Light isn't only illumination — cast it on the inside of a man's visor, and you've blinded him outright. Or use it to check a dark room for hidden traps before you ever set foot inside." Jude ran through the rest — Sleep, to spare a guard rather than kill him; Protection from Good and Evil; Detect Magic, invaluable against warded traps; and, when the conversation strayed toward an old black iron mask Paul had once worn and quickly discarded, Identify as well. "Better safe. And I'll throw in Shield — a reaction spell, the only one at your level that turns aside an incoming strike outright. Stops a magic missile cold, and buys you real protection for the round you cast it."

They spent the rest of the afternoon and well into evening on the practical work of it — parchment in place of a proper spellbook, ink smudged and re-inked a dozen times over, a broken chair mended and broken again before it finally held. Jude proved an exacting teacher, quick with a thrown bowl or a sharp word whenever Paul's concentration slipped, and Paul gave as good as he got, needling him right back at every opportunity — about the mask, about the mysterious tower in the Aldorian Wood Jude claimed as a childhood home and which, by his own later admission, didn't actually exist. By the time full dark had settled over the palace, Paul had Mending down cold, bruised knuckles and bruised pride both, and Light still eluding him more often than not.

"First cantrip's easy," Jude told him, not unkindly, as they finally called an end to it. "The second one's always rougher."

"I got bashed a lot in the meantime."

"Stop taking it personally. Keep some objectivity." Jude studied him a moment, something almost fond beneath the dryness. "Remember that part of you that wanted to kill every Toli on the dock at Jerly Bay? Learn to hold that same fire steady, and you'll get there."

# Chapter Forty-Six — The Wager

They kept talking as they climbed the stairs back toward their own rooms, neither quite ready to let the evening end.

"Let's not call it a day yet," Jude said. "You're tapped out on the arcane arts, fair enough. Why don't you teach me something of your own trade instead? I'll follow your lead — you can knock me about if I fumble it — and we'll slip into Ferdin's without anyone the wiser."

"You want to sneak into your own bar?"

"I want to know who's been drinking there since I chased off some disreputable company. Call it instructional."

Paul turned the idea over, weighing the practicalities of stealth with two men rather than one, and found himself, in the end, disinclined toward Ferdin's specifically. "Why not stealth in on the alchemist's shop instead? See who else visits him, who might be watching the place."

"No," Jude said, flat.

"Why not?"

"Because you're fishing — trying to work out whether I'm something rotten underneath, which I'm not, though I can't exactly prove that to you standing here."

"I never said anything about rotten," Paul said, startled. "I meant the alchemist himself — the one who sold you that mask. Observe him, nothing more."

They went back and forth a while longer, circling the same argument from different angles, until Jude proposed something simpler: a contest, plain and clean. Whoever could creep closest to a given guardsman without being seen would win, and they'd wager on the outcome — a hundred gold pieces, once the haggling settled.

"You'd best believe I'm taking that bet," Paul said.

"Invisibility's about as stealthy as it gets," Jude pointed out, when Paul objected that magic felt like cheating the spirit of the thing.

"That's the whole point of what I'm teaching you," Jude added. "Combine what you already have with what I can give you. On your own you're already better than me at half of this — but put the two together, and that's a real weapon. Walk up on a man invisible, close enough to touch him before he ever knows you're there — that's worth more than either skill alone."

The dice didn't favor either of them especially that night, and the contest dissolved as much in laughter as in any clear victor, but the lesson beneath it held all the same — a wizard-assassin, rare and fearsomely effective when the two crafts truly married, and Paul, whatever grief he gave his teacher over the bruises and the burnt robes, found himself genuinely eager to see how far the combination might carry him.

# Chapter Forty-Seven — What Paul Knew of the Toli

Alone in his own rooms that night, turning the day over before sleep finally claimed him, Paul found himself circling back to a gap in his own education — not of magic, but of the wider politics his uncle moved through daily, and which he himself understood only in broad, half-formed strokes.

He knew Monmurg for what it was: a city-state among several, Fairwind and Flotsam and Jetsam each their own island holding, and Toli a city-state in its own right, ruled by Prince Sconforth and set somewhat apart from the others by the old Suel blood that ran through its royal line, though counted an equal among the five principalities all the same — Toli, Fairwind, Flotsam, Jetsam, and Monmurg. Beyond those five sat two powers who held no vote in council, yet wielded influence enough to shape everything around them regardless: the Duke of Berghof, whose lands supplied the yellow powder that had for a century and a half kept the sahaugin from these shores, and the power of Westkeep, who held the northern reach along the Hool River.

It was a shape of the world Paul had always known in outline, the way a man knows the walls of a house he's lived in his whole life without ever counting the stones — but turning it over now, with Fairwind's betrayal fresh in his mind and Zafar Azane's name lodged somewhere he couldn't quite dislodge it, the outline felt suddenly a good deal less solid than it had a week before.

# Chapter Forty-Eight — Copper and Sulfur

The next evening, the two of them left the palace together as the sun went down, passing through gate after gate before the guards sealed them for the night, and walked the thirty minutes down into the foreign district, where the smell of onion soup and unwashed wool and the sea itself rose up to meet them.

"Smells like home," Paul said, and meant it more than he expected to.

They wound through the district's tangle of narrow roads, past the same corners where Paul had once been set upon by a desperate cutpurse, until the buildings gave way to a small stone courtyard and a single sturdy door standing apart from the rickety driftwood structures around it. Jude knocked, twice, before the bolt finally scraped back and a stooped old man peered out at them through a pair of strange, heavy-lensed spectacles.

"I'm the mage," Jude said. "Bought that sample off you not long ago."

"I remember you," the old alchemist said, and turned his clouded eyes toward Paul. "This is your friend?"

"My student," Jude said. "I'm Jude. Introduce yourself."

"Paul."

"Paul...?"

"Paul Rivera."

Something shifted behind the old man's spectacles, some calculation running quick and quiet. "Rivera. What does Prince Jeon want with me?"

"It's not like that," Jude said quickly. "He's my student, nothing more."

"Ah. Hence." The alchemist studied Paul a moment longer, something almost gentle in the scrutiny. "You do not use the name Azane, then?"

Paul went very still. "I go by Rivera."

"Is that a given name?" The old man didn't press further, only let the question hang a moment before waving them both through the heavy door, sliding the bolt home behind them. "Come in. Be respectful. Touch nothing."

The room beyond was a clutter of curiosities in every direction — jars and vials and strange instruments crowding every shelf, the whole space smelling of sulfur and old parchment and something else Paul couldn't quite name. The alchemist himself moved through it with the ease of long familiarity, hunched and slow but sure of every step.

"How is your uncle?" he asked, not turning around. "Your father — your grandfather? It has been some while since he last visited me."

"Has it?" Paul said, carefully.

"Yes." The old man let the matter drop as quickly as he'd raised it, and turned instead to the business at hand. "You wish to know."

"Tell him what we spoke of," Jude said. "What we found."

"Copper and sulfur," the alchemist said, already moving toward a cluttered workbench. "The elements, combined in a way you'll understand once you see it. Come."

"We brought you a sample," Jude reminded him. "From Helm Island — the barrels."

"I remember. Copper and sulfur, combined in a manner I have never before seen." He gestured toward a small dish of pale, yellowish powder sitting on the bench, the very substance that had emptied a grotto of sahaugin and sharks alike not a week before. "Copper alone — nothing to me. Sulfur alone — nothing to me. To a man, an elf — no harm at all. To the gills of a fish, though—"

"Show us," Jude said.

The old man crossed to a small bowl where a single goldfish circled in slow, contented loops, and took the barest pinch of the powder between two fingers. "This is a thousand times more than would be needed. One part in a thousand of even this pinch would kill the creature outright." He let it fall.

The fish thrashed once, twice, and rolled belly-up near the surface, gasping.

"That is what you saw on the island," the alchemist said, watching it with clinical detachment. "No man is harmed by this. The dose that means nothing to us is death itself to them — it moves straight into the blood."

"You said there were elements you couldn't identify," Jude pressed.

"Trace elements, yes — and more than that, the manner of their combining, which I cannot determine. I do not know how to make this myself. The raw materials are cheap enough, mind — copper carries some value, but sulfur can be bought in any market stall. If the method were known, it could be made by the ton."

"If I told you this substance came originally from the Duke of Berghof's own territories," Jude said, "would that tell you anything?"

The old man's face sharpened with sudden interest. "It tells me a great deal. This sample is perhaps twenty years old — the last time Prince Jeon's people were able to acquire any at all. Made, or discovered, in the mountains along the Duke's southwestern border. The Fhoraxians made this."

"I don't know that name," Jude admitted.

"Few do." The alchemist studied the powder a moment longer, something like old regret crossing his weathered face. "I do not know how it is made. Grayson was pursuing the same question, before the end — did he leave formulae? I have some notes of my own I believe may bear on it."

Paul, listening, turned the whole conversation over in his own mind before finally asking the question plainly. "Why does this matter so much — why go to such lengths to remake it?"

"To hold back the sahaugin," Jude said. "That's the whole of it. The sea principalities have leaned on this for a hundred and fifty years, and the stores are nearly spent."

"And it's explosive too," Jude added, turning back to the alchemist. "You know that?"

"Not ordinarily — only under pressure, or once it's aged some while in seawater. The reaction with brine gives off heat enough to catch, under the right conditions. Otherwise it sits inert as sand."

"Do you have the formulae you mentioned?" Jude asked. "Written down somewhere?"

"Not here. In a book, at my other workroom."

"Bring them to me," Jude said, and the old man studied him a long moment before answering.

"This is an interesting problem, and I would like to help you solve it. But understand what you're asking of me. If this knowledge were to reach the wrong ears, that protection your student enjoys" — he nodded toward Paul — "would not extend to me. I keep a great many secrets, for a great many people. This one would mean my death, and quickly, if it ever got out that I'd shared it."

"There are worse fates," Jude said.

"That is, in fact, the kindest one available in this line of work," the old man said, something wry beneath the grimness of it. He turned, considering. "Step outside a moment, if you would. You need not be present for the rest of this."

Jude stepped out into the courtyard, leaving Paul alone with the old man a moment, and found him waiting there when the door creaked open again some minutes later.

"If I stay here with him a while," Jude said, low, "can you find your own way back into the palace?"

"I'll send the homunculus ahead to check the route," Paul offered, then reconsidered the creature's limited range and settled instead on going himself, by a way he knew that didn't require the front gates at all.

"Go, then," Jude said. "I'll stay, keep an eye on the old man, make sure nothing happens to him before you're back with whatever proof he needs to see. An hour, no more?"

"An hour," Paul agreed, and paused at the mouth of the courtyard, glancing back. "If I come back and find flashes of light coming out of that shop, and things burning—"

"I've been worried about exactly that myself," Jude admitted.

"Then come quickly, and deal with whoever's responsible."

"It will be done," Jude said, and meant it.

Paul turned and made his way back up through the darkened streets of the foreign district, the smell of onion soup and salt water trailing behind him, the old alchemist's question still turning over and over in his mind — *you do not use the name Azane?* — a thread he hadn't meant to pull, and couldn't now put down. Somewhere above him, past the gates and the guards and the long climb back to the palace, his uncle and Lord Jamis were weighing wars and blockades and the fates of princes, and somewhere behind him, in a cluttered little shop that smelled of sulfur and old secrets, an old man was searching for a way to give the sea principalities back the one thing that had always stood between them and the dark water — while Jude waited at his side, watchful, unreadable as ever, one more piece in a game that had grown, in the span of a handful of days, very much larger than either of them had first understood it to be.
                                           
